Three further conventions.  The falling factorial $(Y)_{\underline\ell}$ of the
manuscripts (17 writes $(Y)_\ell$) is $\fall Y\ell$ here, as in the rest of the
article.  The manuscripts' ``factor defect $r$'' and ``log degree $k$'' of a
derivative monomial are this part's exponents $d$ and $j$ in $x^{x-d}(\log
x)^j$.  And all three cite this report as ``Part~I'' of the pinned source:
they mean Part~\ref{xx:part}, Section~2 of this article.  Their bibliography
entry for that source is replaced below by references into this part, and
manuscript~09's entry for its ``preceding companion'' by references to
Subsection~\ref{xx:pm:sec}.

The relation between the two Stirling conventions is
$x^{\overline\mu}=x(x+1)\cdots(x+\mu-1)=\sum_jc(\mu,j)x^j$ and
$F_\mu(y)=\fall y\mu=\sum_\ell s(\mu,\ell)y^\ell$, so that
$c(\mu,j)=(-1)^{\mu-j}s(\mu,j)$.  The two uses do not overlap:
\eqref{xx:eq:stirling} is an exact formula for $b(m,r)$, while $c(\mu,j)$ below
is a residue modulo~$p$.

% ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Complete nonvanishing on prime-multiple offsets (manuscript 12)}\label{xx:pm:sec}

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Abstract of manuscript 12.}\itshape
We prove that every positive-column entry on offset $2(p-1)$ of the
Lehmer--Comtet triangle is nonzero, for every prime $p$, and likewise on offset
$4(p-1)$ for every prime and $3(p-1)$ for every odd prime. More generally, for
every fixed positive multiplier $\mu$, the entire offset $\mu(p-1)$ is
zero-free for all sufficiently large primes $p$, with an explicit
harmonic-number condition and a fully integer sufficient threshold. A
denominator argument confines every possible positive integer root to a small
exponent or a residue class beyond $p^2$. A classical derivative-root barrier
proves the universal positivity cone $Y\ge4d$ and excludes the latter region;
an elementary harmonic-product estimate excludes the former. We also explain a
limitation of a fixed finite-modulus sieve at prime-square offsets. The
complete integer-zero conjecture remains open.
\upshape\par
\smallskip
\emph{Editorial note.}  Printed in the letters of
Table~\ref{xx:lc:tab:notation}: the manuscript's multiplier $m$ is $\mu$ and its
offset $r$ is $d$.
\end{quote}

\subsubsection{The coefficients and the main theorem}

Write
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pm:H}
 H(z)=\frac{(1+z)\log(1+z)}z
 =1+\sum_{j\ge1}h_jz^j,\qquad
 h_j=\frac{(-1)^{j-1}}{j(j+1)},\qquad T_d(Y)=\coef{z}{d}H(z)^Y,
\end{equation}
which is \eqref{xx:eq:H}.  For every fixed $d\ge1$, $T_d\in\Q[Y]$ has degree
$d$ and the factor $Y$ (Lemma~\ref{xx:lem:diagdegree}).  The Lehmer--Comtet
coefficients are
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pm:b}
 b(m,Y)=\frac{m!}{Y!}\coef{z}{m}\bigl((1+z)\log(1+z)\bigr)^Y
 =\frac{m!}{Y!}T_{m-Y}(Y),\quad 1\le Y\le m,
\end{equation}
which is \eqref{xx:eq:diag}.  In particular, $T_d(Y)\ne0$ for all positive
integers $Y$ is exactly nonvanishing of the entire offset $m-Y=d$.  These
coefficients enter the derivative expansion \eqref{xx:eq:expansion}.
Throughout, $\mathsf H_n=\sum_{i=1}^n1/i$, with $\mathsf H_0=0$, denotes a
harmonic number.

\begin{theorem}\label{xx:pm:main}
Let $\mu\ge2$ be an integer, let $p>\mu$ be prime, and set $d=\mu(p-1)$. If
\begin{align}
 p^2+1&\ge 4d,\label{xx:pm:A}\\
 \mathsf H_{d-1}-\mathsf H_{\mu-1}&\ge4(\mu-2),\label{xx:pm:B}
\end{align}
then $T_d(Y)\ne0$ for every positive integer $Y$.

For each fixed $\mu$, both conditions hold for all sufficiently large $p$. The
explicit integer condition
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pm:threshold}
 p\ge\max\{4\mu,\ 1+3^{4\mu-7}\}
\end{equation}
is sufficient.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}\label{xx:pm:twice}
For every prime $p$ and every positive integer $Y$,
\[
 T_{2(p-1)}(Y)\ne0.
\]
Equivalently, $b(m,m-2p+2)\ne0$ for every $m\ge2p-1$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}\label{xx:pm:triple}
For every odd prime $p$ and every positive integer $Y$,
\[
 T_{3(p-1)}(Y)\ne0.
\]
The exclusion of $p=2$ is necessary: $T_3(2)=T_3(5)=0$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}\label{xx:pm:four}
For every prime $p$ and every positive integer $Y$,
\[
 T_{4(p-1)}(Y)\ne0.
\]
\end{corollary}

For derivatives of $x^x$, the monomial $x^{x-d}(\log x)^j$ of $f^{(n)}$ ---
offset $d$, logarithmic degree $j$ --- has coefficient $\binom nm b(m,Y)$ with
$m=n-j$ and $Y=n-d-j$ (Theorem~\ref{xx:thm:expansion}).  On every covered
offset, the only physical cancellation is therefore the structural boundary
$Y=0$, or $j=n-d$; all cells with $Y>0$ are nonzero. The covered offsets are
even, so this conclusion does not remove the known odd-offset reflection
family.

The inspected repository proves the prime-offset case $d=p-1$
(Theorem~\ref{xx:thm:prime}) and completely checks offsets through $16$
(Theorem~\ref{xx:thm:16}).  Corollaries~\ref{xx:pm:twice}--\ref{xx:pm:four}
and Theorem~\ref{xx:pm:main} extend those source-relative conclusions to
complete infinite families, including offsets whose successor is composite. No
claim of exhaustive literature priority is intended.

\subsubsection{Arithmetic localization to two or more digits}

Let $\Z_{(p)}$ be the rationals with denominator coprime to $p$. Polynomial
congruences in this subsection are over that ring.

\begin{lemma}\label{xx:pm:localization}
Suppose $p>\mu\ge2$ and $d=\mu(p-1)$. Then $G(Y)=p^\mu T_d(Y)$ and $G(Y)/Y$ lie
in $\Z_{(p)}[Y]$, and
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pm:congruence}
 G(Y)\equiv\frac{\fall Y\mu}{\mu!}\pmod p.
\end{equation}
For $1\le j\le \mu-1$,
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pm:smallvalues}
 v_p(G(j))\ge \mu-j+1.
\end{equation}
Every positive integer zero of $T_d$ therefore satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pm:digits}
 Y\equiv j\pmod{p^{\mu-j+1}}\quad\text{for some }1\le j\le \mu-1.
\end{equation}
In particular, it either lies in $\{1,\ldots,\mu-1\}$ or is at least $p^2+1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The falling-factorial expansion is
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pm:partition}
 T_d(Y)=\sum_{\sum ji_j=d}\fall Y\ell\prod_{j\ge1}\frac{h_j^{i_j}}{i_j!},
 \qquad \ell=\sum i_j.
\end{equation}
Since $d<p^2-1$, each $h_j$ occurring here has valuation $0$ or $-1$. Call an
index bad when its valuation is $-1$, and let $\omega$ be the total multiplicity
of bad indices. Every bad index is at least $p-1$, so $\omega\le \mu$. Equality
forces the unique partition $i_{p-1}=\mu$; its total denominator valuation is
$\mu$, because $\mu!$ is a unit.

If $\omega<\mu$, every bad-index multiplicity is less than $p$. The total
multiplicity of good indices is at most $(\mu-\omega)(p-1)$. Since all
multiplicities are below $p^2$, their factorial valuations sum to at most
\[
 \left\lfloor\frac{(\mu-\omega)(p-1)}p\right\rfloor=\mu-\omega-1.
\]
Thus every nondistinguished term in~\eqref{xx:pm:partition} has denominator
valuation at most $\mu-1$. Only $i_{p-1}=\mu$ survives after multiplication by
$p^\mu$ and reduction. Since $ph_{p-1}\equiv1\pmod p$, this
proves~\eqref{xx:pm:congruence}. Every falling factorial has a factor $Y$, so
division by that exact factor also preserves $p$-integrality.

To prove~\eqref{xx:pm:smallvalues}, expand $H^j$ as a product of $j$ series. A
term could have denominator valuation $j$ only if all $j$ chosen indices were
positive and bad. Each such index is $0$ or $-1$ modulo $p$. Their sum cannot
equal $d\equiv-\mu\pmod p$, since $j<\mu<p$. Hence the denominator valuation is
at most $j-1$.

The reduction of $G/Y$ has simple roots precisely $1,\ldots,\mu-1$ modulo $p$.
An integer zero must first lie in one of these classes. At its representative
$j$, $G'(j)$ is a unit. In the Taylor expansion at $j$, if
$1\le v_p(Y-j)<\mu-j+1$, the linear term has strictly smaller valuation than
the constant term and all higher terms. It cannot cancel.
This proves~\eqref{xx:pm:digits} and the asserted localization.
\end{proof}

The denominator comparison uses the classical multinomial method also appearing
in Davis's analysis of powers of $\log(1+z)/z$~\cite{xx-davis}; here the
additional factor $1+z$ produces the denominators $j(j+1)$.

\subsubsection{A derivative-root barrier excludes large exponents}

We reproduce the needed classical barrier estimate. A primary source is
Ravichandran's Theorem~1.4, attributed there to Marcus~\cite{xx-ravichandran}.

\begin{lemma}\label{xx:pm:barrier}
Let $P$ have only real roots, let $\xi>\max\operatorname{root}P$, and put
$\Psi_P(\xi)=P'(\xi)/P(\xi)$. For $0\le\kappa<\deg P$,
\[
 \max\operatorname{root}P^{(\kappa)}<\xi-\frac{\kappa}{\Psi_P(\xi)}.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $\delta=1/\Psi_P(\xi)$. For degree at least two, let
$u_i=\delta/(\xi-\lambda_i)$, where the $\lambda_i$ are the roots with
multiplicity. Then $0<u_i<1$ and $\sum u_i=1$, so $\xi-\delta$ remains above
every root of $P$. With
\[
 w_i=\frac{u_i}{1-u_i},\qquad A=\sum w_i,\qquad B=\sum w_i^2,
\]
logarithmic differentiation gives
\[
 \Psi_{P'}(\xi-\delta)=\delta^{-1}(A-B/A).
\]
Regard the $u_i$ as probability weights. Then
\[
 \mathbb E[w]=A-1,\quad \mathbb E[1+w]=A,\quad
 \mathbb E[w(1+w)]=B,
\]
so $B-A(A-1)=\operatorname{Var}(w)\ge0$. Hence $\Psi_{P'}(\xi-\delta)\le1/\delta$.
A smaller displacement gives the same upper bound by monotonicity of the
logarithmic derivative above the roots. Iterate using this fixed $\delta$; every
shifted point stays strictly above the corresponding derivative's roots.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A universal positivity cone]\label{xx:pm:positive}
For positive integers $Y,d$, if $Y\ge4d$, then $T_d(Y)>0$. Consequently every
positive integer zero satisfies $Y<4d$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Set $m=Y+d$ and $F_m(x)=\prod_{h=0}^{m-1}(x-h)$. Differentiating
$(1+z)^x=\sum_{m\ge0}F_m(x)z^m/m!$ exactly $Y$ times in $x$ and setting $x=Y$
gives
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pm:derivative}
 T_d(Y)=\frac{F_m^{(Y)}(Y)}{m!}.
\end{equation}
Choose $\xi=m+d=Y+2d$. Its barrier is
\[
 \Psi_{F_m}(\xi)=\mathsf H_{Y+2d}-\mathsf H_d
 <\int_d^{Y+2d}\frac{dt}{t}=\log\frac{Y+2d}{d}.
\]
Write $\sigma=Y/d\ge4$. The function $\sigma-2\log(\sigma+2)$ increases for
$\sigma\ge4$ and is positive there, since $\log6<2$. Thus
$Y>2d\Psi_{F_m}(\xi)$. Lemma~\ref{xx:pm:barrier} yields
$\max\operatorname{root}F_m^{(Y)}<\xi-Y/\Psi_{F_m}(\xi)<Y$.
The derivative has positive leading coefficient, so its value at $Y$ is
strictly positive.
\end{proof}

\emph{Editorial note.}  Identity~\eqref{xx:pm:derivative} is
Proposition~\ref{xx:prop:falling} combined with \eqref{xx:eq:diag}, derived
there by the same differentiation of the binomial series.

Condition~\eqref{xx:pm:A} therefore excludes every possible zero with
$Y\ge p^2+1$. This uses real-rootedness only of the falling-factorial
polynomial $F_m$, not of $T_d$ as a polynomial in $Y$.

\begin{remark}[Editorial: the cone and the asymptotic sign boundary]\label{xx:lc:rem:cone}
In the ratio $\rho=m/Y$ of row to column, the cone of
Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:positive} is $\rho\le5/4$, and it holds on every offset,
bounded ones included.  Manuscript~31 of the same batch, placed in the new
sibling report \texttt{power-tower-exponent-supports}, proves positivity of
$b(m,Y)$ eventually on compact ranges of $\rho$ inside $(1,\rho_*)$,
$\rho_*\approx1.29843$, without an effective threshold.  Neither statement
implies the other.  The cone is valid but not sharp: in an exact scan of all
rows $m\le200$ at the intake, no entry in the cone is nonpositive, and the
smallest ratio $m/Y$ of a nonpositive entry is $196/148\approx1.324$.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection{Small exponents and completion of the proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{xx:pm:small}
Let $d,j\ge1$, put $\nu=j-1$ and $\Lambda=\mathsf H_{d-1}-\mathsf H_j$. If $j=1$,
then $T_d(j)\ne0$. If $\nu\ge1$ and $\Lambda\ge4\nu$, then $T_d(j)\ne0$, with
sign $(-1)^{d+j}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The case $j=1$ follows from $T_d(1)=h_d\ne0$. Otherwise
\[
 F_{d+j}(j+u)=(-1)^{d-1}j!(d-1)!\,u\Theta(u),\qquad
 \Theta(u)=\prod_{h=1}^j(1+u/h)\prod_{h=1}^{d-1}(1-u/h).
\]
Its logarithm is $-\Lambda u+\sum_{s\ge2}\gamma_su^s$, with $|\gamma_s|\le2$.
Let $\beta_t=\coef{u}{t}\exp(2u^2/(1-u))$. Coefficientwise majorization gives
\[
 \coef{u}{\nu}\Theta(u)=\frac{(-\Lambda)^\nu}{\nu!}+\mathcal R,\qquad
 \frac{|\mathcal R|}{\Lambda^\nu/\nu!}\le\sum_{t=2}^\nu \beta_t\left(\frac
 \nu\Lambda\right)^t.
\]
For $\chi=\nu/\Lambda\le1/4$, the right side is at most
$\exp(2\chi^2/(1-\chi))-1\le e^{1/6}-1<1$.
Thus $\coef{u}{\nu}\Theta$ has sign $(-1)^\nu$. The derivative
identity~\eqref{xx:pm:derivative} proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\emph{Editorial note.}  At $j=1$ and $j=2$ the lemma re-derives, on every
offset, the column formulas of Proposition~\ref{xx:prop:firstcolumns}:
$T_d(1)=h_d$ is $b(m,1)=(-1)^m(m-2)!$, and the sign of
$\coef{u}{1}\Theta=-(\mathsf H_{d-1}-\mathsf H_2)$ is that of
$2H_{m-3}-3$ in \eqref{xx:eq:col2}.  For larger $j$ it has no counterpart in
this part: every fixed column $Y=j$ has $T_d(j)\ne0$ as soon as
$\mathsf H_{d-1}-\mathsf H_j\ge4(j-1)$, so each column has only finitely many
possible zeros, below an explicit offset (an observation of the intake, not
stated in the manuscript).

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{xx:pm:main}]
Arithmetic localization leaves only $1\le Y\le\mu-1$ and $Y\ge p^2+1$. The
latter is excluded by~\eqref{xx:pm:A} and Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:positive}. For
$1\le j\le\mu-1$, condition~\eqref{xx:pm:B} gives
\[
 \mathsf H_{d-1}-\mathsf H_j\ge4(\mu-2)\ge4(j-1),
\]
so Lemma~\ref{xx:pm:small} excludes all remaining exponents.

For the explicit threshold, $p\ge4\mu$ gives $p^2+1>4\mu(p-1)$,
proving~\eqref{xx:pm:A}. Also
\[
 \mathsf H_{d-1}-\mathsf H_{\mu-1}>\log d-1-\log(\mu-1)>\log(p-1)-1.
\]
If $p-1\ge3^{4\mu-7}$, then $\log3>1$ makes this greater than $4(\mu-2)$.
Thus~\eqref{xx:pm:B} holds as well.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:twice}]
Take $\mu=2$. Condition~\eqref{xx:pm:B} holds for every $p>2$, and
$p^2+1\ge8(p-1)$ for every $p\ge7$. Theorem~\ref{xx:pm:main} handles those
primes.

The cases $p=2,3$ have offsets $2,4$, already prime offsets for the primes
$3,5$. For completeness, that known theorem follows from
$pT_{p-1}(Y)/Y\equiv1\pmod p$: only the one-part contribution $h_{p-1}$ survives
after division by $Y$ and multiplication by $p$.  (Editorial: this is
\eqref{xx:eq:punit} in the proof of Theorem~\ref{xx:thm:prime}.)

For $p=5$, a primitive numerator of $T_8(Y)/Y$ is
\begin{align*}
 F(Y)={}&135Y^7-8820Y^6+229950Y^5-3075240Y^4\\
 &+22538215Y^3-89214420Y^2+172998164Y-122821584.
\end{align*}
Its values modulo $11$ at $Y=0,\ldots,10$ are
$5,9,6,2,2,5,5,4,10,4,3$, all nonzero. This restates one of the source's
small-offset certificates solely to close the uniform all-prime assertion.
Finally $Y=1$ is always explicitly nonzero by~\eqref{xx:pm:H}.
\end{proof}

\emph{Editorial note.}  The source certificate restated here is the row $d=8$
of Theorem~\ref{xx:thm:16}: $F(Y)=R_8(Y+8)$, with $R_8$ the primitive residual
of \eqref{xx:eq:Rd} recorded in
\texttt{data/\allowbreak diagonal\_\allowbreak certificates.json}, and
$T_8(Y)=c_8\,Y\,R_8(Y+8)$ with $c_8=1/1393459200$.  The residues listed above
are those of $R_8$ at $X=8,\ldots,18$, the same eleven classes as the residues
recorded for $X=0,\ldots,10$.  The intake checked the identity
$F(Y)=R_8(Y+8)$ coefficient by coefficient.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:triple}]
Take $\mu=3$. If $p\ge11$, then $p^2+1\ge12(p-1)=4d$. The small sector in
Lemma~\ref{xx:pm:localization} contains only $j=1,2$. For $j=2$, the
coefficient in Lemma~\ref{xx:pm:small} is exactly $-(\mathsf H_{d-1}-\mathsf
H_2)$, which is nonzero for $d>3$. Thus the stronger condition~\eqref{xx:pm:B}
is unnecessary here. The remaining odd primes $3,5,7$ give offsets $6,12,18$,
whose successors $7,13,19$ are prime. The known prime-offset theorem covers
these cases.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:four}]
Take $\mu=4$. If $p\ge17$, then $p^2+1\ge16(p-1)=4d$ and $d\ge64$. The cases
$j=1,2$ are as above. For $j=3$, the relevant coefficient is
\[
 \coef{u}{2}\Theta(u)=\Lambda^2/2+\gamma_2,\qquad
 \Lambda=\mathsf H_{d-1}-\mathsf H_3,\qquad |\gamma_2|<2.
\]
Here
$\Lambda\ge\mathsf H_{63}-\mathsf H_3>\int_4^{64}dt/t=\log16>2$,
so this coefficient is strictly positive. Arithmetic localization and the
positivity cone exclude all other $Y$.

The remaining primes $2,3,5,7,11,13$ give offsets $4,8,16,24,40,48$. They are
covered respectively by the prime-offset theorem at $5$, the displayed
offset-eight certificate, the prime-offset theorem at $17$,
Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:twice} at $13$, the prime-offset theorem at $41$, and
Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:triple} at $17$. This completes the proof.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Why prime-square offsets need additional input}\label{xx:pm:sec:square}

The following obstruction concerns a fixed finite-modulus sieve, not every
possible $p$-adic argument.
\begin{theorem}\label{xx:pm:square}
For every prime $p$, put $d=p^2-1$. Then
\[
 J_p(Y)=p^{p+2}\frac{T_d(Y)}Y\in\Z_{(p)}[Y],\qquad
 J_p(Y)\equiv-(Y^p-Y)\pmod p.
\]
Consequently $J_p$ has exactly $p$ simple roots in $\Z_p$, one in each residue
class modulo $p$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
In~\eqref{xx:pm:partition}, let $v_j=v_p(j(j+1))$, $\omega=\sum i_jv_j$ and
$\mathcal V=\omega+\sum v_p(i_j!)$. We use
\[
 j\ge(p-1)v_j,\qquad j\ge1+(p-2)v_j,
\]
with equality in the first only at $j=p-1$. For $v_j>0$, these follow from
$j\ge p^{v_j}-1$; the case $v_j=0$ is immediate. Set $\mu=p+1$. Then
$\omega\le\mu$, and equality forces $i_{p-1}=\mu$, whose denominator valuation
is $p+2$.

For every other partition put $\delta=\mu-\omega\ge1$. The second inequality
gives $\ell\le p+1+(p-2)\delta$. Since each multiplicity is below $p^2$,
\[
 \sum v_p(i_j!)\le\lfloor\ell/p\rfloor
 \le\delta+1-\left\lceil\frac{2\delta-1}p\right\rceil\le\delta.
\]
Thus $\mathcal V\le p+1$ for all nondistinguished terms. Only the distinguished
term survives after scaling by $p^{p+2}$. Wilson's congruence gives its
coefficient $-1$ modulo $p$ (also for $p=2$). Dividing by the exact factor $Y$
leaves $\prod_{j=1}^p(Y-j)=Y^p-Y$ modulo $p$.

The derivative of the reduced polynomial is $1$. Each residue class is a root
modulo $p$ and has exactly one lift at every higher power, since the linearized
congruence determines the next digit uniquely. These compatible residues are
exactly the asserted simple roots in $\Z_p$.
\end{proof}

Thus every fixed modulus $p^e$ retains positive integer candidates in all $p$
compatible classes. This does not exclude a more refined argument showing that
the particular $p$-adic roots are not ordinary integers. For odd $p$, the
offset is even and has no known reflection factor to remove. The complete
prime-power-offset classification remains open.

\emph{Editorial note.}  Theorem~\ref{xx:st:prime-square-reduction} below
(manuscript~09) is such a refined argument for $p\ge5$: it locates the
$p$-adic roots to three digits and, with the cone, reduces the offset $p^2-1$
to $1\le Y\le p-1$.  The classification of that offset stays open.

\subsubsection{Verification and source scope}

The accompanying standard-library checker
(\texttt{code/05-prime-\allowbreak multiple-\allowbreak verify.py}) constructs
$T_d$ exactly through degree $72$, verifies the coefficient congruences and
$64$ stronger lift inequalities, and compares $3{,}132$ coefficients against an
independent integer-triangle recurrence. It checks $14{,}000$ instances of the
three complete prime families, $15{,}880$ positivity-cone instances, $29{,}456$
localization instances through triangle row $400$, the prime-square congruence
for $p=2,3,5,7$, the full offset-eight identity and its modular certificate,
and the main conditions for $(\mu,p)=(2,11),(3,83),(3,89),(4,3001)$. A second
exact convolution replay
(\texttt{code/05-prime-\allowbreak multiple-\allowbreak replay\_convolution.py})
independently checks $1{,}320$ double-family instances, $3{,}000$ localization
instances, $56$ lifts and $1{,}176$ derivative-bridge identities;
finite-difference interpolation also recovers the offset-eight certificate.
These finite replays support reproducibility; the preceding proofs establish
the infinite statements.

The relevant default-branch source was rechecked on 1 October 2026 and still
had blob \texttt{7344f3aa4dc9344fb8648326e74e0d4bcc33eada}, matching the pinned
source (this report at commit \texttt{ca81647a9}). It already contains the
prime-offset theorem and the finite offset-$16$ classification. The global
exact zero list remains unsettled. No real-rootedness assertion about all $T_d$
is used or proved here.

% ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Stirling residues and finite candidate lists (manuscript 09)}\label{xx:st:sec}

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Abstract of manuscript 09.}\itshape
We identify a scaled block of the Lehmer--Comtet triangle modulo a prime with
an ordinary unsigned Stirling row. The formula determines the first nontrivial
digits of the associated $p$-adic roots. Combined with a derivative-root bound,
it confines every positive integer zero on offset $\mu(p-1)$, for $p>\mu$, to
an explicit set of small exponents and at most one larger integer. It proves
complete nonvanishing on offset $5(p-1)$ for every odd prime. Two different
square families have particularly short remaining lists: offset $(p-1)^2$ has
at most one candidate for odd primes, while offset $p^2-1$ has candidates only
in $1,\ldots,p-1$ for $p\ge5$. These are necessary conditions, not assertions
that the listed candidates are zeros. The global exact zero classification
remains open.
\upshape\par
\smallskip
\emph{Editorial note.}  Printed in the letters of
Table~\ref{xx:lc:tab:notation}.  The manuscript's ``preceding companion'' is
manuscript~12, printed in Subsection~\ref{xx:pm:sec}; manuscript~09 was
delivered with an unchanged copy of it, which is not shipped a second time.
\end{quote}

\subsubsection{Coefficients, scope, and the root bound}

The manuscript defines $H(z)$, $h_j$, $T_d(Y)$ and $b(Y+d,Y)$ as in
\eqref{xx:pm:H} and \eqref{xx:pm:b}, and notes that $T_d$ has the structural
factor $Y$.  Write $c(\mu,j)$ for the unsigned Stirling number of the first
kind, defined by
\[
 X(X+1)\cdots(X+\mu-1)=\sum_{j=1}^\mu c(\mu,j)X^j.
\]
All congruences of rational numbers or polynomials are over $\Z_{(p)}$, the
rationals with denominator coprime to $p$.

\begin{remark}[Manuscript 09's restatement of the cone]\label{xx:st:cone}
The manuscript opens with the positivity bound of
Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:positive} --- for integers $d,Y\ge1$, $Y\ge4d$ implies
$T_d(Y)>0$ --- taken from manuscript~12 ``with its brief derivation included''.
That derivation is the proof of Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:positive} in condensed
form: it applies the classical barrier \cite[Theorem~1.4]{xx-ravichandran} to
$F_m^{(Y)}$, $m=Y+d$, with $\Psi(\xi)=\sum_{h=0}^{m-1}1/(\xi-h)$ at
$\xi=Y+2d$, bounds $\Psi(\xi)=\mathsf H_{Y+2d}-\mathsf H_d<\log((Y+2d)/d)$,
and uses $\sigma>2\log(\sigma+2)$ for $\sigma=Y/d\ge4$.  It is the same
argument, quoting the barrier rather than proving it, and is not printed a
second time.
\end{remark}

This note concerns positive $Y$. In derivatives of $x^x$, the offset $d$ and
log degree $j$ of a monomial $x^{x-d}(\log x)^j$ satisfy $Y=n-d-j$; the
structural line $Y=0$, or $j=n-d$, remains a cancellation line. The inspected
source gives the prime-offset theorem and fixed-offset certificates through
$16$ (Theorems~\ref{xx:thm:prime} and~\ref{xx:thm:16}). The preceding companion
(Subsection~\ref{xx:pm:sec}) proves complete families with multipliers $2,3,4$
and a general sufficiently-large-prime theorem. Here the additional input is an
exact Stirling residue and its root displacement. The partition method belongs
to the classical $p$-adic Bernoulli/Stirling
framework~\cite{xx-davis,xx-adelberg}; the results below are proved directly,
without an exhaustive priority claim.

\subsubsection{The Stirling residue}

\begin{theorem}\label{xx:st:residue}
Let $p>\mu\ge2$ be prime and integer, respectively, and let $1\le j<\mu$. For
$d=\mu(p-1)$,
\begin{equation}\label{xx:st:mainresidue}
 p^{j-1}T_d(j)\equiv
 -\frac{(j!)^2(\mu-j-1)!}{\mu!(\mu-1)!}\,c(\mu,j)\pmod p.
\end{equation}
The left side is $p$-integral. If $p\nmid c(\mu,j)$, then
$v_p(T_d(j))=1-j$, in particular $T_d(j)\ne0$.
Equivalently, the triangle entries satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{xx:st:triangleresidue}
 \frac{b(\mu(p-1)+j,j)}{p^{\mu-j}}
 \equiv(-1)^j\frac{j!}{\mu!}c(\mu,j)\pmod p.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Since $d<p^2-1$, each nonconstant coefficient $h_i$ with $i\le d$ has valuation
$0$ or $-1$. Call an index bad in the latter case. In an ordered-product
expansion of $H^j$, all $j$ indices cannot be bad: their residues are $0$ or
$-1$ modulo $p$, whereas their sum is $d\equiv-\mu$, and $j<\mu<p$. Hence
$p^{j-1}T_d(j)$ is integral at $p$.

Only terms with exactly $j-1$ bad indices survive. The remaining index is
positive and good; a constant there would again make the residue sum
impossible. Choose its position in $j$ ways, and write the bad indices as
\[
 \ell_i p-\varepsilon_i,\quad \ell_i\ge1,\quad \varepsilon_i\in\{0,1\},\qquad
 \lambda=\sum\ell_i,\quad \eta=\sum\varepsilon_i.
\]
The good index is $g_0=(\mu-\lambda)p-\mu+\eta$, positive exactly when
$\lambda\le\mu-1$. Both $g_0$ and $g_0+1$ are units, since
$\eta\le j-1\le\mu-2$. Because $p$ is odd,
\[
 ph_{\ell_i p-\varepsilon_i}\equiv\frac{(-1)^{\ell_i+1}}{\ell_i},\qquad
 h_{g_0}\equiv\frac{(-1)^{\eta-\lambda-1}}{(\mu-\eta)(\mu-\eta-1)}\pmod p.
\]
The product sign is $(-1)^{\eta+j-2}$, independently of $\lambda$. Therefore
the surviving residue is
\begin{equation}\label{xx:st:AB}
 j(-1)^{j-2} A_{\mu,j} B_{\mu,j},
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align*}
 A_{\mu,j}&=\sum_{\substack{\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_{j-1}\ge1\\\ell_1+\cdots+\ell_{j-1}\le \mu-1}}
 \frac1{\ell_1\cdots\ell_{j-1}},\\
 B_{\mu,j}&=\sum_{\eta=0}^{j-1}\frac{(-1)^\eta\binom{j-1}\eta}{(\mu-\eta)(\mu-\eta-1)}.
\end{align*}
For $j=1$, the empty tuple gives $A_{\mu,1}=1$.
The standard logarithmic generating function for unsigned Stirling numbers
gives
\[
 A_{\mu,j}=\coef{t}{\mu-1}\frac{(-\log(1-t))^{j-1}}{1-t}
 =\frac{(j-1)!}{(\mu-1)!}c(\mu,j).
\]
A finite binomial sum and a beta integral give
\begin{align*}
 B_{\mu,j}
 &=\int_0^1x^{\mu-2}(1-x)(1-x^{-1})^{j-1}\,dx\\
 &=(-1)^{j-1}\frac{(\mu-j-1)!j!}{\mu!}.
\end{align*}
The integral converges because $j<\mu$. Substitution into~\eqref{xx:st:AB}
proves~\eqref{xx:st:mainresidue}, including the sign.

Finally put $m=\mu(p-1)+j$. Then $v_p(m!)=\mu-1$. Splitting into $p$-blocks and
applying Wilson's congruence gives
\[
 \frac{m!}{p^{\mu-1}}\equiv
 (-1)^{j+1}\frac{(\mu-1)!}{(\mu-j-1)!}\pmod p.
\]
Combining this with~\eqref{xx:pm:b} proves~\eqref{xx:st:triangleresidue}.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Root displacements and the complete candidate list}

\begin{remark}[Manuscript 09's ordinary congruence]\label{xx:st:ordinary}
The manuscript's Lemma~3.1 is the congruence half of
Lemma~\ref{xx:pm:localization}: for $p>\mu\ge2$ and $d=\mu(p-1)$,
$G(Y)=p^\mu T_d(Y)\in\Z_{(p)}[Y]$, $G(Y)\equiv\fall Y\mu/\mu!\pmod p$, and $G/Y$
is also $p$-integral.  Its proof, which the manuscript calls ``the next
elementary denominator observation'', is a condensed version of the first two
paragraphs of the proof of Lemma~\ref{xx:pm:localization} (at most $\mu$ bad
factors, equality only at $i_{p-1}=\mu$, factorial valuations of the other
terms at most $\mu-\omega-1$) and is not printed a second time.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}\label{xx:st:shift}
For every $j=1,\ldots,\mu-1$, the polynomial $G/Y$ has one simple root
$\rho_j\in\Z_p$ in class $j$, and
\begin{equation}\label{xx:st:shiftformula}
 \rho_j\equiv j+
 (-1)^{\mu-1-j}\frac{j!c(\mu,j)}{(\mu-1)!}\,p^{\mu-j+1}
 \pmod{p^{\mu-j+2}}.
\end{equation}
There are no other roots in $\Z_p$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Congruence~\eqref{xx:pm:congruence} gives precisely the simple residue roots
$1,\ldots,\mu-1$, each with a unique lift at every successive power of $p$. Its
derivative before division by $Y$ is
\[
 G'(j)\equiv\frac{j!(-1)^{\mu-1-j}(\mu-1-j)!}{\mu!}\pmod p.
\]
Theorem~\ref{xx:st:residue} determines $G(j)/p^{\mu-j+1}$ modulo $p$. Taylor
expansion at $j$ therefore gives~\eqref{xx:st:shiftformula}. The quadratic
remainder has valuation at least $2(\mu-j+1)\ge\mu-j+2$. The formula remains
valid if $p\mid c(\mu,j)$; it then asserts an additional zero digit.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite candidate list]\label{xx:st:candidates}
Let $p>\mu\ge2$, $d=\mu(p-1)$, and let $C_p\in\{1,\ldots,p-1\}$ be the least
nonnegative residue of $\binom\mu2$ modulo $p$. Every positive integer zero of
$T_d$ belongs to
\begin{equation}\label{xx:st:list}
 \{j:1\le j<\mu,\ p\mid c(\mu,j)\}
 \ \cup\ \{\mu-1+C_pp^2\},
\end{equation}
where the last candidate may be omitted when it is at least $4d$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:positive} confines zeros to $0<Y<4d$. Since $\mu\le p-1$
and $p\ge3$,
\[
 p^3>4(p-1)^2\ge4\mu(p-1)=4d.
\]
For $j\le\mu-2$, formula~\eqref{xx:st:shiftformula} fixes the root at $j$
modulo at least $p^3$. Its only possible positive integer representative below
$4d$ is $j$ itself, excluded by Theorem~\ref{xx:st:residue} unless
$p\mid c(\mu,j)$.

For $j=\mu-1$, the displacement simplifies to
\[
 \rho_{\mu-1}\equiv \mu-1+\binom\mu2p^2\pmod{p^3}.
\]
Here $p\nmid\binom\mu2$, since $p>\mu$. There is at most one representative in
$0<Y<4d$, namely the last candidate in~\eqref{xx:st:list}. The small
representative $\mu-1$ is not a root, again by Theorem~\ref{xx:st:residue}.
\end{proof}

Thus a sufficient complete-offset criterion is: no entry $c(\mu,j)$,
$1\le j<\mu$, is divisible by $p$, and $\mu-1+C_pp^2\ge4\mu(p-1)$. The
candidates in~\eqref{xx:st:list} are necessary possibilities, not a claimed zero
list.

\begin{remark}[Editorial: a second route to Corollaries~\ref{xx:pm:triple} and~\ref{xx:pm:four}]\label{xx:lc:rem:second-route}
Manuscript~09 does not say so, but its criterion re-proves most of manuscript~12's
small-multiplier corollaries without the harmonic condition~\eqref{xx:pm:B}.
For $\mu=3$, the row is $c(3,1)=2$, $c(3,2)=3$, prime to every $p\ge5$, and
$C_p=3$; the candidate $2+3p^2$ exceeds $12(p-1)$ for every $p$, so
Theorem~\ref{xx:st:candidates} gives Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:triple} for every
$p\ge5$.  For $\mu=4$, the row is $6,11,6$ and $C_p=6\bmod p$: at $p=5$ the
candidate $3+25=28$ lies below $4d=64$ and survives; at $p=11$ the entry
$c(4,2)=11$ leaves the candidate $Y=2$; for $p=7$ and every $p\ge13$ the list is
empty, so the criterion gives Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:four} for $p\ge7$, $p\ne11$.
For $\mu=2$ the candidate $1+p^2$ is at least $8(p-1)$ exactly when $p\ge7$,
which is condition~\eqref{xx:pm:A}.  The exceptional primes are covered, in
manuscript~12, by the prime-offset theorem and the offset-eight certificate.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection{Two consequences of the Stirling row}

\begin{corollary}\label{xx:st:five}
For every odd prime $p$ and every positive integer $Y$,
\[
 T_{5(p-1)}(Y)\ne0.
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The unsigned Stirling row for $\mu=5$, omitting its last entry $1$, is
$24,50,35,10$. Its only prime divisor above $5$ is $7$. For every prime
$p\ge11$, the small set in~\eqref{xx:st:list} is empty, $C_p=10$, and
$4+10p^2>20(p-1)$. Theorem~\ref{xx:st:candidates} applies.
The primes $3,5,7$ give offsets $10,20,30$, covered respectively by the
prime-offset theorem at $11$, the double family at $11$
(Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:twice}), and the prime-offset theorem at $31$. The prime
$2$ is correctly excluded: offset $5$ has its known reflection zero at $Y=4$.
\end{proof}

\emph{Editorial note.}  The reflection zero at offset $5$, $Y=4$ is
$b(9,4)=0$, the case $h=2$ of Proposition~\ref{xx:prop:symmetry}.

\begin{corollary}\label{xx:st:squares}
For every odd prime $p$,
\[
 T_{(p-1)^2}(Y)=0,\quad Y\in\Z_{>0}
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad Y=p^2+p-2.
\]
No assertion is made that this one remaining value vanishes.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Take $\mu=p-1$. Over $\mathbb F_p$,
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^{p-1}c(p-1,j)X^j
 =X(X+1)\cdots(X+p-2)
 =\frac{X^p-X}{X-1}=X+\cdots+X^{p-1}.
\]
Thus every relevant Stirling entry is a unit and
$C_p=\binom{p-1}2\bmod p=1$. Theorem~\ref{xx:st:candidates} leaves only
$p-2+p^2$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{A different square family: offset \texorpdfstring{$p^2-1$}{p squared minus one}}

This subsection concerns $p^2-1$, not the offset $(p-1)^2$ in
Corollary~\ref{xx:st:squares}.

\begin{remark}[Manuscript 09's prime-square lemma]\label{xx:st:prime-square-base}
The manuscript's Lemma~5.1 is the congruence of Theorem~\ref{xx:pm:square}: for
any prime $p$ and $d=p^2-1$, $J(Y)=p^{p+2}T_d(Y)/Y\in\Z_{(p)}[Y]$ and
$J(Y)\equiv-(Y^p-Y)\pmod p$; its $J$ is $J_p$.  The manuscript says so
(``This is the prime-square denominator lemma from'' the companion) and repeats
the estimates of the proof of Theorem~\ref{xx:pm:square} --- the two
inequalities for $j$ in terms of $v_j=v_p(j(j+1))$, the bound $\omega\le p+1$
with equality only at $i_{p-1}=p+1$, and $\sum v_p(i_j!)\le\delta$ for the other
terms --- which are not printed a second time.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}\label{xx:st:prime-square-reduction}
For every prime $p\ge5$,
\[
 T_{p^2-1}(Y)=0,\quad Y\in\Z_{>0}
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 1\le Y\le p-1.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $d=p^2-1$.  Theorem~\ref{xx:pm:square} gives one simple $p$-adic root in
each residue class. For $1\le a\le p-1$, expand $H^a$ directly. Every index at
most $d=p^2-1$ has denominator valuation at most one, except $d$ itself, whose
valuation is two. If that index occurs, all other chosen indices are zero.
Hence
\[
 v_p(T_d(a))\ge-\max(a,2),\qquad
 v_p(J_p(a))\ge p+2-\max(a,2)\ge3.
\]
The corresponding root is therefore $a$ modulo $p^3$.

To determine the zero-residue branch, expand $H^p$. Every degree-$d$ term has a
multinomial coefficient divisible by $p$: a pure $p$th-power term would require
$pi=d$, which is impossible. A term containing index $d$ has valuation $-1$,
greater than $1-p$. Otherwise all bad indices have valuation $-1$. Terms with at
most $p-1$ bad factors have valuation at least $2-p$.

If all $p$ factors are bad, their minimum total degree is $p(p-1)$, leaving
increment $p-1$. A bad index greater than $p$ is at least $2p-1$, already
requiring increment $p$. Thus the unique all-bad pattern has one index $p-1$ and
$p-1$ indices $p$. Its contribution is $p h_{p-1}h_p^{p-1}$, so
\[
 p^{p-1}T_d(p)\equiv1\pmod p,\qquad
 J_p(p)\equiv p^2\pmod{p^3}.
\]
Since $J_p'(p)\equiv1$, the zero-residue branch equals $p-p^2$ modulo $p^3$.

By Corollary~\ref{xx:pm:positive}, every positive integer zero has
$Y<4(p^2-1)$. For $p\ge5$, both $p^3$ and $p^3-p^2+p$ exceed this bound. The
only possible representatives of the identified branches in that interval are
$1,\ldots,p-1$.
\end{proof}

The remaining small-column assertions are not proved here. In particular, the
existence of $p$-adic roots is fully compatible with the absence of ordinary
integer roots; the archimedean bound is essential to both square-family
reductions.

\subsubsection{Verification and remaining scope}

The standard-library checker
(\texttt{code/04-stirling-\allowbreak residues-\allowbreak verify\_stirling.py})
independently expands rational powers of $H$ and verifies $134$ instances each
of the Stirling residue, the scaled triangle congruence, and the
derivative/root-displacement formula, covering multipliers through $8$ and
coefficient degrees through $144$. Separate exact computations lift the
prime-square roots for $p=3,5,7,11$ and check the single-candidate values for
$(p-1)^2$ at $p=3,5,7,11,13,17,19$. Those finitely many values are nonzero; they
do not establish a general nonvanishing theorem for the remaining candidate. A
separate integer falling-product replay verifies $162$ instances each of the two
residues, derivatives and first displacements, all prime-square branches through
$p=19$, and the seven stated single candidates. It uses finite $p$-steps for
derivatives and passes with Python assertions disabled.

The repository comparison is relative to the pinned source, whose relevant
default-branch blob was unchanged at the check on 1 October 2026. Our bounded
literature review found the classical partition, higher-order Bernoulli, and
Stirling valuation framework~\cite{xx-davis,xx-adelberg}; the formulas in this
note are proved directly for the Lehmer--Comtet specialization. No worldwide
priority claim, complete global zero list, or formal proof-assistant
verification is asserted.

% ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Uniform \texorpdfstring{$p$}{p}-adic valuations (manuscript 17)}\label{xx:pn:sec}

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Abstract of manuscript 17.}\itshape
We give exact valuations for two infinite families of coefficients of
$((1+z)\log(1+z)/z)^Y$. The first extends the inspected source's prime-offset
theorem to offsets $(p-1)\mu$ when $\mu\le p^{v_p(Y)}$. A second treats offsets
$(p-1)\mu+1$ for odd $p$ under an explicit nonresonance condition. The proof
adapts Davis's minimal-valuation multinomial method directly to the different
coefficient denominators $j(j+1)$, including a lower bound that handles
competitors with many factors. The resulting criteria exclude new infinite
families of possible Lehmer--Comtet zeros; the complete zero classification
remains open.
\upshape\par
\smallskip
\emph{Editorial note.}  Printed in the letters of
Table~\ref{xx:lc:tab:notation}: the manuscript's $m$ is $\mu$, its offset $r$
(and $n$ in its proofs) is $d$.
\end{quote}

\subsubsection{The coefficient and its derivative interpretation}

The manuscript writes $H(z)$, $h_j$ and $T_d(Y)$ as in~\eqref{xx:pm:H}.
Throughout this subsection $Y$ is a \emph{positive integer}; the structural case
$Y=0$ is excluded. We write $v_p$ for the valuation on nonzero rational numbers,
so negative values are allowed.

The first-kind Lehmer--Comtet numbers satisfy
$b(Y+d,Y)=\frac{(Y+d)!}{Y!}T_d(Y)$, which is~\eqref{xx:pm:b}.  They determine
the expanded derivative coefficients of $x^x$: at total derivative order
$n\ge m=Y+d$, the monomial
\[
 x^{x-d}(\log x)^{n-m}
\]
has coefficient $\binom nm b(m,Y)$. Thus nonvanishing of a fixed $b(m,Y)$
proves nonvanishing on its entire persistent derivative line. These identities
are proved in the inspected source (Theorem~\ref{xx:thm:expansion} and
Lemma~\ref{xx:lem:diagdegree}) and belong to the classical Lehmer--Comtet
framework.

\begin{theorem}[Principal progression]\label{xx:pn:principal}
Let $p$ be prime, $\mu\ge1$ and $e=v_p(Y)$. If $\mu\le p^e$, then
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pn:mainval}
 v_p\bigl(T_{(p-1)\mu}(Y)\bigr)=e-v_p(\mu)-\mu.
\end{equation}
In particular the coefficient is nonzero.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Shifted progression]\label{xx:pn:shifted}
Let $p$ be an odd prime, $\mu\ge1$ and $e=v_p(Y)$. If
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pn:conditions}
 \mu<p^e,\qquad p\nmid (p-1)\mu+1,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pn:shiftval}
 v_p\bigl(T_{(p-1)\mu+1}(Y)\bigr)=e-\mu.
\end{equation}
The second condition in~\eqref{xx:pn:conditions} is equivalently
$\mu\not\equiv1\pmod p$.
\end{theorem}

Davis~\cite[Theorem 1.1]{xx-davis} proves the valuation analogous
to~\eqref{xx:pn:mainval} for powers of $\log(1+z)/z$. Multiplication by
$(1+z)^Y$ cannot simply be ignored. We instead prove both claims directly
from~\eqref{xx:pm:H}, adapting the same minimal-multinomial mechanism. The
$\mu=1$ case of Theorem~\ref{xx:pn:principal} is exactly the source's
prime-offset valuation.

\emph{Editorial note.}  That valuation is $v_p(T_{p-1}(Y))=v_p(Y)-1$, in the
proof of Theorem~\ref{xx:thm:prime}.  The proof below is a second route to it:
the one in this part reduces $pT_{p-1}(Y)/Y$ modulo $p$
(\eqref{xx:eq:punit}), the one below compares the valuations of all terms of
the multinomial expansion.

\subsubsection{Two elementary valuation bounds}

For a multiplicity vector $I=(i_1,\ldots,i_d)$ with
\[
 \sum_jji_j=d,\qquad \ell=\sum_ji_j,
\]
the corresponding term in the multinomial expansion of $T_d(Y)$ is
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pn:term}
 C_I=\frac{\fall Y\ell}{\prod_ji_j!}\prod_jh_j^{i_j},
 \qquad \fall Y\ell=Y(Y-1)\cdots(Y-\ell+1).
\end{equation}
Terms with $\ell>Y$ are zero and may be omitted.

\begin{lemma}\label{xx:pn:falling}
If $1\le\ell\le Y$ and $\ell<p^{e+1}$, where $e=v_p(Y)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pn:fallingbound}
 v_p(\fall Y\ell)\ge e+v_p((\ell-1)!).
\end{equation}
There is equality when $\ell\le p^e$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $1\le s<\ell<p^{e+1}$, one has $v_p(s)\le e$. If $v_p(s)<e$, then
$v_p(Y-s)=v_p(s)$. If $v_p(s)=e$, both $Y$ and $s$ are divisible by $p^e$,
giving $v_p(Y-s)\ge v_p(s)$. Sum these inequalities over $s$ and include the
factor $Y$. When $\ell\le p^e$, every $s<\ell$ has valuation below $e$, so all
comparisons are equalities.
\end{proof}

In particular, we do \emph{not} assert the equality when a competing term has
more than $p^e$ factors; such competitors can occur for $p>2$.

The integer identity
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pn:integer}
 \frac{d(\ell-1)!}{\prod_ji_j!}
 =\sum_{j:i_j>0}j\,
 \frac{(\ell-1)!}{(i_j-1)!\prod_{h\ne j}i_h!}
 \in\Z_{>0}
\end{equation}
gives
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pn:multibound}
 v_p((\ell-1)!)-\sum_jv_p(i_j!)\ge-v_p(d).
\end{equation}
Every summand on the right of~\eqref{xx:pn:integer} is an integer multiple of a
multinomial coefficient. Combining~\eqref{xx:pn:term},
Lemma~\ref{xx:pn:falling} and~\eqref{xx:pn:multibound}, whenever
$\ell<p^{e+1}$ we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pn:master}
 v_p(C_I)\ge e-v_p(d)-\sum_ji_jv_p(j(j+1)).
\end{equation}

Because consecutive integers are coprime, $v=v_p(j(j+1))\ge1$ forces
$j\ge p^v-1\ge v(p-1)$. Equality occurs only for $v=1$ and $j=p-1$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pn:cost}
 v_p(j(j+1))\le\frac{j}{p-1},
 \quad\text{with equality exactly at }j=p-1.
\end{equation}
The case $v=0$ is strictly below $j/(p-1)$ since $j\ge1$.

\subsubsection{The principal progression}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{xx:pn:principal}]
Set $d=(p-1)\mu$. Every nonzero term has
\[
 \ell\le d\le(p-1)p^e<p^{e+1},
\]
so~\eqref{xx:pn:master} applies. Inequality~\eqref{xx:pn:cost} gives
\[
 \sum_ji_jv_p(j(j+1))\le\mu,
\]
with equality only for $I=\mu\mathbf e_{p-1}$, where $\mathbf e_j$ has a one at
position $j$. Every other vector has this integer sum at most $\mu-1$. Since
$v_p(d)=v_p(\mu)$, its term has valuation at least
\[
 e-v_p(\mu)-\mu+1.
\]
The distinguished term is $\binom Y\mu h_{p-1}^\mu$. It is nonzero because
$\mu\le p^e\le Y$. The equality part of Lemma~\ref{xx:pn:falling} gives
\[
 v_p\binom Y\mu=e-v_p(\mu),\qquad v_p(h_{p-1})=-1.
\]
Thus its valuation is $e-v_p(\mu)-\mu$, uniquely smaller than all competitors. A
sum with a unique least valuation has that valuation,
proving~\eqref{xx:pn:mainval}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Editorial: manuscripts 12 and 17 on a common range]\label{xx:lc:rem:pm-pn}
For $p>\mu\ge2$ the hypothesis $\mu\le p^e$ of Theorem~\ref{xx:pn:principal}
says $p\mid Y$.  On that range the theorem also follows from the proof of
Lemma~\ref{xx:pm:localization}: $G/Y$ is $p$-integral and congruent to
$(Y-1)\cdots(Y-\mu+1)/\mu!$, which at $Y\equiv0$ is the unit
$(-1)^{\mu-1}/\mu$; so $v_p(G(Y))=v_p(Y)$ and $v_p(T_d(Y))=e-\mu$, which is
\eqref{xx:pn:mainval} since $v_p(\mu)=0$.  Manuscript~12 states only that the
zeros lie in the classes $1\le j\le\mu-1$, not the valuation.  Conversely, its
complete-offset theorems are not implied by manuscript~17, which needs $p\mid Y$
there.  Manuscript~17 is new relative to manuscript~12 and to this part for
$\mu\ge p$ (for instance every offset at $p=2$ or $3$) and on the shifted, odd
offsets of Theorem~\ref{xx:pn:shifted}.  The three manuscripts share the
``bad index'' multinomial count after Davis; \eqref{xx:pn:master} is its
cleanest general form.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection{One step above the progression}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{xx:pn:shifted}]
Set $d=(p-1)\mu+1$. The strict bound $\mu<p^e$ again gives $d<p^{e+1}$, and
$p\nmid d$ removes the term $v_p(d)$ in~\eqref{xx:pn:master}. The
denominator-valuation sum is an integer at most $\mu$. If it is at most
$\mu-1$, the corresponding term has valuation at least $e-\mu+1$.

Otherwise its value is $\mu$, and hence
\[
 \sum_ji_j\bigl(j-(p-1)v_p(j(j+1))\bigr)=1.
\]
For odd $p$, the only indices whose parenthesized value is zero or one are
$j=p-1$ (value zero), and $j=1,p$ (value one). Indeed, valuation at least two
would require
\[
 j\ge p^v-1>v(p-1)+1\qquad(v\ge2,p\ge3).
\]
Therefore only the two vectors
\[
 \mu\mathbf e_{p-1}+\mathbf e_1,\qquad (\mu-1)\mathbf e_{p-1}+\mathbf e_p
\]
remain. Put $A=h_{p-1}$, $B=h_p$. Their combined contribution is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{xx:pn:pair}
 \frac{\fall Y\mu}{(\mu-1)!}A^{\mu-1}
       \left(B+\frac{Y-\mu}{2\mu}A\right).
\end{equation}
The entire prefactor outside the brackets has valuation $e-\mu+1$. Since
$\mu<p^e$, one has $v_p(\mu)<e$, and so $(Y-\mu)/\mu\equiv-1\pmod p$.
Moreover~\eqref{xx:pm:H} gives $pA\equiv pB\equiv1\pmod p$. Multiplying the
bracket in~\eqref{xx:pn:pair} by $p$ gives
\[
 1-\frac12=\frac12\not\equiv0\pmod p.
\]
Thus the bracket has valuation $-1$, and the pair has valuation $e-\mu$,
strictly below every other term. This proves~\eqref{xx:pn:shiftval}.
\end{proof}

The conditions have real content. Allowing $\mu=p^e$ in the shifted theorem
would include the symmetry zeros: for example $p=3,\mu=3,Y=6$ gives
$T_7(6)=0$. Dropping $p\nmid d$ can also invalidate the valuation formula:
$p=3,\mu=4,Y=9$ gives
\[
 T_9(9)=\frac{2021}{268800},\qquad v_3(T_9(9))=-1,
\]
whereas $e-\mu=-2$. This is a failure of the unrestricted valuation formula, not
a new coefficient zero.

\emph{Editorial note.}  $T_7(6)=0$ is the symmetry zero $b(13,6)=0$, the case
$h=3$ of Proposition~\ref{xx:prop:symmetry}.

\subsubsection{New infinite regions and the remaining obstruction}

By~\eqref{xx:pm:b}, either theorem gives the valuation of $b(Y+d,Y)$ by adding
$v_p((Y+d)!)-v_p(Y!)$. Here are four infinite divisibility progressions beyond
the source's completed offsets $d\le16$; each listed $d+1$ is composite.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|c|c|c}
$d$&$p$&condition on $Y$&$v_p(T_d(Y))$\\\hline
17&2&$32\mid Y$&$v_2(Y)-17$\\
17&3&$9\mid Y$&$v_3(Y)-8$\\
20&3&$27\mid Y$&$v_3(Y)-10$\\
21&5&$25\mid Y$&$v_5(Y)-5$
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For their first positive $Y$, the respective valuations of $b(Y+d,Y)$ are
$3,0,1,1$. The supplied exact output records the corresponding integers.

Every hypothetical additional integer zero must avoid all divisibility
progressions covered by the two theorems. In particular, taking $p=2$ in
Theorem~\ref{xx:pn:principal} gives the necessary restriction
\[
 T_d(Y)=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 2^{v_2(Y)}<d.
\]
The criteria do not exclude every pair $(d,Y)$, and they do not prove the
conjectured complete zero list consisting of the symmetry family and $b(8,5)$.

\subsubsection{Verification and positioning}

An integer-triangle verifier
(\texttt{code/06-padic-\allowbreak valuations-\allowbreak verify\_padic.py})
checks both valuation formulas through row $m=360$, for the eleven primes
through $31$: 7,749 principal and 2,299 shifted instances pass. A separate
direct rational power-series recurrence checks 3,073 principal and 437 shifted
instances for $1\le Y\le120$, $d\le60$. A third checker, using direct
integer-scaled convolution, passes 2,421 principal and 463 shifted cases,
including 1,352 principal boundary cases $\mu=p^e$, and 5,500 denominator
checks. These independent finite checks supplement the infinite proof and are
included with their exact outputs.

The source comparison is with the pinned ProveIt manuscript, this report at
commit \texttt{ca81647a9}; its current default-branch file was checked again on
1 October 2026 and still contains the prime-offset theorem and fixed-offset
certificates through 16. Davis's paper~\cite{xx-davis} supplies the classical
valuation method for a related log series. The contribution here is its direct
adaptation to~\eqref{xx:pm:H}, the two-term shifted calculation, and the
resulting exact nonvanishing families for this coefficient array. No
exhaustive priority claim is made. A complete integer-root classification, and
hence the exact A293239 term-count formula, remain open. The proof is not a
proof-assistant formalization or externally refereed publication.

\subsubsection{Provenance}

\texttt{1512ef835} and were placed in this report by commit
\texttt{d292c6765}, as its additions~05, 04 and~06.  All three are dated
1 October 2026, carry the line ``Research note prepared for Vladimir
Reshetnikov with OpenAI'', and are pinned to ProveIt commit
\texttt{ca81647a9f679e96\allowbreak d49ffdbc54b0c0ab\allowbreak 13d10a36},
whose text of this article (blob \texttt{7344f3aa4dc9}) they cite.

\item Manuscript~12 (addition~05), \emph{Complete Nonvanishing on
 Prime-Multiple Offsets: Lehmer--Comtet coefficients and derivatives of $x^x$},
 6-page PDF, archive
 \texttt{ProveIt\_\allowbreak Lehmer\_\allowbreak Comtet\_\allowbreak Prime\_\allowbreak Multiple\_\allowbreak Nonvanishing.zip}.
 Its six sections form
 Subsection~\ref{xx:pm:sec}.  It contributes Theorem~\ref{xx:pm:main}, its
 three corollaries, the localization, barrier, cone and small-exponent lemmas,
 and Theorem~\ref{xx:pm:square}.
\item Manuscript~09 (addition~04), \emph{Stirling Residues and Finite
 Candidate Lists: Arithmetic restrictions on Lehmer--Comtet zeros}, 6-page PDF,
 archive
 \texttt{ProveIt\_\allowbreak Lehmer\_\allowbreak Comtet\_\allowbreak Stirling\_\allowbreak Residues\_\allowbreak and\_\allowbreak Candidate\_\allowbreak Lists.zip}.
 Its six sections form

 displacements, the candidate list, Corollaries~\ref{xx:st:five}
 and~\ref{xx:st:squares}, and Theorem~\ref{xx:st:prime-square-reduction}.
\item Manuscript~17 (addition~06), \emph{Uniform $p$-adic Nonvanishing: Two
 valuation families for Lehmer--Comtet coefficients}, 4-page PDF, archive

 Subsection~\ref{xx:pn:sec}.  It contributes Theorems~\ref{xx:pn:principal}
 and~\ref{xx:pn:shifted} and their valuation lemmas.
\end{itemize}
Every theorem, lemma and corollary of the three manuscripts is printed, with
its proof and with every non-claim and limitation the manuscripts state; none
was weakened or strengthened.  The one exception to printing in full is
recorded in the second item below.  The editorial choices were these.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
\item The additions are placed as subsections at the end of
 Part~\ref{xx:part}, so that no existing section, theorem or equation number
 moves; the order is 12, 09, 17, because 09 builds on 12.

 and~\ref{xx:st:prime-square-base}.  The three manuscripts' identical
 definitions of $H$, $T_d$ and $b(Y+d,Y)$ are printed once,
 as~\eqref{xx:pm:H} and~\eqref{xx:pm:b}.
\item Results of this part that the manuscripts re-derive are credited where
 they occur (the list in Subsection~\ref{xx:lc:sec:intro}); the restated
 offset-eight certificate is kept, with a note identifying it as $R_8(Y+8)$.
\item The letters are changed as in Table~\ref{xx:lc:tab:notation}, with no
 change of normalization.  The manuscripts' citations of ``ProveIt, Part~I''
 are re-targeted to this part, manuscript~09's citations of its companion to
 Subsection~\ref{xx:pm:sec}, and their file names to the shipped names.  The
 references of Davis, Ravichandran and Adelberg--Lengyel are merged into the
 bibliography.
\item Remarks~\ref{xx:lc:rem:cone}, \ref{xx:lc:rem:second-route}
 and~\ref{xx:lc:rem:pm-pn}, the notation subsection and the editorial notes are

Not shipped: the three manuscripts' sources, PDFs and \texttt{README.md}

\subsubsection{Formal status}\label{xx:lc:sec:formal}

Nothing in this addition is formalized, and its placement in this report
confers no formal status.  No Lean or Rocq file in the repository mentions the
Lehmer--Comtet numbers, OEIS A008296 or OEIS A293239.  The manuscripts claim no formal
verification, no referee and no exhaustive priority.
