Two index translations follow from the table and are used repeatedly:
\[
 c_N(j,k)=c_{N,\,k+d,\,k},\qquad
 N=2k+2d+1+q\iff n=2(j_{\mathrm{III}}+m)+1+d_{\mathrm{III}},
\]
where the subscript $\mathrm{III}$ marks the letters of the rest of this part.
In particular the tail line $q=0$ is the borderline $n=2k+1$ of
Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:positive}, and the region $q\ge1$ is the region
$n\ge2k+2$ of \eqref{xxb:eq:gap-region}.

\emph{Renamed symbols.}  Where the manuscript's letter names a different object
of this part, the object is renamed here, with no change of normalization:
the manuscript's residual polynomial $P_d(z)$ is $\Pi_d(\zeta)$ (this part's
$P_n$ is the canonical polynomial); its centered polynomial $Q_k^{(r)}$ is
$\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$; its $A_{M,k}(s)$ is $\mathcal A_{M,k}(s)$; its base moment
$B_d(r)$ is $\mathcal B_d(r)$ and its bulk count $B(N)$ is $\beta(N)$; its
half-interval logarithm $L=\log(t/(1-t))$ is $\tau$ (this part's $L$ is
$\log x$); its hole indicator $h_d(N)$ is $\chi_d(N)$ (the manuscript also used
$h_d$ for a second object, kept below as $h_d(\tau)$); its
$m=\lfloor(d-1)/2\rfloor$ is $\nu_d$ and the $m$ of its count formulas is
$\rho$ (this part's $m$ is the deficit); its Mellin transform $F_{d,k}$ is
$\mathcal F_{d,k}$ (this part's $F_n$ is the falling factorial); its generating
variable $z$ is $\xi$; the auxiliary combination $h(t)$ in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{xxb:dc:lem:mellin} is $\psi(t)$; and product indices written $j$ in
the manuscript are $\ell$.  The manuscript's falling factorial
$(s)_{\underline r}$ is printed $\fall{s}{r}$, as in the rest of this article.
The letters $s$, $t$, $u$, $w$, $y$ are local to the paragraph in which they
are introduced; as in the manuscript, $y$ is a centered variable in
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:models} and $1-t$ in
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:integral}, and $s$ is both a polynomial variable and
$2t-1$.

\subsubsection{Main results of the addition}

Put
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:gamma}
 \varphi(w)=(1+w)^2\log(1+w),\qquad
 \gamma(k,d,M)=[w^M](2+w)^k\varphi(w)^d.
\end{equation}
The depth is $d$, the logarithmic degree is $k$, and $M$ is a coefficient
index.  All three are nonnegative integers.

\begin{theorem}[Bulk positivity; second route to Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:positive}]\label{xxb:dc:thm:bulk}
For every $d\ge1$ and $k\ge0$,
\[
 d\le M\le k+2d+1\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\gamma(k,d,M)>0.
\]
\end{theorem}

In the coordinates of this part the hypothesis reads $m\ge1$, $k\le n\le2k+1$,
so the theorem is exactly the case $m\ge1$ of
Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:positive}; with \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:d0} below, which is
Proposition~\ref{xxb:prop:top}, it is all of it.  The manuscript's proof in
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:bulk} goes through a centered factorization instead
of the transformation \eqref{xxb:eq:mobius}.

For the remaining region it is useful to put
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:q}
 q=M-k-2d-1\ge0,
 \qquad H_{d,k,q}=\frac{(k+2d+1+q)!}{d!}\gamma(k,d,k+2d+1+q).
\end{equation}
The number $H_{d,k,q}$ is an integer.  The $q=0$ line overlaps the already
positive boundary of the bulk.

\begin{theorem}[Effective fixed-depth finiteness]\label{xxb:dc:thm:finite}
For each $d\ge3$ there are explicit positive integers $K_d,Q_d$, given in
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:effective}, such that whenever $k\ge K_d$ or
$q\ge Q_d$,
\[
 H_{d,k,q}=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad q=2d\ \text{and}\ k+d\equiv0\pmod2.
\]
Thus the complete fixed-depth nonvanishing conjecture is equivalent to a
finite, effectively specified collection of integer nonzero tests.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Complete first-four-depth classification]\label{xxb:dc:thm:four}
For $1\le d\le4$, $k\ge0$, and $M\ge d$,
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:classification}
 \gamma(k,d,M)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 M=k+4d+1\ \text{and}\ k+d\equiv0\pmod2.
\end{equation}
For $d=1,2$ the proof is entirely analytic.  For $d=3,4$ it uses finite
exact-integer certificates after a proved infinite-to-finite reduction.
\end{theorem}

In the coordinates of this part: for $1\le m\le4$ and every $n\ge k$, the
coefficient $c_{n,k,j}$ vanishes if and only if $k$ is even and $n=4k-2j+1$.
For $m=1,2$ this is Theorems~\ref{xxb:thm:positive}, \ref{xxb:thm:m1}
and~\ref{xxb:thm:m2}; for $m=3,4$ it is new, and it removes $m=3,4$ from the
region \eqref{xxb:eq:gap-region}.

The practical exceptional rectangles for this theorem are
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:rectangles}
 \begin{array}{ll}
 d=3:&0\le k\le12,\quad0\le q\le37,\\
 d=4:&0\le k\le83,\quad0\le q\le184.
 \end{array}
\end{equation}
They contain 48 symmetry zeros and 15,986 nonzero entries in total.
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:certificates} explains every finite obligation and
its verification.  Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:zero-count} gives a separate
all-depth bound on the number of zeros for each fixed $k$.

\subsubsection{What is and is not new here}

The manuscript's own assessment, relative to the repository notes it read:
the support reduction, the target quasipolynomial, the reflection idea, and
the first two depths are prior repository material \cite{xxbdc-notes}.
Meixner--Pollaczek polynomials are classical \cite{xxbdc-dlmf}; no new
orthogonal-polynomial family is proposed.  The contribution is their use to
prove all-depth bulk positivity, the effective uniform fixed-depth reduction,
and its rigorous completion at depths three and four.  The real-zero bound and
term-count lower bound are additional consequences.

\emph{Relative to this part} the assessment is narrower.  All-depth bulk
positivity is Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:positive}, and the reflection zeros, the
deficit-one and deficit-two classification and the upper bound are all proved
above; the manuscript's proofs of them are second routes.  New relative to this
part are: the centered factorization (Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:factor}); the
closed form of the branch-cut kernel for every depth and its folding
(Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:integral}); the monotone comparisons and the
effective finite reduction at every fixed depth $d\ge3$
(Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:finite}); the completion at deficits three and four
(Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four}); the zero-count bound
(Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:zero-count}); and the lower bound of
Corollary~\ref{xxb:dc:cor:lower}, which improves $\Lambda(n)$ of
Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:main-bounds}.

The manuscript's literature and repository checks support it as a continuation
beyond the inspected A290268 notes, not an assertion of absolute historical
priority.  Related results on generalized Bernoulli polynomials, including
integer interlacing in the order-equals-degree family, appear in
\v{S}tampach's work \cite{xxbdc-stampach}.  Those results concern a different
parameter regime and do not by themselves settle the coefficient nonvanishing
considered here.  The addition is a research draft with explicit proof and
computation boundaries, not an announcement that the entire OEIS conjecture has
been solved.

\subsection{Depth coordinates: exact coefficient and polynomial models}\label{xxb:dc:sec:models}

\subsubsection{The lattice recurrence and the depth reduction}
For $x>0$, write
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:lattice}
 \frac{d^N}{dx^N}x^{x^2}
 =x^{x^2}\sum_{j\in\Z}\sum_{k\ge0}c_N(j,k)x^j(\log x)^k.
\end{equation}
Only finitely many coefficients are nonzero, and all are integers.  The
sequence in question is $a(N)=\#\{(j,k):c_N(j,k)\ne0\}$.  The OEIS entry,
proposed by Reshetnikov in 2017 and subsequently given alternative forms by
Luschny, records the conjecture
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:countconj}
 a(2\rho)=2\rho^2+2\rho+1,\qquad
 a(2\rho-1)=2\rho^2-\lfloor \rho/4\rfloor\quad(\rho\ge1),
\end{equation}
which is \eqref{xxb:eq:U-parity} with $\rho=r+1$ in the odd case.  The
equivalent rational generating function remained labeled conjectural in the
entry accessed for the manuscript on 29 September 2026 \cite{oeis290268}.
The precise coefficient model is used rather than the output conventions of
any computer algebra system.

The functions $x^j(\log x)^k$ appearing in a finite expansion are linearly
independent; this is Lemma~\ref{xxb:lem:unique}, and the manuscript's
argument is the same one.  After setting $x=e^t$, a putative relation becomes a
finite sum of exponentials $\exp(jt)$ times polynomials in $t$.  Divide by the
largest exponential and let $t\to+\infty$; the corresponding polynomial must
vanish.  Repeat.  Thus the coefficient support is an intrinsic object within
this normal form.

The repository notes already reduce the support problem to a three-parameter
coefficient and prove or discuss several initial strata \cite{xxbdc-notes}.
The Lean module \texttt{Main.lean} explicitly consumes the support
characterization as a hypothesis in its support-to-count theorem
\cite{xxbdc-main}; that conditional theorem is not treated as an existing
proof of the conjecture (Section~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:formal}).

Differentiating a basis term in \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:lattice} yields
\begin{align}\label{xxb:dc:eq:rec-lattice}
 c_{N+1}(j,k)={}&2c_N(j-1,k-1)+c_N(j-1,k)\\
 &+(j+1)c_N(j+1,k)+(k+1)c_N(j+1,k+1),\notag
\end{align}
with $c_0(0,0)=1$ and all other initial coefficients zero.  Negative
logarithmic indices are interpreted as zero.  This is the recurrence
\eqref{xxb:eq:c-recurrence} re-indexed, and it is verbatim the definition of
\texttt{coeff} in the Lean development.  In particular,
\[
 |j|\le N,\quad j\equiv N\pmod2,\quad 0\le k\le(N+j)/2
\]
are necessary for a nonzero coefficient.

\begin{proposition}[Exact depth bridge]\label{xxb:dc:prop:bridge}
For an integer pair $(j,k)$ in this support window, set
$d=(N+j)/2-k$ and $M=N-k$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:bridge}
 c_N(j,k)=\frac{N!}{k!d!}\gamma(k,d,M).
\end{equation}
If $d\ge1$ and $j\le-1$, so that $q=-j-1\ge0$, this becomes the
integer identity
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:integer-bridge}
 c_N(j,k)=\binom Nk H_{d,k,q},\qquad
 N=2k+2d+1+q.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Taylor expansion gives
\[
 \sum_{N\ge0}\frac{\xi^N}{N!}x^{-x^2}\frac{d^N}{dx^N}x^{x^2}
 =\exp\bigl((2x\xi+\xi^2)\log x\bigr)
  \exp\bigl((x+\xi)^2\log(1+\xi/x)\bigr).
\]
Set $w=\xi/x$.  The contribution of logarithmic degree $k$ in the first
exponential and degree $d$ in the second is
\[
 \frac{x^{2k+2d}}{k!d!}\,w^k(2+w)^k\varphi(w)^d(\log x)^k.
\]
Extracting $\xi^N$ introduces $x^{-N}$, so $j=2k+2d-N$ and the remaining
coefficient index is $N-k=M$.  This proves \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:bridge}.
Substitution of \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:q} gives \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:integer-bridge}.
\end{proof}

Since $A(t)^kB(t)^d=t^k(2+t)^k\varphi(t)^d$, the bridge
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:bridge} is \eqref{xxb:eq:coefficient-AB} re-indexed, and the
proof above is essentially the Taylor-expansion proof of
Proposition~\ref{xxb:prop:egf}.

For completeness,
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:d0}
 \gamma(k,0,M)=
 \begin{cases}2^{k-M}\binom{k}{M},&0\le M\le k,\\0,&M>k,\end{cases}
\end{equation}
which is Proposition~\ref{xxb:prop:top}.  Also $\gamma(k,d,M)=0$ for $M<d$,
since $\varphi$ has order one at the origin.

\subsubsection{A parameter derivative}
Define
\[
 \mathcal A_{M,k}(s)=[w^M](2+w)^k(1+w)^s.
\]
This is a polynomial in $s$.  Differentiation with respect to $s$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:param}
 \gamma(k,d,M)=\left.\frac{\partial^d}{\partial s^d}\mathcal A_{M,k}(s)\right|_{s=2d}.
\end{equation}
Thus the question is about Taylor coefficients of explicit polynomials,
not the cancellation of numerical transcendental values.  (This is the
depth-coordinate form of the finite-difference formula
\eqref{xxb:eq:difference}.)

\begin{definition}[Centered polynomial]\label{xxb:dc:def:Q}
For an integer $r\ge0$, define $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}(y)$ by
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:Qgf}
 \sum_{k\ge0}\frac{\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}(y)}{k!}\xi^k
 =(1-\xi^2)^{-(r+1)/2}\exp\bigl(2y\atanh \xi\bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Centered factorization]\label{xxb:dc:prop:factor}
If $M\ge k$ and $r=M-k$, then
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:factor}
 M!\,\mathcal A_{M,k}(s)=\fall{s}{r}\,
 \mathcal Q_k^{(r)}\!\left(s-\frac{r-1}{2}\right).
\end{equation}
The polynomial $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$ has degree $k$, leading coefficient $2^k$,
nonnegative coefficients, and parity $k$.  It satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:Qrec}
 \mathcal Q_0^{(r)}=1,\quad \mathcal Q_1^{(r)}=2y,\quad
 \mathcal Q_{k+1}^{(r)}=2y\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}+k(k+r)\mathcal Q_{k-1}^{(r)}\quad(k\ge1).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Expanding $(2+w)^k$ and factoring the common falling factorial gives
\[
 M!\,\mathcal A_{M,k}(s)
 =\fall{s}{r}\sum_{\ell=0}^k\binom k\ell2^\ell
       \frac{(r+k)!}{(r+\ell)!}\fall{(s-r)}{\ell}.
\]
The sum divided by $k!$ is
\[
 [t^k](1+t)^{r+k}(1+2t)^{s-r}.
\]
Under the formal residue substitution $t=\xi/(1-\xi)$, this coefficient is
\[
 [\xi^k](1+\xi)^{s-r}(1-\xi)^{-s-1}.
\]
With $y=s-(r-1)/2$, the last generating function is exactly
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Qgf}.  This proves the factorization.
Both $(1-\xi^2)^{-(r+1)/2}$ and $\atanh \xi=\xi+\xi^3/3+\cdots$ have nonnegative
coefficients.  Expansion of the exponential proves coefficient
nonnegativity and parity, and its top $y$-degree term gives $2^ky^k$.
Finally, if $G$ is the generating function in \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Qgf}, then
\[
 (1-\xi^2)G'(\xi)=(2y+(r+1)\xi)G(\xi).
\]
Coefficient comparison gives \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Qrec}.
\end{proof}

In the standard normalization of the classical Meixner--Pollaczek
polynomials $P_k^{(\lambda)}(x;\phi)$ (classical notation, unrelated to the
$P_n$ of this part), the generating function in \cite[Eq.~18.23.7]{xxbdc-dlmf}
gives
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:MP}
 \mathcal Q_k^{(r)}(y)=k!\,i^kP_k^{((r+1)/2)}(-iy;\pi/2).
\end{equation}
All uses of this family in the proof follow directly from
Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:factor}, so no orthogonality theorem is an
unstated dependency.

\begin{corollary}[Mixed real and imaginary zeros]\label{xxb:dc:cor:imaginary}
The zeros of $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$ are simple and purely imaginary.  More
precisely, for positive numbers $\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}$,
\[
 \mathcal Q_k^{(r)}(y)=2^ky^{k\bmod2}
 \prod_{\ell=1}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}(y^2+\eta_\ell^2).
\]
Thus \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:factor} separates the consecutive real roots of the
falling factorial from a centered imaginary-root factor.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The monic polynomials $p_k(x)=2^{-k}i^{-k}\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}(ix)$ satisfy
\[
 p_{k+1}(x)=xp_k(x)-\frac{k(k+r)}4p_{k-1}(x).
\]
They are the characteristic polynomials of real symmetric tridiagonal
matrices with zero diagonal and positive off-diagonal entries
$\sqrt{\ell(\ell+r)}/2$.  The eigenvalues are real.  They are simple because an
eigenvector is determined by its first coordinate through the tridiagonal
recurrence, and a zero first coordinate would force the entire vector to
vanish.  The zero diagonal and alternating sign conjugation make the
spectrum symmetric about zero.  Transform back to $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Depth coordinates: bulk positivity and symmetry holes}\label{xxb:dc:sec:bulk}

Both results of this subsection were proved earlier in this part, by other
means: the bulk is Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:positive} and the holes are
Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:reflection-zeros}.  The manuscript's proofs are printed
as second routes.

\begin{proof}[Second proof of Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:positive} for $m\ge1$
(manuscript~06): proof of Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:bulk}]
First suppose $M\ge k$ and put $r=M-k$.  The stated range implies
$r\le2d+1$.  In \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:factor}, set $s=2d+u$.  Every factor of
\[
 \fall{(2d+u)}{r}=\prod_{\ell=0}^{r-1}(u+2d-\ell)
\]
has nonnegative constant term.  At most one has constant term zero.  If no
factor vanishes at $u=0$, all coefficients through degree $r$ are positive;
if one vanishes, all coefficients of degrees $1$ through $r$ are positive.
The empty product is one.

The argument of $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$ is $u+y_0$ with
$y_0=2d-(r-1)/2\ge d>0$.  Its coefficients through degree $k$ are all
positive: the leading term $2^k(u+y_0)^k$ already has that property, and
all remaining contributions are nonnegative.  Since $1\le d\le r+k=M$,
the coefficient of $u^d$ in the product is strictly positive.  Equation
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:param} proves the claim in this case.

To handle $M<k$, induct on $k$ for the entire claimed range, using
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:pascalgamma}
 \gamma(k,d,M)=2\gamma(k-1,d,M)+\gamma(k-1,d,M-1).
\end{equation}
The $k=0$ case has already been proved.  When $M<k$ and $M\ge d$, the first
term on the right is positive by induction.  The second is positive when
$M-1\ge d$ and is zero otherwise.  Both indices lie in the preceding bulk
range.  This completes the induction.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Positive derivative columns]\label{xxb:dc:cor:positivecols}
Within the structural support window, every coefficient with $j\ge0$ is
positive.  In the $j=-1$ column every coefficient with $d\ge1$ is positive,
while the $d=0$ coefficient is zero.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $d\ge1$, the condition $M\le k+2d+1$ is exactly $j\ge-1$ by
Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:bridge}.  For $d=0$, use \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:d0};
its nonzero condition $M\le k$ is exactly $j\ge0$.
\end{proof}

In the coordinates of this part, $j\ge0$ is $n\le2k$ and $j=-1$ is $n=2k+1$,
so the corollary restates Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:positive} together with
Proposition~\ref{xxb:prop:top}.

\begin{theorem}[All-depth symmetry holes; second route to Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:reflection-zeros}]\label{xxb:dc:thm:holes}
For $d\ge1$ and $k\ge0$,
\[
 k+d\equiv0\pmod2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \gamma(k,d,k+4d+1)=0.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Here $r=4d+1$ and the center $(r-1)/2$ is $2d$.  With $s=2d+u$,
\[
 \fall{s}{r}=u\prod_{\ell=1}^{2d}(u^2-\ell^2)
\]
is odd, whereas $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}(u)$ has parity $k$.  The product has parity
$k+1$.  Its $u^d$ coefficient therefore vanishes if $k+d$ is even.  Apply
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:param}.
\end{proof}

This proves the hole direction without relying on the analytic integral
below.  The polynomial proof also distinguishes the symmetry statement
from the separate assertion that there are no other zeros.  Translated,
$M=k+4d+1$ is $q=2d$, that is $n=2k+2m+1=4k-2j+1$ in this part's letters, and
$k+d$ even is ``$k$ even'' there; so the hole family is exactly the reflection
family of Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:reflection-zeros}, and the proof above is the
odd-falling-factorial parity argument that Section~\ref{syn:share} identifies
as common to all three parts.

\subsection{Depth coordinates: the branch-cut integral and its folding}\label{xxb:dc:sec:integral}

\subsubsection{An exact integral valid in the negative region}
Let
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:Rd}
 R_d(v)=\frac{\operatorname{Im}(v+i\pi)^d}{\pi}.
\end{equation}
It is a real polynomial of degree $d-1$ with leading coefficient $d$.

\begin{proposition}[Branch-cut formula]\label{xxb:dc:prop:integral}
For $d\ge1$, $k\ge0$, and $M=k+2d+1+q$ with integer $q\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:cut}
 \gamma(k,d,M)=(-1)^{M+1}
 \int_0^1 t^q(1-t)^{2d}(2t-1)^k
 R_d\!\left(\log\frac{1-t}{t}\right)dt.
\end{equation}
The integral is absolutely convergent.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Apply Cauchy's coefficient formula to
$(2+w)^k(1+w)^{2d}\log^d(1+w)$, using the principal logarithm and deforming
the circle to the cut $(-\infty,-1]$.  The small circle about $-1$ gives
zero in the limit because its contribution is bounded by a constant times
$\varepsilon^{2d+1}(1+|\log\varepsilon|^d)$.  The large circle vanishes
because $M>k+2d$, with bound $O(R^{k+2d-M}\log^d R)$.

At $w=-x$, $x>1$, the upper and lower logarithms are
$\log(x-1)\pm i\pi$.  Their jump divided by $2\pi i$ is
$R_d(\log(x-1))$.  The factor $w^{-M-1}$ contributes $(-1)^{M+1}$.
Consequently the coefficient is
\[
 (-1)^{M+1}\int_1^\infty
 \frac{(2-x)^k(x-1)^{2d}}{x^{M+1}}R_d(\log(x-1))\,dx.
\]
The substitution $t=1/x$ gives \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:cut}.  At $t=0$ and $t=1$ the
integrand is bounded by a constant times a nonnegative integral power
of the distance to the endpoint, multiplied by a fixed power of its
logarithm.  This is integrable.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Relation to Lemma~\ref{xxb:lem:beta}; editorial]\label{xxb:dc:rem:beta}
Formula \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:cut} is the $\varepsilon$-expansion of
Lemma~\ref{xxb:lem:beta} carried out for every deficit.  Indeed
$\frac{\sin(\pi\varepsilon)}{\pi}\bigl(\tfrac{t}{1-t}\bigr)^{\varepsilon}
=\operatorname{Im}\exp\bigl((\sigma+i\pi)\varepsilon\bigr)/\pi$ with
$\sigma=\log\frac{t}{1-t}$, whose coefficient of $\varepsilon^m$ is
$R_m(\sigma)/m!$.  Hence \eqref{xxb:eq:beta-c} reads
\[
 c_{n,k,j}=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}n!}{j!\,m!}\int_0^1t^{2m}(1-t)^{d}(2t-1)^j
 R_m\Bigl(\log\frac{t}{1-t}\Bigr)dt
\]
in the letters of this part, and the substitution $t\mapsto1-t$ together with
the dictionary of Table~\ref{xxb:dc:tab:notation} turns it into
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:integer-bridge} with \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:cut}.  For $m=1,2,3$ the
kernel $R_m/m!$ is $1$, $\sigma$ and
$\tfrac12\sigma^2-\tfrac{\pi^2}{6}$: the integrands
\eqref{xxb:eq:m1-integral}, \eqref{xxb:eq:m2-integral} and
\eqref{xxb:eq:m3-kernel}.  What is new below is the treatment of the sign change
that Section~\ref{xxb:sec:small-m} identified as the obstruction at $m=3$.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection{The residual polynomial}
For $d\ge1$, put
\[
 \nu_d=\left\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\right\rfloor,\qquad
 h_d(\tau)=\begin{cases}1,&d\text{ odd},\\ \tau,&d\text{ even},\end{cases}
\]
and define the monic polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:Pd}
 \Pi_d(\zeta)=\prod_{\ell=1}^{\nu_d}\left(\zeta-\pi^2\cot^2\frac{\ell\pi}{d}\right).
\end{equation}
The empty product is one.  Factoring the imaginary part in
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Rd} gives
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:Rfactor}
 R_d(-\tau)=(-1)^{d-1}d\,h_d(\tau)\Pi_d(\tau^2)\qquad(\tau>0).
\end{equation}
Indeed, the roots of $R_d$ are the distinct numbers
$\pi\cot(\ell\pi/d)$ for $1\le \ell\le d-1$; pairing opposite roots proves the
identity, including its leading coefficient.

The initial cases are
\begin{align}\label{xxb:dc:eq:Pinitial}
 \Pi_1(\zeta)&=\Pi_2(\zeta)=1,\notag\\
 \Pi_3(\zeta)&=\zeta-\pi^2/3,\qquad \Pi_4(\zeta)=\zeta-\pi^2,\\
 \Pi_5(\zeta)&=\zeta^2-2\pi^2\zeta+\pi^4/5,\notag\\
 \Pi_6(\zeta)&=\zeta^2-\tfrac{10}{3}\pi^2\zeta+\pi^4.\notag
\end{align}
Thus the first two depths have no residual sign change.  At depths three
and four the residual polynomial is increasing.  At higher depths that
last property fails and cannot be silently reused.

\subsubsection{Positive measures on the half interval}
For $1/2<t<1$, abbreviate
\[
 y=1-t,\quad s=2t-1,\quad \tau=\log(t/y),\quad p=2d,\quad
 \delta=q-p,\quad b_d(t)=(ty)^p h_d(\tau).
\]
Reflect the part $0<t<1/2$ in \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:cut}.  Apart from the sign and
positive factor $(-1)^{d-1}d$, the result is
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:fold}
 \int_{1/2}^1 b_d(t)s^k\Pi_d(\tau^2)
 \bigl(t^\delta+(-1)^{k+d-1}y^\delta\bigr)dt.
\end{equation}
When $k+d$ is odd, the bracket is the positive sum
$S_\delta(t)=t^\delta+y^\delta$.  When $k+d$ is even, it is the difference
$D_\delta(t)=t^\delta-y^\delta$; it is identically zero for $\delta=0$ and
otherwise has constant sign $\sgn\delta$.

For a sum case, define a probability measure by normalizing
$b_ds^kS_\delta\,dt$.  For a nonzero difference case, normalize
$b_ds^k|D_\delta|\,dt$.  Write $\mathcal E_{d,k,q}$ for expectation with
respect to the relevant measure.  The following sign rule is exact:
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:sign-rule}
 \sgn H_{d,k,q}=
 \begin{cases}
 (-1)^q\sgn\mathcal E_{d,k,q}[\Pi_d(\tau^2)],&k+d\text{ odd},\\
 (-1)^{q+1}\sgn(q-2d)\sgn\mathcal E_{d,k,q}[\Pi_d(\tau^2)],
      &k+d\text{ even},\ q\ne2d.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
At a symmetry hole $H_{d,k,2d}=0$ by Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:holes}.
All the measures above have finite logarithmic moments of every fixed
order.

\subsection{Depth coordinates: monotone comparisons}\label{xxb:dc:sec:monotone}

\begin{lemma}[Covariance comparison]\label{xxb:dc:lem:covariance}
Let $\mu$ be a probability measure on an interval, and let $f,g$ be
nondecreasing functions with the needed integrability, $g>0$.  Then
\[
 \frac{\int fg\,d\mu}{\int g\,d\mu}\ge\int f\,d\mu.
\]
In particular, multiplying a positive density by a nondecreasing function
can only increase expectations of nondecreasing functions.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The covariance equals
\[
 \frac12\iint(f(u)-f(v))(g(u)-g(v))\,d\mu(u)\,d\mu(v)\ge0.
\]
Truncation proves the same conclusion for integrable unbounded functions.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Radial likelihood ratios]\label{xxb:dc:lem:ratios}
On $(1/2,1)$ the following assertions hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item For every integer $\delta$, $S_\delta$ is nondecreasing.  The cases
$\delta=0,1$ are constant.
\item For every nonzero integer $\delta$, $|D_\delta|/s$ is positive and
nondecreasing.  The positive cases $\delta=1,2$ are constant.
\item If $\delta\ge1$ is an integer, both
$S_{\delta+1}/S_\delta$ and $D_{\delta+1}/D_\delta$ are nondecreasing.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $v=\frac12\log(t/y)>0$, so
$t=e^v/(2\cosh v)$ and $y=e^{-v}/(2\cosh v)$.  Direct calculation gives
\begin{align}\label{xxb:dc:eq:ratios}
 \frac{S_{\delta+1}}{S_\delta}
 &=\frac12\bigl(1+\tanh v\tanh(\delta v)\bigr),\notag\\
 \frac{D_{\delta+1}}{D_\delta}
 &=\frac12\bigl(1+\tanh v\coth(\delta v)\bigr).
\end{align}
The first expression is nondecreasing for $\delta\ge0$.  For $\delta\ge1$,
\[
 \frac{d}{dv}\log\bigl(\tanh v\coth(\delta v)\bigr)
 =\frac{2}{\sinh(2v)}-\frac{2\delta}{\sinh(2\delta v)}\ge0,
\]
using $\sinh(\delta x)\ge\delta\sinh x$ for $\delta\ge1$, $x>0$.
This proves (iii).  Products of these ratios, starting at $S_1=1$ and
$D_1=s$, prove (i) and (ii) for positive $\delta$.  For a negative integer
$\delta=-a$, use
\[
 S_{-a}=(ty)^{-a}S_a,\qquad |D_{-a}|=(ty)^{-a}D_a.
\]
The extra factor is nondecreasing because $ty$ decreases on the half
interval.  The stated constant cases are immediate.
\end{proof}

For real $r\ge0$, let $\mu_{d,r}$ denote the probability measure obtained
by normalizing $b_d(t)s^r\,dt$, and set
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:base-moment}
 \mathcal B_d(r)=\mathbb E_{\mu_{d,r}}[\tau^2].
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Uniform comparison with a symmetric base]\label{xxb:dc:prop:base}
For every integer $q\ge0$ outside a symmetry hole,
\begin{align}\label{xxb:dc:eq:basecmp}
 \mathcal E_{d,k,q}[\tau^2]&\ge \mathcal B_d(k),&&k+d\text{ odd},\notag\\
 \mathcal E_{d,k,q}[\tau^2]&\ge \mathcal B_d(k+1),&&k+d\text{ even}.
\end{align}
The function $\mathcal B_d(r)$ is nondecreasing in $r$.  Within either parity
class of $k$, the expectations of every increasing function of $\tau^2$
increase when $k$ increases by two.  They also increase when $q$ increases by
one in the region $q\ge2d+1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For a sum measure, its ratio to the base density with exponent $k$ is
$S_\delta$, which is nondecreasing by Lemma~\ref{xxb:dc:lem:ratios}.  For a
difference measure, its ratio to the base with exponent $k+1$ is
$|D_\delta|/s$, again nondecreasing.  Apply Lemma~\ref{xxb:dc:lem:covariance}.
Increasing $r$ multiplies a base density by a positive power of $s$.
Increasing $k$ by two multiplies either type of density by $s^2$.
The assertion about $q$ follows from the adjacent ratios in
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:ratios}, with $\delta\ge1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The role of integer parameters]\label{xxb:dc:rem:integer}
The global base comparison uses integer $\delta$.  For noninteger
$0<\delta<1$, the sum $S_\delta$ has different monotonicity.  No conclusion
about that interval is being smuggled into the integer coefficient
problem.  The real-zero theorem in Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:zero-count} uses
a separate argument and does not depend on the integer comparison.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Depth coordinates: an explicit finite reduction at every depth}\label{xxb:dc:sec:effective}

This subsection proves Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:finite} with deliberately crude
but fully explicit bounds.  Their purpose is decidability of a fixed-depth
stratum, not efficient computation.  The next subsection replaces them by
small bounds at depths three and four.

\subsubsection{A monotone lower bound for every residual polynomial}
For $d\ge3$, put
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:constants}
 \begin{split}
 \nu_d&=\lfloor(d-1)/2\rfloor,\qquad
 C_d=[3(d^2+1)]^{\nu_d}+d^2+1,\\
 A_d&=4C_d+2,\qquad E_d=(2d+1)A_d+6d+2,\\
 K_d&=2^{A_d}(E_d+A_d+2),\\
 Q_d&=2d+2^{A_d+1}(E_d+A_d+6).
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
These are positive integers constructed by elementary integer operations
and exponentiation.

\begin{lemma}[Affine minorant]\label{xxb:dc:lem:minorant}
For all $\zeta\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:minorant}
 \Pi_d(\zeta)\ge\tfrac12\zeta-C_d.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every root $\beta$ of $\Pi_d$ satisfies $0<\beta<d^2$, because
$\pi\cot(\pi/d)<d$.  For $\zeta\ge2(d^2+1)$, each factor $\zeta-\beta$ is at
least $\zeta/2$, and $\nu_d\ge1$ implies $\Pi_d(\zeta)\ge(\zeta/2)^{\nu_d}\ge\zeta/2$.
For $0\le\zeta\le2(d^2+1)$, the absolute value of the product is at most
$[3(d^2+1)]^{\nu_d}$.  Meanwhile $\zeta/2-C_d$ is at most the negative of that
quantity.  This proves the inequality on both ranges.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Explicit concentration of the base measure}
Write $A=A_d$, $E=E_d$, and choose rational points
\[
 t_0=1-2^{-A},\quad t_1=1-2^{-A-1},\quad t_2=1-2^{-A-2}.
\]
On $t\ge t_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:largeL}
 \tau^2\ge\log^2(2^A-1)>\frac{(A-1)^2}{4}>4C_d,
\end{equation}
where $\log2>1/2$ and $A=4C_d+2$ were used.
Below $t_0$, the base factor $b_d$ has integral at most $A$: use
$(t(1-t))^{2d}\le1$ and, when $d$ is even, $\tau<A$.
On $[t_1,t_2]$, the interval length is $2^{-A-2}$,
$t(1-t)\ge2^{-A-3}$, and $h_d(\tau)\ge1$.  Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:base-mass}
 \int_{t_1}^{t_2}b_d(t)\,dt\ge2^{-E}.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Large base exponent]\label{xxb:dc:lem:largek}
If $r\ge K_d$, then $\mathcal B_d(r)>2C_d$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $s_0=2t_0-1=1-2^{1-A}$ and
$s_1=2t_1-1=1-2^{-A}$.  The ratio of the unnormalized mass below $t_0$ to
the mass on $[t_1,t_2]$ is at most
\[
 A2^E(s_0/s_1)^r\le A2^E\exp(-2^{-A}r)<1.
\]
For the last inequality, substitute $r\ge2^A(E+A+2)$ and use
$\log A<A$, $\log2<1$.  Thus the normalized mass above $t_0$ exceeds
one half.  Equation~\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:largeL} gives $\mathcal B_d(r)>2C_d$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Explicit concentration in the tail parameter}
\begin{lemma}[Large coefficient tail]\label{xxb:dc:lem:largeq}
If $q\ge Q_d$, then in both parity types and for every $k\ge0$,
\[
 \mathcal E_{d,k,q}[\tau^2]>2C_d.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Within each parity type it suffices, by
Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:base}, to use its smallest possible $k$, either
zero or one.  Put $\delta=q-2d\ge1$.  On the part below $t_0$, the bracket
$S_\delta$ or $D_\delta$ is at most $2t_0^\delta$, so the unnormalized
mass is at most $2At_0^\delta$.
On $[t_1,t_2]$, the factor $s^k$ is at least $1/2$.  Both brackets are at
least $t_1^\delta/2$: for the difference use
$D_\delta=t^\delta(1-(y/t)^\delta)$ and $y/t<1/2$ there.
The interval mass is consequently at least $2^{-E}t_1^\delta/4$.
The ratio of the bad mass to this interval mass is at most
\[
 8A2^E(t_0/t_1)^\delta
 \le8A2^E\exp(-2^{-A-1}\delta)<1,
\]
by $\delta\ge2^{A+1}(E+A+6)$.  Once again, more than half of the
probability lies above $t_0$, and \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:largeL} finishes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:finite}]
If $k\ge K_d$, the base comparison and Lemma~\ref{xxb:dc:lem:largek} imply
$\mathcal E_{d,k,q}[\tau^2]>2C_d$ for every nonhole $q\ge0$.  If $q\ge Q_d$,
Lemma~\ref{xxb:dc:lem:largeq} gives the same conclusion for every $k$.
In either case, Lemma~\ref{xxb:dc:lem:minorant} gives
\[
 \mathcal E_{d,k,q}[\Pi_d(\tau^2)]
 \ge\tfrac12\mathcal E_{d,k,q}[\tau^2]-C_d>0.
\]
The sign rule \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:sign-rule} proves nonvanishing outside the
stated symmetry holes.  The holes themselves were proved independently.
Combined with bulk positivity and the structural zeros, this confines every
possible extra zero to $0\le k<K_d$, $0\le q<Q_d$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A terminating fixed-depth decision algorithm]\label{xxb:dc:cor:decision}
Given $d\ge1$, there is an algorithm that decides whether
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:classification} holds for every pair of integer parameters
$(k,M)$ at that depth.  For $d\ge3$, it computes the finite rectangle
specified by \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:constants} using integer polynomial recurrences.
If the answer is negative, it returns an exact counterexample.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}[The bound depends on the depth]\label{xxb:dc:rem:depthdependent}
The bound is uniform in both $k$ and $q$, but depends on $d$.  It is not a
finite verification of the full conjecture over all depths.  Nor do the
enormous universal constants justify a practical exhaustive computation.
Their value is the proved reduction; efficient certificates need sharper
analysis, as the next subsection demonstrates.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Depth coordinates: completing depths one through four}\label{xxb:dc:sec:four}

\subsubsection{The first two depths}
For $d=1,2$, the residual polynomial $\Pi_d$ is identically one.  Every
positive-weight expectation in \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:sign-rule} is strictly
positive.  Together with bulk positivity and the symmetry theorem, this proves
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:classification} at the first two depths.  In particular, no
higher Peano-kernel argument is needed for depth two.  (This is a second route
to the zero classification of Theorems~\ref{xxb:thm:m1} and~\ref{xxb:thm:m2},
which prove more: the complete sign pattern.  The manuscript's folding
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:fold} is the pairing $t\leftrightarrow1-t$ used in their
proofs.)

\subsubsection{A small-certificate criterion for depths three and four}
At these depths, $\Pi_d(\tau^2)$ is an increasing affine function of $\tau^2$.
The comparisons in Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:base} apply to its expectation
itself, not merely to a lower bound.

\begin{proposition}[Three signs and a finite rectangle]\label{xxb:dc:prop:smallcert}
Let $d\in\{3,4\}$.  Choose $R\ge2$ of parity $d-1$ and an integer
$Q\ge2d+1$.  Put $k_s=(d-1)\bmod2$ and $k_a=d\bmod2$.
Suppose
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:seeds}
 H_{d,R,2d}>0,\qquad
 (-1)^QH_{d,k_s,Q}>0,\qquad
 (-1)^{Q+1}H_{d,k_a,Q}>0.
\end{equation}
Then every nonhole entry with $k\ge R-1$ or $q\ge Q$ is nonzero.
Consequently the full depth is settled by testing
$0\le k\le R-2$, $0\le q\le Q-1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Since $R+d$ is odd and $2d$ is even, the first sign in
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:seeds} says exactly that the base expectation
$\mathbb E_{\mu_{d,R}}\Pi_d(\tau^2)$ is positive.  In a sum case with
$k\ge R-1$, parity forces $k\ge R$.  In a difference case the comparison base
has exponent $k+1\ge R$.  Thus all nonhole entries in this $k$-range have
positive reduced expectation.

The remaining two signs are exactly positive reduced expectations for
the smallest sum and difference parity classes at $q=Q$; here
$Q>2d$.  Increasing $k$ by two and increasing $q$ by one preserves that
positivity by Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:base}.  This covers every $q\ge Q$.
\end{proof}

For $d=3$, take $(R,Q)=(14,38)$.  For $d=4$, take $(R,Q)=(85,185)$.
The six seed signs and the two rectangles are verified in the next
subsection.  This proves Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four}.

\subsubsection{What the result says about individual coefficients}
At depth three the only holes have
\[
 q=6,\quad k\text{ odd};\qquad
 j=-7,\quad N=2k+13.
\]
At depth four they have
\[
 q=8,\quad k\text{ even};\qquad
 j=-9,\quad N=2k+17.
\]
(In this part's letters: $m=3$, $j$ odd, $n=2j+13$; and $m=4$, $j$ even,
$n=2j+17$ --- in both cases $k=j+m$ even and $n=4k-2j+1$.)
All other entries at those depths are nonzero, regardless of derivative
order.  Above the practical $k$ or $q$ thresholds their signs are the
explicit signs in \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:sign-rule} with the reduced expectation
replaced by a positive number.

Nonzero does not mean positive.  For example, in the $d=3,k=0$ row the
reduced expectation is positive at $q=0$, negative for $1\le q\le37$,
and positive for all $q\ge38$.  These sign changes explain why a naive
one-sign induction can fail without producing an integer zero.  The exact
finite sign data are shipped as
\texttt{data/02-depth-\allowbreak certificates-\allowbreak sign\_thresholds.csv}.
\emph{Update (30 September 2026):} the sign of the $d=3$, $k=0$ row is that
of the harmonic quantity $\mathsf E_q$, so this observation is now proved
(Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:E-sign}); the second route of
Subsection~\ref{xxb:d3:sec:tail} reproduces every $d=3$ row of that file
(Remark~\ref{xxb:d3:rem:compare}).

\subsection{Depth coordinates: the integer certificates and independent checks}\label{xxb:dc:sec:certificates}

\subsubsection{First recurrence: fixed tail column}
Set $r=2d+1+q$ and work in $\Z[u]/(u^{d+1})$.  From
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:factor},
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:Hjet}
 H_{d,k,q}=[u^d]\fall{(2d+u)}{r}\,
 \mathcal Q_k^{(r)}\!\left(u+\frac{2d-q}{2}\right).
\end{equation}
Although the displayed center may be half-integral, the recurrence only
uses the integer linear polynomial $2u+2d-q$.  If $V_k(u)$ denotes the
product on the right, truncated after degree $d$, then
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:Vrec}
 \begin{split}
 V_0(u)&=\fall{(2d+u)}{r}\pmod{u^{d+1}},\\
 V_1(u)&=(2u+2d-q)V_0(u)\pmod{u^{d+1}},\\
 V_{k+1}(u)&=(2u+2d-q)V_k(u)+k(k+r)V_{k-1}(u)
                 \pmod{u^{d+1}}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
The required integer is the coefficient of $u^d$ in $V_k$.
Multiplication by each linear factor of $V_0$ and every recurrence step
are implemented directly on lists of integers.  Induction proves that
truncation cannot affect any coefficient being tested.

\subsubsection{Second recurrence: Pascal propagation}
Equation~\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:pascalgamma} gives the independent array recurrence
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:Hpascal}
 H_{d,k+1,q}=2H_{d,k,q+1}+(k+2d+2+q)H_{d,k,q},
\end{equation}
with
\[
 H_{d,0,q}=[u^d]\fall{(2d+u)}{2d+1+q}.
\]
To compute $0\le k\le K$, $0\le q\le Q$, initialize the $k=0$ row
through $q=Q+K$ and shorten it at each step.  This recurrence comes from
multiplying by $(2+w)$, rather than from the centered-polynomial
three-term recurrence.  Every entry of both finite rectangles was
recomputed by both methods and compared as an exact integer.

\subsubsection{Finite obligations and results}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{crrrrr}
\toprule
Depth & $k$ maximum & $q$ maximum & Cells & Symmetry zeros & Nonzero\\
\midrule
3 & 12 & 37 & 494 & 6 & 488\\
4 & 83 & 184 & 15,540 & 42 & 15,498\\
\midrule
Total &&&16,034&48&15,986\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
No additional zero occurs in either rectangle.  Every nonzero integer
also has a nonzero residue modulo at least one of $1009$ and $1013$.
The first modulus suffices for every nonzero depth-three entry; nineteen
depth-four entries require the second modulus.  Primality is not needed
for this particular witness rule: a nonzero residue modulo any integer
greater than one already proves the original integer is nonzero.

The necessary seed signs are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{crrrl}
\toprule
$d$ & $k$ & $q$ & $\sgn H_{d,k,q}$ & Role\\
\midrule
3&14&6&$+$&central base\\
3&0&38&$+$&sum tail\\
3&1&38&$-$&difference tail\\
4&85&8&$+$&central base\\
4&1&185&$-$&sum tail\\
4&0&185&$+$&difference tail\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For example,
\[
 H_{3,14,6}=9707604961443840000>0,
 \qquad H_{3,12,6}=-14670288691200000<0.
\]
The exact larger seed integers, rather than floating-point
approximations, are stored in
\texttt{data/02-depth-\allowbreak certificates-\allowbreak verification.json}.
The preceding central values at $k=12$ and $k=83$ are negative, so the
chosen central exponents are the first positive ones in their relevant
parity classes.  This observation is not needed for validity of the
certificates.

\begin{remark}[The rectangles lie inside the range certified in this part; editorial]\label{xxb:dc:rem:inside}
By $N=2k+2d+1+q$, the depth-three rectangle reaches derivative order
$N\le2\cdot12+7+37=68$ and the depth-four rectangle $N\le2\cdot83+9+184=359$;
the six seeds sit at $N=41,45,47$ ($d=3$) and $N=187,194,196$ ($d=4$).  All of these lie inside the range $n\le3000$ of
Proposition~\ref{xxb:prop:finite}, whose modular certificate already shows
that every rectangle cell outside the reflection family is nonzero.  The
nonvanishing half of the manuscript's finite computation is therefore a second,
independent confirmation of cells this part had already certified; what
Proposition~\ref{xxb:prop:finite} does not supply is the six seed
\emph{signs} \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:seeds}, which the reduction needs and which the
manuscript computes.  The contribution of the addition is the proved reduction
(Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:smallcert}) that makes a finite set of cells
decide every $n$, not the finite range itself.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection{Additional cross-check against differentiation}
The shipped program independently generates the original lattice using
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:rec-lattice} through $N=80$.  It checks:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Exact test & Number of cells\\
\midrule
Positive bulk cells & 69,701\\
Identity $c_N(j,k)=\binom NkH_{d,k,-j-1}$ & 21,320\\
First-four-depth zero classification in that lattice & 5,780\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
These are consistency checks on implementation and index translation.
They are not the justification for the infinite theorem; that
justification is Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:smallcert} together with the
finite obligations just listed.

\subsubsection{Reproduction and trust boundary}
From the report directory, on a copy (see below), run
\begin{lstlisting}
python3 code/02-depth-certificates-minimal_verify.py
python3 code/02-depth-certificates-verify.py
\end{lstlisting}
The first prints \texttt{PASS}; the second prints a JSON report with
\texttt{"status": "PASS"} and writes nothing.  The manuscript's regeneration
command, \texttt{verify.py --write-data}, writes the unprefixed files
\texttt{finite\_certificate.csv}, \texttt{sign\_thresholds.csv} and
\texttt{verification.json} into \texttt{data/} beside the shipped prefixed
copies, so it should be run only on a copy of the report directory.
The code uses only Python's standard library and arbitrary-precision
integers.  It uses no floating point, symbolic algebra library, random
search, external service, or precomputed presumed support formula.
Failures raise explicit exceptions, even if Python assertions are
disabled.  The CSV file supplies the sign and both modular residues for
every rectangle cell.

The finite checks were run for the manuscript and returned \texttt{PASS}; they
were rerun on a copy during the intake and passed again, with byte-identical
CSV files.  Ordinary program execution is still not a proof-assistant kernel
check.  A formal version would need the polynomial-model bridge, correctness
of one recurrence, and a proved modular checker; the analytic lemmas would
also need formalization.  No such formalization is claimed.  The two exact
recurrences reduce the risk of implementation or indexing error, but do not
replace independent mathematical review.

\subsection{Depth coordinates: an all-depth bound on the number of zeros}\label{xxb:dc:sec:zero-count}

The branch-cut formula also controls how often cancellation can occur,
without any fixed-depth classification assumption.
For fixed $d\ge1$ and $k\ge0$, define for real $q>-1$
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:Mellin}
 \mathcal F_{d,k}(q)=\int_0^1 t^q(1-t)^{2d}(2t-1)^k
 R_d\!\left(\log\frac{1-t}{t}\right)dt.
\end{equation}
For integer $q\ge0$, its zeros are precisely those of $H_{d,k,q}$.

\begin{lemma}[Mellin zero bound from sign changes]\label{xxb:dc:lem:mellin}
Let $W$ be a nonzero continuous real weight on $(0,1)$ with finitely
many zeros and exactly $J$ sign changes.  Assume that $t^qW(t)$ is
integrable for every $q>-1$.  Then
$F(q)=\int_0^1t^qW(t)\,dt$ has at most $J$ distinct real zeros in
$(-1,\infty)$.  Here a sign change refers to a crossing of sign, not an
even-multiplicity zero of the weight.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $q_0<\cdots<q_J$ are zeros.  There is a nonzero linear combination
$\psi(t)=\sum_{i=0}^Ja_it^{q_i}$ vanishing at the $J$ sign-change points of
$W$: solve the corresponding homogeneous system of $J$ linear equations.
A nonzero linear combination of $J+1$ distinct real powers has at most
$J$ positive zeros, counting multiplicities.  This follows inductively by
dividing by the smallest power, differentiating, and applying Rolle's
theorem.  It also establishes linear independence of the powers.

Hence the prescribed zeros of $\psi$ are simple and there are no others.
After changing the overall sign, $\psi W$ is nonnegative throughout the
interval and positive on a set of positive measure.  Therefore
\[
 0<\int_0^1\psi(t)W(t)\,dt=\sum_{i=0}^Ja_iF(q_i)=0,
\]
a contradiction.  Continuity and the finite zero set ensure strict
positivity of $\psi W$ on an interval, as needed above.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[At most $d$ cancellations in a fixed row]\label{xxb:dc:thm:zero-count}
The number of distinct real zeros of $\mathcal F_{d,k}$ on $(-1,\infty)$ is at
most
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:J}
 J(d,k)=
 \begin{cases}
 d-1,&k\text{ even},\\
 d,&k\text{ odd and }d\text{ odd},\\
 d-2,&k\text{ odd and }d\text{ even}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
In particular, for fixed $k,d$ there are at most $d$ integer zeros in the
entire negative-region tail.  If a symmetry zero is present, it consumes
one of these permitted zeros.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The polynomial $R_d$ has $d-1$ distinct real roots.  The logarithmic
argument in \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Mellin} is strictly decreasing from $+\infty$ to
$-\infty$, so these initially give $d-1$ sign changes.
If $k$ is even, $(2t-1)^k$ adds no sign change.  If $k$ is odd and $d$ is
odd, $R_d(0)\ne0$, and the factor adds one sign change at $t=1/2$.
If $k$ is odd and $d$ is even, $R_d$ has a simple zero at zero; its sign
change at $t=1/2$ is canceled by the odd power of $2t-1$, leaving $d-2$.
The remaining factors are positive.  Apply Lemma~\ref{xxb:dc:lem:mellin}.
\end{proof}

The theorem is a zero-count statement, not an assertion that all zeros
are simple or that the upper bound is always attained.  It also does not
rule out an integer zero by locating nearby real zeros; the finite
certificates are what supply that additional information at depths three
and four.  In this part's letters, it bounds by $J(m,j)$ the number of $n$ at
which a fixed $(j,m)$ diagonal can vanish with $n\ge2k+1$.

\subsection{Depth coordinates: consequences for the term count}\label{xxb:dc:sec:counts}

\subsubsection{An unconditional upper bound (second route to Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:upper})}
The triangular support window and \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:d0} imply that, in negative
columns, the top $d=0$ term is absent.  Removing those structural zeros,
the number of remaining cells is
$2\rho^2+2\rho+1$ when $N=2\rho$ and $2\rho^2$ when $N=2\rho-1$.
For even $N$ no symmetry hole is possible.  For odd $N=2\rho-1$, a hole has
\[
 k=\rho-2d-1\ge0,\qquad d\equiv \rho-1\pmod2.
\]
Counting these depths gives $\lfloor \rho/4\rfloor$ holes (or check the four
residue classes of $\rho$).  Thus, independently of unknown nonvanishing,
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:upper}
 a(2\rho)\le2\rho^2+2\rho+1,\qquad
 a(2\rho-1)\le2\rho^2-\lfloor \rho/4\rfloor.
\end{equation}
The conjecture is exactly equality in these bounds.  This is
Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:upper} and its equality clause, with the hole count
$\lfloor\rho/4\rfloor=\lfloor(n+1)/8\rfloor$ of
Lemma~\ref{xxb:lem:reflection-count} counted by depth instead of by $k$.

\subsubsection{A certified lower bound from the proved regions}
Let $\beta(N)$ count the proved positive bulk cells, including $d=0$
where it is nonzero.  Direct summation gives
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:B}
 \beta(2\rho)=\frac{(\rho+1)(3\rho+2)}2,\qquad
 \beta(2\rho-1)=\frac{3\rho^2+3\rho-2}2\quad(\rho\ge1).
\end{equation}
These are exactly the two cases of $\Lambda(N)$ in \eqref{xxb:eq:bounds}: the
bulk is the region of Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:positive}, and
Corollary~\ref{xxb:cor:lower} counts the same cells.
For a fixed depth $1\le d\le4$, the cells outside this bulk have $q\ge1$.
The identity $N=2k+2d+1+q$ shows that their number before holes is
$\max(0,\lfloor N/2\rfloor-d)$.  Define
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:hd}
 \chi_d(N)=\mathbf 1\{N\ge4d+1\text{ and }N\equiv2d+1\pmod4\}.
\end{equation}
This is exactly the number, zero or one, of symmetry holes at that depth
and derivative order.

\begin{corollary}[Explicit unconditional lower bound]\label{xxb:dc:cor:lower}
For all $N\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:dc:eq:lower}
 a(N)\ge \beta(N)+\sum_{d=1}^4\max(0,\lfloor N/2\rfloor-d)
                  -\sum_{d=1}^4\chi_d(N).
\end{equation}
In particular, the lower bound has leading term $3N^2/8$, whereas the
conjectured upper bound has leading term $N^2/2$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Count the positive bulk and the disjoint first-four-depth cells with
$q\ge1$.  By Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four}, only the indicated holes must be
removed.  The counts give \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:lower}.
\end{proof}

For example, for even $N=2\rho$ with $\rho\ge4$, the lower bound simplifies to
$(3\rho^2+13\rho-18)/2$.  It agrees with the conjectured value at $N=8,10$ and is
84 at $N=12$, compared with the conjectured upper value 85.  The unresolved
cell there has depth five.  These examples illustrate the precise
boundary of the argument; they are not evidence that depth five fails.
No asymptotic equivalence $a(N)\sim N^2/2$ is deduced here.

\begin{remark}[How much this improves $\Lambda$; editorial]\label{xxb:dc:rem:improve}
Since $\beta=\Lambda$, the gain of \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:lower} over
Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:main-bounds} is the sum of the last two terms.  For even
$N\ge8$ no hole occurs and every maximum is attained by
$N/2-d\ge0$; for odd $N\ge17$ each maximum is $(N-1)/2-d>0$ and exactly two
indicators equal one (at $d=1,3$ if $N\equiv3$, at $d=2,4$ if $N\equiv1\pmod4$).
Hence
\[
 a(N)\ge
 \begin{cases}
 \Lambda(N)+2N-10=\dfrac{3N^2+26N-72}{8},&N\text{ even},\ N\ge8,\\[8pt]
 \Lambda(N)+2N-14=\dfrac{3N^2+28N-111}{8},&N\text{ odd},\ N\ge17.
 \end{cases}
\]
The leading constant $3/8$ is unchanged; the linear term improves.  Below these thresholds the gain is read off
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:lower} directly; the bound equals $\U(N)$ for every $N\le11$.  Of the even gain
$2N-10$, the part $N-3$ from depths one and two already followed from
Theorems~\ref{xxb:thm:m1}--\ref{xxb:thm:m2}, although Part~\ref{xxb:part} did
not add it to $\Lambda$; the part $N-7$ from depths three and four is new.  At
odd $N\ge17$ the split is $(N-5)+(N-9)$.  The two closed forms were checked
against a direct count of the proved cells for every $N<400$.  $\Lambda(n)$
remains the lower bound stated in Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:main-bounds}; the
certified range $n\le3000$ of Proposition~\ref{xxb:prop:finite} makes both
bounds irrelevant there, since $a(n)=\U(n)$ is known for those $n$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Depth coordinates: further research questions}\label{xxb:dc:sec:future}

\subsubsection{Depths five and six: a stronger moment criterion}
The explicit quadratics in \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Pinitial} suggest a practical next
step.  Since $\mathbb E[Z^2]\ge(\mathbb E Z)^2$, where $Z=\tau^2$, sufficient
conditions for positive reduced expectation are
\begin{align}\label{xxb:dc:eq:nextcriteria}
 d=5:&\qquad \mathbb E Z>\pi^2(1+2/\sqrt5),\notag\\
 d=6:&\qquad \mathbb E Z>3\pi^2.
\end{align}
These follow by applying the variance inequality to the two quadratic
polynomials and placing the mean above their largest root.  Unlike the
crude affine minorant, these criteria retain the exact quadratic
structure.  Their usefulness is that $\mathbb E Z$ is monotone under all the
proved likelihood-ratio comparisons even though $\Pi_5$ and $\Pi_6$ are not
monotone on the entire positive axis.

\textbf{Question 1.}  Determine certified practical $k$ and $q$ cutoffs
from \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:nextcriteria}, and settle the resulting finite rectangles
at depths five and six.  A successful computation must be accompanied by
the mean-moment bounds; an unbounded inference from a numerical scan
would not suffice.  (Editorial: the range $n\le3000$ of
Proposition~\ref{xxb:prop:finite} already certifies nonvanishing of every
depth-five and depth-six cell with $n\le3000$; what is missing is the
reduction, and the seed signs it will require.)

\subsubsection{Sharpening the fixed-depth reduction}
\textbf{Question 2.}  How small can uniform fixed-depth cutoffs be made as
functions of $d$?  The constants in \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:constants} are
intentionally wasteful.  The largest residual root is $\pi^2\cot^2(\pi/d)$, of
order $d^2$, so endpoint concentration on a logarithmic scale is a natural
quantity to analyze.  This observation does not yet prove an optimal
cutoff growth rate.

\textbf{Question 3.}  Can lower bounds involving the first few moments of
$\tau^2$, or positivity in an orthogonal-polynomial basis adapted to
$\mu_{d,r}$, replace the affine minorant for all depths?  The target is a
small finite certificate rather than an enormous merely terminating
algorithm.
(\emph{Update, 30 September 2026:} at $d=3$ the second route of
Subsections~\ref{xxb:d3:sec:harmonic}--\ref{xxb:d3:sec:tail} gives an
explained cutoff, the exact sign change of $\mathsf E_q$ between $q=37$ and
$q=38$, and a different small certificate; it bears on Questions~2 and~3
without answering them for general $d$.)

\subsubsection{The remaining full conjecture}
\textbf{Question 4.}  Prove that every fixed-depth rectangle has only the
symmetry zeros, uniformly in depth, or exhibit the first exact
counterexample.  The present results show where additional zeros must be
searched for at each depth, but they do not supply an induction in $d$.
The polynomial $\Pi_d$ acquires additional oscillations, which must be
addressed rather than ignored.  (This is Conjecture~\ref{xxb:conj:support}
restricted to $m\ge5$.)

\textbf{Question 5.}  Can the real-zero bound \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:J} be combined
with arithmetic separation to exclude integer zeros uniformly?  The Mellin
argument limits the number of candidates in each row, but integer
nonvanishing needs a second ingredient.  An effective distance-from-integers
theorem for its zeros would be particularly valuable.

\textbf{Question 6.}  Establish the conjectured leading term
$a(N)\sim N^2/2$ without first proving every individual nonvanishing
claim.  This would require bounding the total number of extra zeros
across growing depths.  The fixed-row bound alone does not give such a
bound after summing over the whole triangular array.

\subsubsection{Formal proof and symbolic computation}
\textbf{Question 7.}  Formalize the polynomial factorization, bulk theorem,
and integer certificate in the existing A290268 Lean development.
The algebraic route has a small interface: coefficient extraction,
falling factorials, the recurrence \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Qrec}, and a finite modular
nonzero checker.  The branch-cut and likelihood-ratio proofs are a
separate analytic layer.  Keeping these layers explicit would avoid
confusing a verified finite table with a verified infinite theorem.
(Section~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:formal} lists what that development has and which
of its hypotheses these results would discharge.  \emph{Update, 30 September
2026:} a module-by-module algebraic blueprint for depth three is
Section~\ref{xxb:d3:sec:formalization}; none of its modules exists.)

\textbf{Question 8.}  Extend the method to derivative expansions of
$x^{x^r}$ for integer $r\ge3$, or to other exponential-logarithmic
families.  The correct depth generating function and the symmetry center
must first be rederived.  The success of the square-exponent case does
not justify assuming an analogous support quasipolynomial elsewhere.

\subsubsection{Suggested order of work}
The most direct continuation is to formalize the bulk factorization while
independently implementing certified moment bounds for depths five and
six.  After those bounds determine practical finite rectangles, the
existing integer recurrence can generate exact certificates immediately.
Only then should one search for a depth-uniform induction or arithmetic
zero-separation theorem.  This separates achievable finite obligations
from the genuinely new ingredient needed for the complete conjecture.

\subsection{Depth coordinates: conclusion, verifier and source audit}\label{xxb:dc:sec:conclusion}

\subsubsection{Conclusion of the addition}
The obstruction in A290268 is not merely the existence of a few symbolic
cancellations.  Its coefficients combine a consecutive-root factor with
an imaginary-root factor, and their tail values are signed Mellin
moments of an explicitly oscillating logarithmic polynomial.  Making both
structures visible yields three concrete advances in the manuscript's
account: positivity in the whole bulk at every depth, an explicit finite
decision problem at every fixed depth, and a complete nonvanishing
classification at depths three and four, supplementing the first two depths.
Of these, the first was already Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:positive} of this part,
and the second and third are new.

The 16,034-cell verification is logically effective because the manuscript
first proves that it is exhaustive for those two infinite-parameter
strata.  It is not extrapolation from a finite sample.  The full counting
formula remains a separate target at depths five and higher, with the
remaining questions and the precise proof boundary now substantially
narrowed.

\subsubsection{A compact standalone finite verifier}\label{xxb:dc:sec:verifier}
The following standalone version verifies the two exact rectangles and six seed
signs; it is shipped, byte for byte, as
\texttt{code/02-depth-\allowbreak certificates-\allowbreak minimal\_verify.py} (whose docstring calls
this listing ``Appendix A of article.tex'', the manuscript's numbering).  The
fuller program \texttt{code/02-depth-\allowbreak certificates-\allowbreak verify.py} additionally
checks the independent Pascal recurrence, produces the CSV witnesses, and
cross-checks the original differentiation lattice.

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
def must(test):
    if not test:
        raise ArithmeticError("certificate failure")

def column(d, q, K):
    r = 2*d + 1 + q
    a = [1] + [0]*d
    for j in range(r):
        a = [(2*d-j)*a[i] + (a[i-1] if i else 0)
             for i in range(d+1)]
    out = [a[d]]
    if K == 0:
        return out
    b = [(2*d-q)*a[i] + (2*a[i-1] if i else 0)
         for i in range(d+1)]
    out.append(b[d])
    for k in range(1, K):
        c = [(2*d-q)*b[i] + (2*b[i-1] if i else 0)
             + k*(k+r)*a[i] for i in range(d+1)]
        out.append(c[d])
        a, b = b, c
    return out

for d, R, Q in [(3, 14, 38), (4, 85, 185)]:
    for q in range(Q):
        for k, value in enumerate(column(d, q, R-2)):
            hole = (q == 2*d and (k+d) % 2 == 0)
            must((value == 0) == hole)
            if not hole:
                must(value % 1009 != 0 or value % 1013 != 0)
    must(column(d, 2*d, R)[R] > 0)
    ks, ka = (d-1) % 2, d % 2
    must((-1)**Q * column(d, Q, ks)[ks] > 0)
    must((-1)**(Q+1) * column(d, Q, ka)[ka] > 0)
print("PASS")
\end{lstlisting}

The loop bounds are conclusions of the analytic reduction, not values
chosen merely because the program happened to finish.  Each arithmetic
step is the recurrence \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Vrec} in the ring $\Z[u]/(u^{d+1})$.
(The Python variable \texttt{j} in \texttt{column} is a loop index over the
factors of the falling factorial, not either meaning of $j$ in
Table~\ref{xxb:dc:tab:notation}.)  Thus the program is a finite, reproducible
arithmetic certificate with a fully specified mathematical meaning.

\subsubsection{Source audit}\label{xxb:dc:sec:audit}
The manuscript's inspected repository snapshot is
\texttt{afb2d1227d8bc5df\allowbreak 3beffc1463e958db\allowbreak 3544960b}.  At that snapshot the
A290268 research note explicitly identified higher-depth negative-region
nonvanishing and the general-$k$ bulk as open parts of its analysis; the
general-$k$ bulk was in fact already Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:positive} of this
report, which the manuscript did not inspect.  The \texttt{Main.lean} file
makes the support characterization a hypothesis.  The proof in the manuscript
does not depend on uninspected formal claims or on the consistency of
unrelated parts of the repository.

The manuscript's scoped directory listing contains the core, closed-form,
counting, structural, table, and main modules.  The research note also
discusses other module names and development intentions.  Those descriptions
are not treated as evidence that every discussed file is present or that every
corresponding theorem is kernel-certified.  (At the intake, the three module
names that note lists beyond these six, \texttt{A290268.Hole},
\texttt{A290268.DepthOne} and \texttt{A290268.Series}, were confirmed absent
from the repository's entire history.)

External checks included the live OEIS entry, the NIST generating
function, and the primary paper by \v{S}tampach, whose bibliographic data the
manuscript cross-checked against a paper of R.~B.~Paris (bibliographic
verification only, recorded in \texttt{02-depth-\allowbreak certificates-\allowbreak SOURCE\_AUDIT.md}).
Search results did not supply a prior published proof of the specific
fixed-depth statements proved here.  That limited search is not a proof of
universal novelty.

\subsection{Depth certificates: provenance, files and formal status}\label{xxb:dc:sec:provenance}

\subsubsection{Provenance}
Subsections~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:intro}--\ref{xxb:dc:sec:provenance} were built from
\textbf{one} manuscript: batch~42, manuscript~06, archive
\texttt{ProveIt\_A290268\_Finite\_Certificates.zip} (22 pages, dated 29
September 2026), pinned to ProveIt commit
\texttt{afb2d1227d8bc5df\allowbreak 3beffc1463e958db\allowbreak 3544960b}, and placed in this report
by commit \texttt{3609d0473}.  Its Section~1 is split between
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:intro} (main results, novelty) and the opening of
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:models} (the conjecture, linear independence); its
Sections~2--11 map one for one onto
Subsections~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:models}--\ref{xxb:dc:sec:future}; its Section~12
and its two appendices form Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:conclusion}.  Every theorem, proposition, lemma, corollary,
definition, remark and question of the manuscript is printed; none was
weakened or strengthened.  The editorial choices were these.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
\item It is placed as subsections at the end of Part~\ref{xxb:part} rather than
 as a new part, so that no existing section, theorem or equation number moves.
\item Its bulk positivity, symmetry holes, depths one and two, upper bound,
 depth bridge, linear independence and branch-cut formula are marked as second
 routes to, or re-indexings of, results of this part, which the manuscript did
 not read.  The editorial Remarks~\ref{xxb:dc:rem:beta}, \ref{xxb:dc:rem:inside}
 and~\ref{xxb:dc:rem:improve} and the parenthetical translations are additions
 of the intake, not of the manuscript.
\item The manuscript's letters are kept, with the renamings of
 Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:notation}; its reproduction commands are rewritten
 to the shipped file names; its archive table is replaced by the file list
 below; its OEIS citation is merged with this part's.
\item Its statement that the finite checks ``have actually been run'' is kept,
 with the intake's rerun recorded beside it.
\end{itemize}
Not shipped: the manuscript's \texttt{article.tex}, \texttt{article.pdf},
\texttt{README.md} and \texttt{SHA256SUMS.txt} (verified at placement, 10 of 10
entries); the archive itself survives in the history of commit
\texttt{8315d24e3}.

\subsubsection{Formal status}\label{xxb:dc:sec:formal}
Nothing in this addition is formalized, and its placement beside a Lean
development confers no formal status.  The Lean development
\texttt{Combinatorics/\allowbreak DerivativeExpansions/\allowbreak A290268/\allowbreak Lean} (library
\texttt{A290268}, namespace \texttt{LeanProofs.A290268}, imported by the root
\texttt{ProveIt.lean}) relates to it as follows.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \texttt{coeff n j k} (\texttt{Core.lean}) is the recurrence
 \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:rec-lattice} in the same coordinates; \texttt{S n}
 (\texttt{Count.lean}) is the conjectured support, and \texttt{card\_S} proves
 $|S_n|$ equal to the closed form unconditionally.
\item The manuscript relies on the \emph{statements} of the three conditional
 theorems of \texttt{Main.lean}: \texttt{a\_\allowbreak eq\_\allowbreak closedForm\_\allowbreak of\_\allowbreak support}
 (hypothesis: the support of \texttt{coeff n} is \texttt{S n}),
 \texttt{a\_\allowbreak eq\_\allowbreak closedForm\_\allowbreak of\_\allowbreak two\_\allowbreak sided} (the structural and nonvanishing
 inclusions as separate hypotheses) and
 \texttt{a\_\allowbreak eq\_\allowbreak closedForm\_\allowbreak of\_\allowbreak support\_\allowbreak all} (the support hypothesis at every
 order).  It reads them, correctly, as conditional.
\item \texttt{Structural.mem\_\allowbreak S\_\allowbreak of\_\allowbreak coeff\_\allowbreak ne\_\allowbreak zero} proves the structural
 inclusion from a hypothesis \texttt{hhole}: vanishing on the line $N$ odd,
 $-j\equiv N\pmod4$, $-j\ge3$, $2(-j)-1\le N$, $2k=N+2j+1$.  With $-j=2d+1$
 that line is exactly the hole family of Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:holes}, and
 so of Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:reflection-zeros}; either proof would discharge
 \texttt{hhole} on paper.  Neither is formalized, and the module
 \texttt{A290268.Hole} that the declaration's docstring names as the discharge
 does not exist in the repository.
\item Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four} would discharge the nonvanishing inclusion
 only on cells of depth at most four; depths $d\ge5$ remain, so the
 \texttt{Main.lean} theorems stay conditional.
\item The only unconditional statement about the sequence itself is
 \texttt{Table.a\_\allowbreak values\_\allowbreak le\_\allowbreak 53}, $a(n)$ equal to the closed form for
 $n\le53$, a \texttt{native\_decide} certificate.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{A second route to depth three}\label{xxb:d3:sec:intro}

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{What this addition is.}  Subsections~\ref{xxb:d3:sec:intro}--\ref{xxb:d3:sec:provenance}
were written into this part on 30 September 2026 from a later manuscript,
\emph{Depth Three Is Complete for OEIS A290268: A beta--sine transform,
centered moment polynomials, and exact nonvanishing} (batch~70,
manuscript~03; provenance in Section~\ref{xxb:d3:sec:provenance}).  Its main
theorem (Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:main}) is \textbf{the case $d=3$ of
Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four}}, which the batch-42 addition had already proved
in this part; it is printed here as a \emph{second proof} of that case.  Nothing
in it changes the proof boundary of this part: the unproved region is still
$m\ge5$, $n\ge2k+2$, and the full A290268 conjecture is \emph{not} proved.

\smallskip
\textbf{A correction to the manuscript.}  The manuscript says, in its
abstract, its main-contribution box, its novelty paragraph, its conclusion and
its source audit, that depth three was ``the stated open core'' of the
problem at its pinned commit \texttt{a02ab3567} and that its theorem is new
relative to that commit.  \textbf{That is mistaken.}  The pin descends from
commit \texttt{0e6632abf}, which wrote Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four} (depths
one to four, every $k$ and $M$) into this report on 29 September 2026; and
the note the manuscript cites, \texttt{Oeis/A290268/README.md}, already says
at the pin, in its dated correction, that this report's batch-42 addition
``closes logarithmic deficits 3 and 4 for every \texttt{n}''.  The manuscript
read only that note's older line ``Open core: \texttt{D >= 3}''.  Its
sentences are printed below as the manuscript wrote them, each followed by an
editorial note; none of its mathematics depends on them.

\smallskip
\textbf{What is new relative to this part.}  Several results of the manuscript
re-derive results printed above, by different proofs; they are printed once
more, marked as second routes, with the earlier result credited:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
\item its main theorem and lattice corollary
 (Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:main}, Corollary~\ref{xxb:d3:cor:lattice}) are
 Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four} at $d=3$ and the depth-three holes of
 Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:four};
\item its exact reduction and parameter derivative
 (Proposition~\ref{xxb:d3:prop:exact-reduction},
 Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:parameter-derivative}) are
 Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:bridge} and \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:param};
\item its beta--sine transform (Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:beta-sine}) is the
 branch-cut formula of Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:integral}, obtained, as in
 Remark~\ref{xxb:dc:rem:beta}, from the beta-integral mechanism of
 Lemma~\ref{xxb:lem:beta};
\item its centred recurrence and vertical-line roots
 (Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:R-recurrence},
 Corollary~\ref{xxb:d3:cor:vertical-roots}) are \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Qrec} and
 Corollary~\ref{xxb:dc:cor:imaginary}, proved here by an integration by parts
 and a Favard argument;
\item its bulk positivity (Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:bulk-positive}) is
 Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:bulk} at $d=3$, hence a case of
 Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:positive}.
\end{itemize}
New relative to this part are the harmonic factorization at the root $x=7$
(Subsection~\ref{xxb:d3:sec:harmonic}); the sign law of the harmonic quantity
$\mathsf E_q$ (Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:E-sign}), which \emph{proves} the
$q=37/38$ sign change that Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:four} recorded only as
an observation of the certificate data; the propagation recurrence
\eqref{xxb:d3:eq:U-recurrence} in $k$ and the finite certificate it needs
(Proposition~\ref{xxb:d3:prop:finite-certificate}), a different finite set
from the batch-42 rectangle; the eventual signs of
Corollary~\ref{xxb:d3:cor:stable-signs} for every $q$; the sign phase diagram
of the log-free diagonal; the beta representation of the uncentred quotient
(Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:beta-Q}); and the interlacing clause of
Corollary~\ref{xxb:d3:cor:vertical-roots}.  Remark~\ref{xxb:d3:rem:compare}
compares the two proofs and shows that their finite anchors partly coincide.

\smallskip
\textbf{Notation.}  These subsections use the depth coordinates of
Table~\ref{xxb:dc:tab:notation} --- \textbf{$k$ is the exponent of $\log x$},
$j$ the exponent of $x$, $d$ the depth --- and the batch-42 names for the
objects batch~42 already named.  The manuscript's own letters clash with
both, so many are renamed; Table~\ref{xxb:d3:tab:notation} lists every
renaming and is binding from here to the end of the part.

\medskip
\textbf{Abstract of manuscript 03.}\itshape
OEIS A290268 counts the terms in the fully expanded $n$th derivative of
$x^{x^2}$.  The ProveIt reduction expresses every coefficient through
$\gamma(k,D,M)=[w^M](2+w)^k\bigl((1+w)^2\log(1+w)\bigr)^D$,
and identifies the remaining support conjecture with a nonvanishing problem
for these rational numbers.  At the pinned repository snapshot, depths
$D=1,2$ are solved in the accompanying research notes, whereas $D\geq3$ is
the stated open core.

This article proves the complete zero classification at the first open depth:
for every $k\geq0$ and $M\geq3$,
\[
  \gamma(k,3,M)=0
  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  M=k+13\ \text{and}\ k\ \text{is odd}.
\]
Thus every depth-three coefficient is nonzero except for the symmetry-forced
holes already predicted by the global conjecture.

The proof has three new ingredients.  First, a general beta--sine transform
represents the tail coefficients at every depth by a single logit moment with
an explicit kernel
$K_D(L)=\bigl((L+i\pi)^D-(L-i\pi)^D\bigr)/(2i\pi)$.
Second, a family of centered beta-moment polynomials satisfies the positive
three-term recurrence
$R_{N,k+1}(y)=2yR_{N,k}(y)+k(N+k)R_{N,k-1}(y)$.
Third, at $D=3$ the problem reduces to a signed harmonic quantity whose only
phase transition occurs between $q=37$ and $q=38$.  Positivity propagation
then reduces the remaining proof to a finite exact certificate over
$\Q$.  The supplied standard-library Python verifier uses
\texttt{fractions.Fraction}; no floating-point assertion enters the proof.

This is a theorem relative to the definitions in the pinned ProveIt snapshot,
not a claim that the full A290268 conjecture has been proved: depths $D\geq4$
remain open.  No independent peer review or Lean formalization is claimed.
\upshape\par
\smallskip
\emph{Editorial notes.}  (i) The abstract is printed in the manuscript's
letters; below, $D$ is $d$, $n$ is $N$, $K_D$ is $R_d$, $L$ is $\tau$, and
$R_{N,k}$ is $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$ (Table~\ref{xxb:d3:tab:notation}).
(ii) ``$D\ge3$ is the stated open core'', ``the first open depth'' and
``depths $D\ge4$ remain open'' are stale at the pin: depths three and four
were settled by Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four}, and the open depths are
$d\ge5$.  (iii) Of the ``three new ingredients'', the first two are not new
relative to this part (the bullets above); the third, the harmonic phase
transition with its propagation in $k$, is.
\end{quote}

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Main contribution (the manuscript's summary box).}
The first previously open depth in the ProveIt support program is completely
settled.  In the original coefficient lattice, if
$d=(N+j)/2-k=3$ and $N\geq k+3$, then
\[
 c_N(j,k)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 j=-7,\quad k\ \text{odd},\quad N=2k+13.
\]
Every other depth-three lattice point is nonzero.

\smallskip
\emph{Editorial note.}  ``First previously open depth'' is mistaken (see
above): this is the depth-three content of Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four},
whose holes Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:four} lists as $q=6$, $k$ odd;
$j=-7$, $N=2k+13$.  In the letters of the rest of this part: $m=3$, $j$ odd,
$n=2j+13$.
\end{quote}
