Further changes, also without change of normalization: the manuscript's
$\phi$ is $\varphi$ of \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:gamma}; its centre $c_N=(N+1)/2$ is
written $(r+1)/2$, because $c_N(j,k)$ is the coefficient; its evaluation point
$a=2D+1$ is written $2d+1$ (it is $7$ at depth three); its $B=-E_q$ is written
$|\mathsf E_q|$; summation and product indices written $r$ in the manuscript
are $\ell$; the hole index $r$ of its lattice corollary is $\ell$; the
index $m$ of its count formula is $\rho$, as in \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:countconj};
and its indeterminate $a$ in Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:parameter-derivative} is
$\theta$.  The
manuscript's falling factorial $P_M$ is the batch-42 $\fall{s}{M}$ at $s=x-1$,
and $\mathcal H_{M,k}(x)=M!\,\mathcal A_{M,k}(x-1)$, so the manuscript's
$x$ is the batch-42 $s$ plus one: the evaluation point $x=2d+1$ is $s=2d$ of
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:param}.  By Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:factor} the
manuscript's quotient polynomial $Q_{N,k}$ is the centred polynomial
$\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$ translated by $(r+1)/2$, and its $R_{N,k}$ \emph{is}
$\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$; that is why both are printed with the batch-42 letter.
Its kernel $K_D$ and the batch-42 $R_d$ are the same polynomial, since
$\bigl((\tau+i\pi)^d-(\tau-i\pi)^d\bigr)/(2i\pi)=\operatorname{Im}(\tau+i\pi)^d/\pi$
for real $\tau$.  Finally, the two tail values are related by
\begin{equation}\label{xxb:d3:eq:dictionary}
 H_{3,k,q}=720\,(-1)^q\,q!\;\mathsf F_k(q),
\end{equation}
which follows from \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:q} and \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-F} below and
was checked at the intake in exact arithmetic for $0\le k\le14$, $0\le q<60$.
In particular $\sgn H_{3,k,q}=(-1)^q\sgn\mathsf F_k(q)$.

\subsubsection{Problem, status and scope (manuscript Section~1)}\label{xxb:d3:sec:scope}

\paragraph{The sequence and its support conjecture.}
Let
\[
  \left(\frac{d}{dx}\right)^N x^{x^2}
  =\sum_{j,k}c_N(j,k)x^{x^2+j}(\log x)^k.
\]
OEIS A290268 is $a(N)=\#\{(j,k):c_N(j,k)\neq0\}$.  The public OEIS entry still
labels as conjectural the generating function
\[
 \sum_{N\geq0}a(N)z^N
 =\frac{1+z+2z^2+2z^3+2z^4+2z^5+2z^6+z^7+2z^8+z^9}
 {(1-z)(1-z^2)(1-z^8)},
\]
equivalently $a(2\rho)=2\rho^2+2\rho+1$,
$a(2\rho-1)=2\rho^2-\lfloor \rho/4\rfloor$ \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:countconj}.
(Here $z$ is a generating-function variable, local to this display.)  The
entry records $54$ terms, through $N=53$; the manuscript inspected it on
30 September 2026 \cite{oeis290268}.

The pinned ProveIt notes reduce the conjecture to an exact support statement.
They describe a depth statistic $d$ and a coefficient $\gamma(k,d,M)$, prove
or outline the symmetry-forced holes, record complete arguments at depths
$d=1,2$, and identify $d\geq3$ as the open nonvanishing core
\cite{xxbd3-notes}.  The Lean module \texttt{A290268.Main} makes the final
logical boundary explicit: the closed form follows as soon as the coefficient
support is identified with the conjectured finite set \cite{xxbd3-main}.

\emph{Editorial note.}  The notes do say ``Open core: \texttt{D >= 3}'' at the
pin, in their section on solved strata; the same file's dated correction
(``Correction (29 September 2026)'') says that this report's batch-42 addition
closes deficits three and four for every $n$.  The reading of
\texttt{A290268.Main} is correct: its three theorems are conditional on the
support characterization (Section~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:formal}).

\paragraph{What is proved here.}
The present report resolves the entire $d=3$ stratum.  It does not infer the
result from a numerical scan.  The infinite ranges in both $k$ and $M$ are
proved using recurrences and positivity.  A finite exact computation is used
only for a compact critical strip where a signed kernel prevents a direct
one-sign argument.

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Proof boundary (the manuscript's status box).}
The analytic and algebraic reductions are proved in the text.  One finite
lemma checks exact rational signs for
$q\in\{1,\ldots,5\}\cup\{7,\ldots,37\}$, $0\leq k\leq14$,
together with the centre $q=6$.  The included verifier reproduces that lemma
from integer polynomial recurrences and rational harmonic numbers.  Large
additional boxes are checked only as cross-validation and are not needed for
the theorem.
\end{quote}

\paragraph{Novelty discipline.}
The result is new relative to repository commit \texttt{a02ab356}: the
pinned A290268 status document explicitly calls $d\geq3$ open
\cite{xxbd3-notes}.  The OEIS entry also continues to call the global
formula conjectural \cite{oeis290268}.  A targeted search found no later
public proof of this depth-three stratum.  This statement is deliberately
narrow: the report has not undergone independent refereeing, and it does not
claim exhaustive priority over inaccessible or unpublished work.

\emph{Editorial note.}  The first sentence is mistaken: at
\texttt{a02ab356} the repository already contained
Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four}, whose $d=3$ case is the manuscript's theorem,
and the cited document's own correction points to it.  The theorem is
therefore \emph{not} new relative to the pin; its proof is a second, different
route.  The remaining sentences --- the OEIS status, the narrow search, no
refereeing, no exhaustive priority --- stand.

\subsection{Second route to depth three: the coefficient model and a falling-factorial transform}\label{xxb:d3:sec:model}

Set $\varphi(w)=(1+w)^2\log(1+w)$.  The Taylor identity
\[
 \sum_{N\geq0}\frac{\xi^N}{N!}
 \left(\frac{d}{dx}\right)^N x^{x^2}
 =(x+\xi)^{(x+\xi)^2}
\]
becomes, after dividing by $x^{x^2}$ and putting $w=\xi/x$,
\[
 x^{-x^2}(x+\xi)^{(x+\xi)^2}
 =\exp\!\bigl(x^2w(2+w)\log x\bigr)
   \exp\!\bigl(x^2\varphi(w)\bigr).
\]
This identity is valid formally in $w$ over a coefficient ring containing
$x,x^{-1},\log x$; no analytic choice of logarithm is needed for coefficient
extraction.

\begin{definition}[Depth and reduced coefficient]
For integers $N\geq0$, $k\geq0$, and an exponent shift $j$ of the correct
parity, define $d=(N+j)/2-k$ and $M=N-k$, and, for $d,M\geq0$,
$\gamma(k,d,M)=[w^M](2+w)^k\varphi(w)^d$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Exact reduction; second route to Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:bridge}]
\label{xxb:d3:prop:exact-reduction}
Whenever $d=(N+j)/2-k$ and $M=N-k$ are nonnegative integers,
\[
  c_N(j,k)=\frac{N!}{k!\,d!}\,\gamma(k,d,M).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Choose the $k$th term from the first exponential and the $d$th term from the
second.  Their product is
\[
 \frac{x^{2k+2d}w^k(2+w)^k\varphi(w)^d(\log x)^k}{k!\,d!}.
\]
Extracting $w^N=\xi^N/x^N$ yields the power $x^{2k+2d-N}=x^j$ and the
coefficient $[w^{N-k}](2+w)^k\varphi(w)^d$.  Multiplication by $N!$ gives the
claim.
\end{proof}

(The proof is the one printed for Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:bridge},
itself the Taylor-expansion proof of Proposition~\ref{xxb:prop:egf}.)  The
global support conjecture can therefore be stated entirely in terms of
$\gamma$.  For $d\geq1$, the predicted zeros are exactly
$M=k+4d+1$, $k+d\equiv0\pmod2$; at $d=3$ this becomes $M=k+13$, $k$ odd.

\subsubsection{A falling-factorial transform}\label{xxb:d3:sec:falling}

Define $\mathcal P_M(x)=\prod_{\ell=1}^{M}(x-\ell)$, with $\mathcal P_0(x)=1$.
The following elementary identity is the algebraic entrance to the proof.

\begin{lemma}[Coefficient as a parameter derivative]
\label{xxb:d3:lem:parameter-derivative}
For $M,d\geq0$ and an indeterminate $\theta$,
\[
 M!\,[w^M](1+w)^{\theta}\log^d(1+w)=\mathcal P_M^{(d)}(\theta+1).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $[w^M](1+w)^{\theta}=\binom{\theta}{M}=\mathcal P_M(\theta+1)/M!$,
differentiate $d$ times with respect to $\theta$.
\end{proof}

(The manuscript's indeterminate is $a$; it is $\theta$ here because $a$ is the
term count.  With $\theta=s$ this is \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:param}.)  Because
$2+w=1+(1+w)$, $(2+w)^k=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom k\ell(1+w)^\ell$.  Taking
$\theta=2d+\ell$ in Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:parameter-derivative} gives
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-H}
 M!\,\gamma(k,d,M)
 =\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom k\ell \mathcal P_M^{(d)}(2d+1+\ell).
\end{equation}
Introduce $\mathcal H_{M,k}(x)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom k\ell\mathcal P_M(x+\ell)$.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:H-derivative}
 M!\,\gamma(k,d,M)=\mathcal H_{M,k}^{(d)}(2d+1).
\end{equation}

Suppose now that $r=M-k\geq0$.  Every summand $\mathcal P_M(x+\ell)$ is
divisible by $\mathcal P_r(x)$, and direct cancellation gives
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:H-factorization}
 \mathcal H_{M,k}(x)=\mathcal P_r(x)\,\widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k}(x),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:Q-sum}
 \widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k}(x)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom k\ell
 \rise{x}{\ell}\,\rise{(x-M+\ell)}{k-\ell}.
\end{equation}
Here $\rise{x}{\ell}=x(x+1)\cdots(x+\ell-1)$.  (This is the factorization
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:factor} at $s=x-1$, reached by direct cancellation instead of
the formal residue substitution; so $\widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k}(x)
=\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}\bigl(x-\frac{r+1}2\bigr)$.)

\subsection{Second route to depth three: the beta--sine transform and centred moments}\label{xxb:d3:sec:beta-sine}

The quotient polynomial has a useful integral representation.

\begin{lemma}[Beta representation]
\label{xxb:d3:lem:beta-Q}
Let $r\geq0$, $k\geq0$, $M=r+k$, and $0<\Re x<r+1$.  Then
\[
 \widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k}(x)
 =\frac{M!}{\Gamma(x)\Gamma(r-x+1)}
 \int_0^1t^{x-1}(1-t)^{r-x}(2t-1)^k\,dt.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Expand
$(2t-1)^k=(t-(1-t))^k=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}(-1)^{k-\ell}\binom k\ell t^\ell(1-t)^{k-\ell}$.
Termwise beta integration gives $\mathrm B(x+\ell,M-x-\ell+1)$.  After
multiplication by $M!/[\Gamma(x)\Gamma(r-x+1)]$, the first gamma ratio is
$\rise{x}{\ell}$, while the second, together with $(-1)^{k-\ell}$, is
$\rise{(x-M+\ell)}{k-\ell}$.  The sum is exactly \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:Q-sum}.
\end{proof}

Using $\mathcal P_r(x)=\Gamma(x)/\Gamma(x-r)$ and Euler's reflection formula
yields
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:H-beta-sine}
 \mathcal H_{M,k}(x)
 =(-1)^r\frac{M!\sin(\pi x)}{\pi}
 \int_0^1t^{x-1}(1-t)^{r-x}(2t-1)^k\,dt.
\end{equation}
(This is the mechanism of Lemma~\ref{xxb:lem:beta}, applied to
$\mathcal H_{M,k}$ rather than to the finite difference of that lemma.)

Define the kernel polynomial
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:KD}
 R_d(\tau)=\left.\frac{d^d}{d\varepsilon^d}
 \left(\frac{\sin(\pi \varepsilon)}{\pi}e^{\tau \varepsilon}\right)\right|_{\varepsilon=0}
 =\frac{(\tau+i\pi)^d-(\tau-i\pi)^d}{2i\pi}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
$R_d(\tau)=\sum_{\mu\geq0}(-1)^\mu\binom d{2\mu+1}\pi^{2\mu}\tau^{d-2\mu-1}$.
(This is the polynomial $R_d$ of \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Rd}, the manuscript's $K_D$;
the summation index is $\mu$ because $m$ is the deficit.)

\begin{theorem}[Beta--sine transform; second route to Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:integral}]
\label{xxb:d3:thm:beta-sine}
Let $d,k,M\geq0$, set $r=M-k$, and assume $r\geq 2d+1$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:beta-sine-final}
 \gamma(k,d,M)=(-1)^{r+2d+1}
 \int_0^1 t^{2d}(1-t)^{r-2d-1}(2t-1)^k
 R_d\!\left(\log\frac{t}{1-t}\right)dt.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:H-beta-sine} $d$ times at the integer
$x=2d+1$ and use \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:H-derivative}.  Near $2d+1$, write
$x=2d+1+\varepsilon$ and
\[
 t^{x-1}(1-t)^{r-x}
 =t^{2d}(1-t)^{r-2d-1}
 \exp\!\left(\varepsilon\log\frac{t}{1-t}\right).
\]
Also $\sin(\pi(2d+1+\varepsilon))=(-1)^{2d+1}\sin(\pi \varepsilon)$.  The
derivative in $\varepsilon$ is exactly $(-1)^{2d+1}R_d(\tau)$, giving
\eqref{xxb:d3:eq:beta-sine-final}.
\end{proof}

Here $r-2d-1=q$, so the sign is $(-1)^q$, and the substitution
$t\mapsto1-t$ turns \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:beta-sine-final} into
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:cut}; the batch-42 proof deforms a Cauchy contour onto the
branch cut, this one differentiates a beta integral.  The first kernels are
\[
 R_1(\tau)=1,
 \qquad R_2(\tau)=2\tau,
 \qquad R_3(\tau)=3\tau^2-\pi^2,
 \qquad R_4(\tau)=4\tau^3-4\pi^2\tau.
\]
Thus the transform recovers the one-sign depth-one integral and the
antisymmetric logarithmic depth-two integral described in the ProveIt notes.
At depth three the kernel is even but changes sign, explaining why the previous
pointwise positivity strategy stops precisely there.  (This is the obstruction
\eqref{xxb:eq:m3-kernel} of Section~\ref{xxb:sec:small-m}, whose kernel is
$R_3/3!$.)

\subsubsection{Centred beta-moment polynomials}\label{xxb:d3:sec:centered}

Fix $r\geq1$.  The manuscript writes the centred quotient
$\widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k}\bigl(\frac{r+1}2+y\bigr)$ as a new polynomial
$R_{N,k}(y)$; by \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:factor} it is $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}(y)$,
and it is printed so.  These polynomials contain the decisive extra
structure.

\begin{theorem}[Positive three-term recurrence; second proof of \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Qrec}]
\label{xxb:d3:thm:R-recurrence}
For every $r\geq1$, $\mathcal Q_0^{(r)}(y)=1$, $\mathcal Q_1^{(r)}(y)=2y$, and for
$k\geq1$
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:R-recurrence}
 \mathcal Q_{k+1}^{(r)}(y)=2y\,\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}(y)+k(r+k)\,\mathcal Q_{k-1}^{(r)}(y).
\end{equation}
Consequently $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$ has parity $k$, and every nonzero coefficient
in the basis $y^{k},y^{k-2},\ldots$ is a positive integer.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal I_k(x)=\int_0^1t^{x-1}(1-t)^{r-x}(2t-1)^k\,dt$.
Integrate the derivative of $t^x(1-t)^{r-x+1}(2t-1)^k$.  The boundary terms
vanish in the beta strip.  With $y=x-(r+1)/2$, the resulting identity is
\[
 (r+k+1)\,\mathcal I_{k+1}=2y\,\mathcal I_k+k\,\mathcal I_{k-1}.
\]
Multiplying by the factorial prefactor in Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:beta-Q}
gives \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:R-recurrence}.  The parity and coefficient positivity
follow by induction.
\end{proof}

(The batch-42 proof of \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Qrec} compares coefficients in a
first-order differential equation for the generating function
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Qgf}.  The integer and positivity clauses sharpen that
proposition's ``nonnegative coefficients'' only in wording: the recurrence has
integer coefficients.)

\begin{corollary}[Vertical-line roots; second route to Corollary~\ref{xxb:dc:cor:imaginary}]
\label{xxb:d3:cor:vertical-roots}
Every zero of $\widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k}$ lies on the vertical line
$\Re x=(r+1)/2$.  The zeros are simple, and consecutive degrees interlace
after rotation to the real axis.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Put $\mathcal T_{r,k}(s)=i^{-k}\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}(is)$.  After monic
normalization, \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:R-recurrence} becomes a Favard recurrence with
positive subdiagonal coefficient $k(r+k)/4$.  Hence $\mathcal T_{r,k}$ has
simple real zeros with the standard interlacing property.  Rotating back gives
the claim.
\end{proof}

This root geometry is not required for the finite certificate, but it explains
why the centred recurrence is so stable and suggests a classical
orthogonal-polynomial interpretation worth developing further.  (The monic
polynomials of the proof are the $p_k$ of the proof of
Corollary~\ref{xxb:dc:cor:imaginary}, which reaches simplicity and the
imaginary axis through a tridiagonal matrix; the interlacing clause is new to
this part.  The classical interpretation is \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:MP}.)

\subsection{Second route to depth three: the harmonic phase transition}\label{xxb:d3:sec:harmonic}

Set $d=3$.  In the tail range write
$M=k+q+7$, $q\geq0$, $r=M-k=q+7$.
The evaluation point in \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:H-derivative} is $x=7$, which is a
root of $\mathcal P_r$.  Factor $\mathcal P_r(x)=(x-7)\Omega_r(x)$.
At $x=7$,
\begin{align}
 \Omega_r(7)&=(-1)^q\,6!\,q!,\label{xxb:d3:eq:S-value}\\
 \frac{\Omega_r'(7)}{\Omega_r(7)}&=\mathrm H_6-\mathrm H_q=:u_q,\label{xxb:d3:eq:S-log1}\\
 \frac{\Omega_r''(7)}{\Omega_r(7)}
 &=(\mathrm H_6-\mathrm H_q)^2-\mathrm H_6^{(2)}-\mathrm H_q^{(2)}=:2\mathsf E_q.\label{xxb:d3:eq:S-log2}
\end{align}
Here $\mathrm H_q=\sum_{\ell=1}^{q}1/\ell$,
$\mathrm H_q^{(2)}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{q}1/\ell^2$, $\mathrm H_0=\mathrm H_0^{(2)}=0$.
Thus
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:E-def}
 \mathsf E_q=\frac{(\mathrm H_q-\mathrm H_6)^2-\mathrm H_q^{(2)}-\mathrm H_6^{(2)}}{2}.
\end{equation}

Let $y_q=7-\frac{r+1}{2}=\frac{6-q}{2}$.  Using
$\mathcal H_{M,k}=\mathcal P_r\widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k}
=(x-7)\Omega_r\widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k}$, one has
$\mathcal H_{M,k}'''(7)=3(\Omega_r\widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k})''(7)$.
Define, with $r=q+7$ and $\mathcal Q=\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$,
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:F-def}
 \mathsf F_k(q)=\frac12\left(
 \mathcal Q''(y_q)+2u_q\mathcal Q'(y_q)+2\mathsf E_q\mathcal Q(y_q)
 \right).
\end{equation}
Then \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:S-value} gives the exact normalization
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-F}
 \boxed{
 \gamma(k,3,k+q+7)
 =\frac{4320(-1)^qq!}{(k+q+7)!}\,\mathsf F_k(q).}
\end{equation}
(With \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:q} this is the dictionary \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:dictionary},
$H_{3,k,q}=720(-1)^qq!\,\mathsf F_k(q)$.)

\subsubsection{The signed harmonic quantity}

The $\mathsf E_q$ sequence has a remarkably simple first difference.

\begin{lemma}[Unique harmonic phase transition]
\label{xxb:d3:lem:E-sign}
For $q\geq0$,
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:E-diff}
 \mathsf E_{q+1}-\mathsf E_q=\frac{\mathrm H_q-\mathrm H_6}{q+1}.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
$\mathsf E_0>0$, $\mathsf E_q<0$ for $1\leq q\leq37$, and $\mathsf E_q>0$ for
$q\geq38$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Substitute $\mathrm H_{q+1}=\mathrm H_q+1/(q+1)$ and
$\mathrm H_{q+1}^{(2)}=\mathrm H_q^{(2)}+1/(q+1)^2$ in
\eqref{xxb:d3:eq:E-def}; the square and second-order increments cancel to give
\eqref{xxb:d3:eq:E-diff}.

Hence $\mathsf E_q$ strictly decreases through $q=6$, has
$\mathsf E_6=\mathsf E_7$, and strictly increases thereafter.  It remains only
to locate the two crossings.  Exact arithmetic gives
\[
 \mathsf E_0=\frac{203}{90}>0,
 \qquad \mathsf E_1=-\frac7{36}<0,
\]
\[
 \mathsf E_{37}=-\frac{43927248584204635973}
 {2111705758564129728000}<0,
 \qquad
 \mathsf E_{38}=\frac{31458788673623539948663}
 {1243794691794272409792000}>0.
\]
Monotonicity supplies all intervening and subsequent signs.
\end{proof}

For $k=0$, \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-F} and $\mathsf F_0(q)=\mathsf E_q$ already
prove an infinite new nonvanishing diagonal.  More importantly, the same
phase transition controls all $k$ through the recurrence of
Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:R-recurrence}.

\emph{Editorial note.}  The $k=0$ diagonal is not new as a nonvanishing
statement: it is the $k=0$ row of Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four} at $d=3$, and
the formula behind it is the $D=3$ case of the elementary-symmetric formula in
the notes (\eqref{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-k0} below).  What the lemma adds is the exact
sign law.  Through \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:dictionary} and the sign rule
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:sign-rule}, the reduced expectation of the $d=3$, $k=0$ row has
the sign of $\mathsf E_q$; so the lemma \emph{proves} the observation of
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:four} that this expectation ``is positive at $q=0$,
negative for $1\le q\le37$, and positive for all $q\ge38$'', which the
batch-42 addition read off its certificate data.

\subsection{Second route to depth three: the complete tail and the full classification}\label{xxb:d3:sec:tail}

\subsubsection{Automatic positivity outside the critical strip}

For $q<6$, put $\lambda_q=\frac{6-q}{2}>0$ and $\alpha_q=\mathrm H_6-\mathrm H_q>0$.
For $q>6$, put $\lambda_q=\frac{q-6}{2}>0$ and $\alpha_q=\mathrm H_q-\mathrm H_6>0$.
By parity of $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$, after removing the factor $(-1)^k$ when
$q>6$, the relevant expression is
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:U-general}
 \mathsf U_{q,k}=\mathcal Q''(\lambda_q)+2\alpha_q\mathcal Q'(\lambda_q)
 +2\mathsf E_q\mathcal Q(\lambda_q),
 \qquad \mathcal Q=\mathcal Q_k^{(q+7)}.
\end{equation}
More precisely,
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:F-U}
 2\mathsf F_k(q)=
 \begin{cases}
 \mathsf U_{q,k},&q<6,\\
 (-1)^k\mathsf U_{q,k},&q>6.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Since $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$ has positive coefficients,
$\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}(\lambda)>0$ and its derivatives are nonnegative for
$\lambda>0$.  Therefore Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:E-sign} immediately gives
$\mathsf U_{0,k}>0$ and $\mathsf U_{q,k}>0$ for $q\geq38$, for every $k$.

\subsubsection{Positivity propagation in the finite strip}

For $q\in\mathcal C:=\{1,\ldots,5\}\cup\{7,\ldots,37\}$, $\mathsf E_q<0$, and
with $r=q+7$ fixed the functional reads
$\mathsf U_k:=\mathsf U_{q,k}=\mathcal Q_k''(\lambda_q)+2\alpha_q\mathcal Q_k'(\lambda_q)
-2|\mathsf E_q|\,\mathcal Q_k(\lambda_q)$, where $\mathcal Q_k=\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$
(the manuscript's $U_k$, with $B=|\mathsf E_q|$).  Applying this differential
operator to \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:R-recurrence} gives
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:U-recurrence}
 \mathsf U_{k+1}=2\lambda_q\mathsf U_k+k(r+k)\mathsf U_{k-1}
 +4\bigl(\mathcal Q_k'(\lambda_q)+\alpha_q\mathcal Q_k(\lambda_q)\bigr).
\end{equation}
The final term is strictly positive.  Consequently, once two consecutive
$\mathsf U_k$ are positive, every later $\mathsf U_k$ is positive.

The only remaining work is therefore finite.

\begin{proposition}[Exact critical-strip certificate]
\label{xxb:d3:prop:finite-certificate}
For every $q\in\mathcal C$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*),nosep]
  \item $\mathsf U_{q,k}\neq0$ for $0\leq k\leq14$;
  \item $\mathsf U_{q,13}>0$ and $\mathsf U_{q,14}>0$.
\end{enumerate}
At $q=6$, $\mathsf F_k(6)=0$ for odd $k$, while $\mathsf F_k(6)\neq0$ for even
$0\leq k\leq14$, and $\mathsf F_{14}(6)>0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
This is the finite exact component of the proof.  Starting from
$\mathcal Q_0=1$, $\mathcal Q_1=2y$, the verifier constructs every
$\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$ by \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:R-recurrence} with integer
coefficients.  It evaluates \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:U-general} at the half-integer
$\lambda_q=|q-6|/2$ using \texttt{fractions.Fraction}; harmonic numbers are
also exact fractions.  The compressed sign results are displayed in
Table~\ref{xxb:d3:tab:F-signs}.  The supplied file
\texttt{code/03-depth-\allowbreak three-\allowbreak verify\_depth3.py}
(delivered as \texttt{verify\_\allowbreak depth3.py}) performs all assertions
independently of any computer algebra system.
\end{proof}

(The manuscript writes the threshold in the caption as $r$; it is $r_0$ here
because $r=M-k$.  The verifier prints these signs for $0\le q\le38$; the rows'
behaviour for $q\ge38$ is the analytic statement $\mathsf U_{q,k}>0$ above,
through \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:F-U}.)

\subsubsection{The centre \texorpdfstring{$q=6$}{q=6}}

When $q=6$, the evaluation point is $y=0$, and $u_6=0$.  By parity,
$\mathcal Q_k^{(13)}(0)=\mathcal Q_k^{(13)\prime\prime}(0)=0$ for odd $k$,
hence $\mathsf F_k(6)=0$.  For even $k$, set
$\mathsf V_k=\mathcal Q_k^{(13)\prime\prime}(0)+2\mathsf E_6\mathcal Q_k^{(13)}(0)=2\mathsf F_k(6)$.
At $\lambda=\alpha=0$, \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:U-recurrence} reduces, for odd $k$, to
\[
 \mathsf V_{k+1}=k(13+k)\mathsf V_{k-1}+4\mathcal Q_k^{(13)\prime}(0).
\]
The derivative term is positive.  The finite certificate gives
$\mathsf V_{14}>0$, so every even $\mathsf V_k$ with $k\geq14$ is positive.
The even cases below $14$ are nonzero by the same certificate.  Thus the
centre has exactly the odd-$k$ zeros.

\begin{theorem}[Complete depth-three tail]
\label{xxb:d3:thm:tail}
For all $k,q\geq0$,
\[
 \gamma(k,3,k+q+7)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 q=6\ \text{and}\ k\ \text{is odd}.
\]
Equivalently, in the tail $M\geq k+7$,
$\gamma(k,3,M)=0$ if and only if $M=k+13$ and $k$ is odd.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $q=0$ and $q\geq38$, positivity follows directly from
Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:E-sign} and \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:F-U}.  For $q\in\mathcal C$,
the two positive anchors in Proposition~\ref{xxb:d3:prop:finite-certificate}
and recurrence \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:U-recurrence} imply $\mathsf U_{q,k}>0$ for
all $k\geq13$; the smaller $k$ are nonzero by the finite certificate.  The
centre $q=6$ was handled above.  Finally use \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-F}, whose
prefactor never vanishes.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Stable tail signs]
\label{xxb:d3:cor:stable-signs}
For even $k\geq14$, $\mathsf F_k(q)>0$ for every $q\geq0$.  For odd $k\geq13$,
$\mathsf F_k(q)>0$ for $q<6$, $\mathsf F_k(6)=0$, and $\mathsf F_k(q)<0$ for
$q>6$.
\end{corollary}

\subsubsection{Bulk positivity and the full depth-three theorem}\label{xxb:d3:sec:bulk}

It remains to treat $3\leq M\leq k+6$.  Multiplication by $2+w$ gives the
Pascal-type recurrence
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-k-recurrence}
 \gamma(k+1,3,M)=2\gamma(k,3,M)+\gamma(k,3,M-1),
\end{equation}
the case $d=3$ of \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:pascalgamma}.
For $k=0$ and $3\leq M\leq6$, $M!\,\gamma(0,3,M)=\mathcal P_M'''(7)>0$,
because every factor $7-\ell$ is positive and the product-rule expansion of
the third derivative has positive summands.  (This is the ``$k=0$ bulk'' of
the notes \cite{xxbd3-notes}.)  The tail anchor $\gamma(k,3,k+7)>0$ for every
$k$ follows from $q=0$ in Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:tail} together with
$\mathsf F_k(0)>0$.

\begin{lemma}[Bulk positivity; second route to Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:bulk} at $d=3$]
\label{xxb:d3:lem:bulk-positive}
For every $k\geq0$ and $3\leq M\leq k+6$, $\gamma(k,3,M)>0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Induct on $k$.  The case $k=0$ was just proved.  Suppose the claim holds for
$k$.  For $3\leq M\leq k+6$, both terms on the right of
\eqref{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-k-recurrence} are nonnegative and the first is
positive.  At the new endpoint $M=k+7$, the two terms are
$2\gamma(k,3,k+7)>0$ and $\gamma(k,3,k+6)>0$.  This proves the range
$3\leq M\leq(k+1)+6$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Complete depth-three zero classification; second proof of Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four} at $d=3$]
\label{xxb:d3:thm:main}
For every $k\geq0$ and $M\geq3$,
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:main-zero}
 \boxed{
 \gamma(k,3,M)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 M=k+13\ \text{and}\ k\ \text{is odd}.}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The bulk $M\leq k+6$ is strictly positive by
Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:bulk-positive}.  The complementary range $M\geq k+7$ is
Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:tail}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Depth three in the original coefficient lattice]
\label{xxb:d3:cor:lattice}
Let $N,k$ be nonnegative integers with $N\geq k+3$, and put $j=2k+6-N$, so
that $(N+j)/2-k=3$.  Then
\[
 c_N(j,k)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 N=2k+13\ \text{and}\ k\ \text{is odd}.
\]
In the zero case $j=-7$.  Thus the depth-three holes are exactly
$c_{4\ell+15}(-7,2\ell+1)=0$, $\ell\geq0$.
Every other depth-three coefficient is nonzero.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply Proposition~\ref{xxb:d3:prop:exact-reduction} and
Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:main} with $M=N-k$.  The positive factor $N!/(k!\,3!)$
does not affect vanishing.  The equation $N-k=k+13$ is $N=2k+13$, and
substituting into $j=2k+6-N$ gives $j=-7$.
\end{proof}

This is exactly the hole predicted by the repository's global support model at
$d=3$: the hole line is $M=k+4d+1$, and the parity condition $k+d$ even is
$k$ odd.

\begin{remark}[The two proofs of depth three compared; editorial]\label{xxb:d3:rem:compare}
Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:main} and the case $d=3$ of
Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four} share the factorization
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:factor} and the recurrence \eqref{xxb:dc:eq:Qrec}, and differ
in how they control the tail.  Batch~42 folds the branch-cut integral onto
the half interval, compares likelihood ratios against a symmetric base, and
needs three seed signs and the rectangle $0\le k\le12$, $0\le q\le37$ (494
integer cells, Proposition~\ref{xxb:dc:prop:smallcert} with $(R,Q)=(14,38)$).
Manuscript~03 factors the root $x=7$ out of $\mathcal P_r$, reads the tail
sign off the harmonic quantity $\mathsf E_q$, and propagates positivity in
$k$ by \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:U-recurrence}; it needs exact rational values for
the 36 values of $q$ in the strip $\mathcal C$ with $0\le k\le14$, plus the
centre $q=6$ (Proposition~\ref{xxb:d3:prop:finite-certificate}).
The finite anchors partly coincide.  By \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:dictionary}, the
batch-42 central seed $H_{3,14,6}=9707604961443840000>0$ is
$720^2\,\mathsf F_{14}(6)$ with $\mathsf F_{14}(6)=18726089817600$, the
manuscript's centre anchor $\mathsf V_{14}>0$; the batch-42 sum-tail seed
$H_{3,0,38}>0$ is $720\cdot38!\,\mathsf E_{38}>0$, the manuscript's harmonic
anchor; and the batch-42 difference-tail seed $H_{3,1,38}<0$ is
$\mathsf F_1(38)<0$, which in the manuscript is automatic from
$\mathsf E_{38}>0$.  The two finite computations also agree where they
overlap: through \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:dictionary} and the sign rule
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:sign-rule}, the rows $0\le k\le12$ of
Table~\ref{xxb:d3:tab:F-signs} reproduce every $d=3$ row of
\texttt{data/02-depth-\allowbreak certificates-\allowbreak sign\_thresholds.csv}
(checked at the intake).  Neither proof is formalized.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Second route to depth three: further exact consequences}\label{xxb:d3:sec:consequences}

\subsubsection{A closed harmonic formula for the log-free diagonal}

For $k=0$ and $M=q+7$, the reciprocal multiset at the root $x=7$ is
$1,\frac12,\ldots,\frac16,-1,-\frac12,\ldots,-\frac1q$.
Its second elementary symmetric function is $\mathsf E_q$.  Therefore
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-k0}
 \gamma(0,3,q+7)
 =\frac{4320(-1)^qq!}{(q+7)!}\mathsf E_q.
\end{equation}
In the original derivative expansion, for $N\geq7$,
\begin{equation}
 \label{xxb:d3:eq:c-logfree}
 c_N(6-N,0)
 =360(-1)^{N-7}(N-7)!
 \left((\mathrm H_{N-7}-\mathrm H_6)^2-\mathrm H_{N-7}^{(2)}-\mathrm H_6^{(2)}\right).
\end{equation}
The sign changes of $\mathsf E_q$ yield an exact phase diagram:
$\gamma(0,3,M)>0$ for $3\leq M\leq7$;
$\sgn\gamma(0,3,M)=(-1)^M$ for $8\leq M\leq44$;
$\sgn\gamma(0,3,M)=(-1)^{M+1}$ for $M\geq45$.
The two consecutive positive values at $M=44,45$ mark the phase reversal.

(Formula \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-k0} is the case $D=3$ of the formula
\[
 \gamma(0,D,M)\,\frac{M!}{D!}=(-1)^q(2D)!\,q!\;
 e_{D-1}\Bigl(1,\ldots,\frac1{2D},-1,\ldots,-\frac1q\Bigr),\qquad q=M-2D-1,
\]
of the notes \cite{xxbd3-notes}; the phase diagram is new.  The intake
rechecked \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-k0} for $q\le123$ and the phase diagram for
$M\le130$ in exact arithmetic.)

\subsubsection{A structural explanation of the holes}

At $q=6$, the centre of the vertical root line in
Corollary~\ref{xxb:d3:cor:vertical-roots} is exactly the evaluation point
$x=7$.  The polynomial $\mathcal Q_k^{(13)}$ has parity $k$, while the
depth-three differential functional at the centre uses only even derivatives.
It therefore annihilates precisely the odd degrees.  The symmetry-forced hole
is not an accidental cancellation of large rational numbers; it is the parity
centre of the beta-moment family.  (This is the parity argument of
Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:holes} at $d=3$, and so the odd-falling-factorial
symmetry of Section~\ref{syn:share}.)

\subsubsection{What this does and does not imply for A290268}

The theorem proves the conjectured support description on the entire
$d=3$ layer.  It does not yet determine all of $a(N)$, because a coefficient
of the $N$th derivative may have depth as large as $N$.  To prove the OEIS
formula, one still needs the corresponding nonvanishing theorem for every
$d\geq4$ (or a global argument that treats all depths simultaneously).

\emph{Editorial note.}  In this report the depth-four theorem is
Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four}; what is still needed is every $d\ge5$, the
region $m\ge5$, $n\ge2k+2$ of Section~\ref{xxb:sec:remaining}.

\subsection{Second route to depth three: certificate, formalization blueprint and research questions}\label{xxb:d3:sec:certificate}

\subsubsection{Exact certificate and reproducibility}\label{xxb:d3:sec:audit}

The manuscript's artifact package contained its source and PDF, the
verifier \texttt{verify\_\allowbreak depth3.py}, the recorded output
\texttt{depth3\_\allowbreak certificate.txt}, and a README.  Of these, this report ships
the verifier and its recorded output, byte-identical to the delivery, as
\texttt{code/03-depth-\allowbreak three-\allowbreak verify\_depth3.py} and
\texttt{data/03-depth-\allowbreak three-\allowbreak depth3\_certificate.txt}
(Section~\ref{xxb:d3:sec:provenance}).

The verifier uses no third-party package.  Its proof-critical path is:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
  \item construct $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$ by the integer recurrence
    \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:R-recurrence};
  \item compute $\mathrm H_q,\mathrm H_q^{(2)},\mathsf E_q$ with \texttt{Fraction};
  \item evaluate $\mathsf F_k(q)$ and $\mathsf U_{q,k}$ exactly in the finite strip;
  \item assert the signs and induction anchors in
    Proposition~\ref{xxb:d3:prop:finite-certificate}.
\end{enumerate}
As independent checks, the script also verifies the normalized $\mathsf F$
recurrence on $0\leq k\leq60$, $0\leq q\leq1000$; extracts
$[w^M](2+w)^k\varphi(w)^3$ directly for $0\leq k\leq24$, $3\leq M\leq180$; and
confirms agreement with \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:gamma-F} and the complete zero
classification throughout that box.  These large-box checks are diagnostic
rather than logical substitutes for the infinite proof.

The recorded certificate-body digest is
\begin{center}
\small\ttfamily
2e284dcc4a8cb79925716f96401b49b0\allowbreak a5f3c523437bdab8399b1ee7133bded9.
\end{center}
A successful run exits with status $0$ and rewrites the certificate
deterministically.  The manuscript's rerun command is
\texttt{python3 verify\_depth3.py}; with the shipped name it is
\begin{lstlisting}
py code/03-depth-three-verify_depth3.py
\end{lstlisting}
run on a copy of the report directory: the program writes
\texttt{depth3\_\allowbreak certificate.txt}, under that unprefixed name, into the
directory that contains it, here \texttt{code/}.  Its checks are Python
\texttt{assert} statements, so it must not be run with \texttt{python -O}.
At the intake it was rerun on a copy: exit status $0$, and the rewritten
certificate is byte-identical to the shipped
\texttt{data/03-depth-\allowbreak three-\allowbreak depth3\_certificate.txt}.

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{No hidden trust upgrade (the manuscript's warning box).}
The exact Python certificate is substantially stronger than a floating-point
scan, but it is not a kernel-checked theorem.  The mathematical reduction to a
finite sign problem must still be reviewed, and a future ProveIt integration
should formalize both the recurrence and the finite checker in Lean or Rocq.
\end{quote}

\subsubsection{Formalization blueprint for ProveIt}\label{xxb:d3:sec:formalization}

The proof was designed to split cleanly along formalization boundaries.
The manuscript suggests the following module structure.
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
  \item \texttt{A290268.SeriesReduction}: formalize
  Proposition~\ref{xxb:d3:prop:exact-reduction} in formal power series and
  connect it to the existing lattice recurrence.
  \item \texttt{A290268.FallingFactorial}: define $\mathcal P_M$, prove
  Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:parameter-derivative}, and derive
  \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:H-derivative}.
  \item \texttt{A290268.CenteredMoments}: define $\widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k}$
  algebraically by \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:Q-sum} and prove
  \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:R-recurrence} without analytic integration, by polynomial
  identities or coefficient induction.
  \item \texttt{A290268.DepthThreeHarmonic}: formalize $\mathsf E_q$,
  \eqref{xxb:d3:eq:E-diff}, and the four exact anchor inequalities in
  Lemma~\ref{xxb:d3:lem:E-sign}.
  \item \texttt{A290268.DepthThreeCertificate}: reflect the finite sign table
  into a Boolean checker and discharge it with \texttt{native\_decide} or a
  kernel-reduced rational computation, according to the project's trust
  policy.
  \item \texttt{A290268.DepthThree}: combine positivity propagation, centre
  parity, bulk induction, and Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:main}.
\end{enumerate}
A natural public endpoint is schematically
\begin{lstlisting}
theorem gamma_depth_three_eq_zero_iff
    (k M : Nat) (hM : 3 <= M) :
    gamma k 3 M = 0 <-> M = k + 13 /\ Odd k
\end{lstlisting}
followed by a bridge to the existing coefficient array:
\begin{lstlisting}
theorem coeff_depth_three_ne_zero_unless_hole
    (n k : Nat) (h : k + 3 <= n) :
    coeff n (2*k + 6 - n) k = 0 <->
      n = 2*k + 13 /\ Odd k
\end{lstlisting}
The actual types will need to respect the repository's integer exponent index
and natural log-degree conventions.

\paragraph{Avoiding analytic overhead.}
The beta--sine transform is conceptually useful but not necessary for the Lean
proof of depth three.  The formal path can remain algebraic:
$\gamma\to\mathcal P_M^{(3)}\to\mathcal P_r\widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k}
\to\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}\text{ recurrence}\to\mathsf E_q,\mathsf U_{q,k}$.
This avoids formal integration, gamma functions, and complex analysis.  The
integral theorem can be formalized separately as a reusable analytic layer.

\emph{Editorial note.}  None of the six modules exists; the manuscript
proposes them and does not claim them.  The library \texttt{A290268} has the
modules \texttt{Core}, \texttt{ClosedForm}, \texttt{Count},
\texttt{Structural}, \texttt{Table} and \texttt{Main}
(Section~\ref{xxb:d3:sec:formal}); it defines no \texttt{gamma}, and its
\texttt{coeff} has type $\N\to\Z\to\Z\to\Z$, so the sketched
\texttt{coeff n (2*k + 6 - n) k} needs the integer index the last sentence
asks for.  The same algebraic interface is Question~7 of
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:future}.

\subsubsection{Further research questions}\label{xxb:d3:sec:further}

The proof suggests a focused continuation program.  The manuscript's nine
questions are printed as it states them; the editorial notes after seven of
them record what this report already contains.

\begin{question}[Depth four]\label{xxb:d3:q:depth-four}
Can the same centred-polynomial method classify all zeros at $d=4$?  The
beta--sine kernel is $R_4(\tau)=4\tau^3-4\pi^2\tau$,
and the root reduction involves a third-order differential functional on
$\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$.  The main challenge is to find a finite-dimensional
positivity cone stable under the recurrence.
\end{question}

\emph{Editorial note: already answered as a classification.}  All zeros at
$d=4$ are classified by Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four} (only $q=8$, $k$ even;
$j=-9$, $N=2k+17$), which was in this report at the manuscript's pin.  What the
question still asks is only methodological: whether the centred-polynomial
route of this addition also proves depth four, perhaps with a smaller finite
certificate than the 15,540 cells of the batch-42 rectangle.

\begin{question}[Uniform depth induction]
For general $d$, evaluation at the root $x=2d+1$ produces an order-$(d-1)$
differential functional of $\widetilde{\mathcal Q}_{r,k}$.  Is there a uniform
matrix recurrence whose invariant cone proves that only the parity-centre holes
occur?
\end{question}

\emph{Editorial note.}  This is a proposed route to Question~4 of
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:future}, which is open.

\begin{question}[Critical-strip bounds]
At $d=3$, positivity becomes automatic outside the finite harmonic strip
$1\leq q\leq37$, and two consecutive $k$-anchors propagate all larger degrees.
Can one bound the analogous strip and anchor degree explicitly as functions of
$d$?
\end{question}

\emph{Editorial note: partly answered.}  Explicit (deliberately crude) bounds
$K_d,Q_d$ as functions of $d$, beyond which every entry at depth $d$ is
nonzero except the holes, are Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:finite}
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:constants}.  Small or sharp bounds, for this route or that
one, remain open; they are Questions~2 and~3 of
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:future}.

\begin{question}[Classical identification]
Identify $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}$ precisely within the continuous Hahn or related
orthogonal-polynomial schemes, including its measure after the rotation
$y\mapsto iy$.  Can known connection coefficients or total-positivity theorems
replace the finite certificate?
\end{question}

\emph{Editorial note: the identification is answered.}  By
\eqref{xxb:dc:eq:MP}, $\mathcal Q_k^{(r)}(y)=k!\,i^kP_k^{((r+1)/2)}(-iy;\pi/2)$,
a Meixner--Pollaczek polynomial (a member of the Hahn class of the DLMF
\cite{xxbdc-dlmf}), so its orthogonality measure after the rotation is the
classical Meixner--Pollaczek weight with $\lambda=(r+1)/2$, $\phi=\pi/2$.  The
second sentence, replacing the certificate, remains open.

\begin{question}[Eliminate the finite certificate]
Find a human inequality proving the signs in Table~\ref{xxb:d3:tab:F-signs},
perhaps from bounds on the logarithmic derivatives of the rotated orthogonal
polynomials.  This would make Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:main} entirely symbolic.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[General hole theorem in the proof assistant]
The repository notes give a clean $w=e^u-1$ parity argument for
$M=k+4d+1$.  Formalizing that argument would make the structural inclusion of
the conjectured support unconditional at every depth, leaving only
nonvanishing.
\end{question}

\emph{Editorial note.}  On paper the hole theorem is proved three times in
this report and the notes: Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:reflection-zeros},
Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:holes}, and the $w=e^u-1$ argument of the notes.  The
formal side is open: the hypothesis \texttt{hhole} of
\texttt{Structural.mem\_\allowbreak S\_\allowbreak of\_\allowbreak coeff\_\allowbreak ne\_\allowbreak zero}
is exactly this statement, and the module \texttt{A290268.Hole} its docstring
names does not exist (Section~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:formal}).

\begin{question}[Neighbouring derivative families]
The notes report a sporadic zero in the analogous $x^x$ family.  Which
functions $x^{x^m}$ have only symmetry-forced holes, and for which $m$ do
sporadic cancellations occur?  The beta--sine transform has a plausible
$m$-parameter generalization.
\end{question}

\emph{Editorial note.}  The sporadic $x^x$ zero is
$b(8,5)=0$, \eqref{xx:eq:exception}, proved in Part~\ref{xx:part}; for
$x^{x^x}$ additional zeros provably exist (Part~\ref{xxc:part}).  The question
for $x^{x^m}$, $m\ge3$, is Question~8 of Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:future}
(there written $x^{x^r}$); it is open.

\begin{question}[Asymptotic harmonic phases]
For $k=0$ and general $d$, the tail is governed by an elementary symmetric
function of $1,\frac12,\ldots,\frac1{2d},-1,-\frac12,\ldots,-\frac1q$.
Determine the number and location of its sign transitions as $q\to\infty$.
The $d=3$ transition $37\to38$ is the first nontrivial case.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Global A290268]
Can the depthwise results be assembled into a single support theorem without
proving each $d$ separately?  A successful uniform argument would immediately
establish the OEIS generating function through the existing ProveIt counting
reduction.
\end{question}

\emph{Editorial note.}  This is Questions~4 and~6 of
Subsection~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:future}; the counting reduction is
Theorem~\ref{xxb:thm:equivalence} on paper and
\texttt{Main.a\_\allowbreak eq\_\allowbreak closedForm\_\allowbreak of\_\allowbreak support\_\allowbreak all}
in Lean.

\subsubsection{Conclusion of manuscript 03}

The A290268 support problem admits more structure than a raw coefficient scan
reveals.  Parameter differentiation converts coefficient extraction into a
falling-factorial derivative.  Factoring the common roots exposes centred
beta-moment polynomials with a positive three-term recurrence.  At depth three,
the remaining signed information collapses to one harmonic phase sequence and
a recurrence-stable positivity functional.  These ingredients prove the full
zero classification $\gamma(k,3,M)=0\iff M=k+13$ and $k$ odd,
settling the first open depth in the pinned ProveIt program.

The result is both mathematically substantive and formalization-friendly.  Its
only finite component is explicit, exact, small, and independently
reproducible.  The next decisive test is depth four: success there would show
whether the invariant-cone mechanism is a one-depth phenomenon or the start of
a uniform proof of the full OEIS conjecture.

\emph{Editorial note.}  ``Settling the first open depth'' is mistaken: depth
three was settled at the pin by Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four}, and this is a
second proof.  ``The next decisive test is depth four'' is a test of the
\emph{method} (Question~\ref{xxb:d3:q:depth-four} and its note); depth four
itself is settled, and the first open depth is $d=5$.

\subsubsection{Core verifier logic (manuscript Appendix~A)}

The complete verifier is shipped with this report.  The following excerpt
shows the proof-critical recurrences; all quantities are exact fractions.
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
def E(q):
    a = H(q) - H(6)
    return (a*a - H(q, 2) - H(6, 2)) / 2

def centered_polynomials(N, max_k):
    R = [[1], [0, 2]]                 # 1, 2*y
    for k in range(1, max_k):
        R.append(2*y_times(R[k]) + k*(N+k)*R[k-1])
    return R

def F_from_polynomial(k, q):
    N = q + 7
    y = Fraction(6-q, 2)
    u = H(6) - H(q)
    R = centered_polynomials(N, k)[k]
    return (R2(y) + 2*u*R1(y) + 2*E(q)*R0(y)) / 2
\end{lstlisting}
The actual source implements the polynomial operations explicitly and asserts
all signs, recurrence identities, tail zeros, bulk positivity checks, and
direct coefficient comparisons.  (The excerpt keeps the manuscript's letters:
\texttt{N} is $r$, \texttt{R} is $\mathcal Q^{(r)}$, \texttt{H} the harmonic
numbers, and \texttt{y\_times}, \texttt{R0}, \texttt{R1}, \texttt{R2} stand
for operations the full program spells out.)

\subsubsection{Formal status}\label{xxb:d3:sec:formal}
Nothing in this addition is formalized, and its placement beside a Lean
development confers no formal status.  The Lean library \texttt{A290268}
(\texttt{Combinatorics/\allowbreak DerivativeExpansions/\allowbreak A290268/\allowbreak Lean})
relates to it exactly as described in Section~\ref{xxb:dc:sec:formal}.  The
manuscript cites only \texttt{A290268.Main}, whose theorems
\texttt{a\_\allowbreak eq\_\allowbreak closedForm\_\allowbreak of\_\allowbreak support},
\texttt{a\_\allowbreak eq\_\allowbreak closedForm\_\allowbreak of\_\allowbreak two\_\allowbreak sided} and
\texttt{a\_\allowbreak eq\_\allowbreak closedForm\_\allowbreak of\_\allowbreak support\_\allowbreak all}
are conditional on the support characterization; it reads them correctly.
Theorem~\ref{xxb:d3:thm:main} would discharge the nonvanishing inclusion only
on cells of depth three, which Theorem~\ref{xxb:dc:thm:four} already covers on
paper; the \texttt{Main} theorems stay conditional.  The six modules of
Section~\ref{xxb:d3:sec:formalization} are proposals and do not exist.

\clearpage
\section{\texorpdfstring{$x^{x^x}$}{x^(x^x)}: OEIS A281434}\label{xxc:part}

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Framework.}  This part instantiates
Theorem~\ref{fw:thm:canonical}\ref{fw:can:a} with the three-variable frame
$u=x^x$, $v=x^{-1}$, $z=\log x$; Proposition~\ref{xxc:prop:recurrence} below is
that instance, derived here independently, and it is the only one of the three
whose frame is not two-dimensional.  Because $u=x^x$ is not of the form
$x^{\lambda}$ with $\lambda$ constant, the independence statement this part
needs is \emph{not} a special case of Theorem~\ref{fw:thm:independence}; the
asymptotic argument that replaces it is given in full below.

\smallskip
\textbf{Notation.}  In this part \textbf{$z$ denotes $\log x$}, not $x^2$;
compare Part~\ref{xxb:part}.  See Section~\ref{fw:notation}.  Throughout this
part $a(n)$ denotes the term count for $x^{x^x}$.

\medskip
\textbf{Abstract of the original report.}\itshape
Let $a(n)$ be the number of nonzero monomials in the fully expanded $n$th
derivative of $x^{x^x}$, after collecting identical terms. We replace repeated
symbolic differentiation by a seven-transition integer recurrence on three
exponents. It computes the prefix through $n$ in $O(n^4)$ integer arithmetic
operations and $O(n^3)$ live integers. A faster implementation performs the
recurrence modulo a machine-size integer and verifies every possible zero
by an independent exact coefficient formula; this method is deterministic,
not a probabilistic zero test. We prove the explicit bounds
\[
 \frac{7n^3+39n^2+26n+3\epsn(n-1)}{24}
 \ \le a(n)\le\ \frac{2n^3+3n^2+4n}{3},\qquad n\ge1,
\]
where $\epsn$ is $1$ for odd $n$ and $0$ for even $n$. In particular,
$a(n)=\Theta(n^3)$. The upper bound counts an explicit lattice envelope;
the lower bound uses nonvanishing leading coefficients and high-multiplicity
roots of coefficient polynomials. We also identify an unavoidable family of
logarithm-free cancellations and an infinite family with positive logarithm
exponent. The stronger statement $a(n)\sim \tfrac23n^3$, and the still stronger
linear-deficit conjecture, are \upshape\emph{not proved here}\itshape. Exact
code, tests, terms through $n=100$, and selected computations through $n=200$
accompany this article.
\end{quote}

\subsection{The problem and the scope of the results}

The sequence A281434 is defined in the OEIS as the number of terms in the fully
expanded $n$th derivative of $x^{x^x}$, with offset $0$~\cite{oeis281434}.
Exponentiation is right-associated. Thus the function in question is
\[
 f(x)=x^{(x^x)}=\exp\bigl(x^x\log x\bigr),\qquad x>0,
\]
not $(x^x)^x=x^{x^2}$. Its initial term counts are
\[
1,3,12,30,64,113,188,285,415,577,780,1017,1312,\ldots.
\]
The positivity restriction supplies an unambiguous real logarithm; all
subsequent coefficient manipulations are exact polynomial identities.

The phrase ``fully expanded'' must include collecting like terms and deleting
zero coefficients. Counting product-rule branches is a different problem.
Even recording every monomial that could be reached by differentiation gives
an overcount: distinct contributions can cancel exactly.

For $n\ge1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:B}
 B(n)=\frac{2n^3+3n^2+4n}{3},\qquad
 \Delta(n)=B(n)-a(n).
\end{equation}
The polynomial $B(n)$ is A037237$(n-1)$~\cite{oeis037237}. The related OEIS
entry A352697 records the differences $\Delta(n)$ and conjectures both
$\Delta(n)=O(n)$ and infinitely many zero differences~\cite{oeis352697}.
These observations are useful motivation, but neither is needed by our
algorithms or proofs.

\begin{theorem}[Main results]\label{xxc:thm:main}
There is a deterministic exact algorithm for A281434 requiring $O(n^4)$
integer arithmetic operations and $O(n^3)$ stored integers to compute
$a(0),\ldots,a(n)$. There is also a machine-modular implementation for a
single $a(n)$, with exact verification of every candidate cancellation.
For $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:mainbounds}
 L(n):=\frac{7n^3+39n^2+26n+3\epsn(n-1)}{24}
 \le a(n)\le B(n),
 \quad \epsn=\frac{1-(-1)^n}{2}.
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:theta}
 a(n)=\Theta(n^3),\qquad
 \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log a(n)}{\log n}=3.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

The distinction between a growth \emph{order} and an asymptotic
\emph{equivalent} is important. Equation~\eqref{xxc:eq:theta} is unconditional.
An equivalent $a(n)\sim\tfrac23n^3$ would additionally require
$\Delta(n)=o(n^3)$; the present bounds do not establish that. The computations
are consistent with a much smaller deficit, but this numerical observation
will not be substituted for a nonvanishing theorem.

\subsection{A canonical differential-polynomial representation}

\subsubsection{Three auxiliary variables}
Put
\[
 u=x^x,\qquad v=x^{-1},\qquad z=\log x,\qquad A=z(z+1).
\]
The three elementary derivatives and the logarithmic derivative of $f$ are
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:basicderivatives}
 u'=u(z+1),\qquad v'=-v^2,\qquad z'=v,
 \qquad \frac{f'}{f}=u(A+v).
\end{equation}
There is a unique polynomial $P_n\in\Z[u,v,z]$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:Pdefinition}
 f^{(n)}(x)=f(x)P_n(x^x,x^{-1},\log x).
\end{equation}
Write
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:coeffdefinition}
 P_n(u,v,z)=\sum_{k,j,\ell\ge0}c_n(k,j,\ell)u^kv^jz^\ell.
\end{equation}
The corresponding expanded term of the original derivative is
\[
 c_n(k,j,\ell)\,x^{x^x+kx-j}(\log x)^\ell.
\]
Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:count}
 a(n)=\#\{(k,j,\ell):c_n(k,j,\ell)\ne0\}.
\end{equation}
In particular, a negative coefficient contributes one term, not minus one.

\subsubsection{Why the representation is unambiguous}

Suppose a finite linear combination of $u^kv^jz^\ell$, after substitution,
vanishes for all sufficiently large positive $x$. Group it by $k$ and choose
the largest $k$ having a nonzero Laurent-polynomial coefficient in $x$ and
$\log x$. Division by $x^{kx}$ makes every smaller-$k$ group exponentially
small relative to that coefficient. Within the largest group, choose the
smallest $j$, and then the largest $\ell$ with nonzero coefficient. Its
asymptotic magnitude cannot be canceled by the remaining Laurent-logarithmic
terms. This is a contradiction. Hence the displayed monomials are linearly
independent in the required finite sense.

This argument is also a safeguard against a representation-dependent term
count: we are counting a specified canonical basis, not the summands in an
arbitrarily factored expression.

\subsubsection{The recurrence and its proof}

\begin{proposition}[Differential recurrence]\label{xxc:prop:recurrence}
Starting from $P_0=1$, the polynomials satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:PRec}
 P_{n+1}=u(z^2+z+v)P_n+u(z+1)\partial_uP_n
          -v^2\partial_vP_n+v\partial_zP_n.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Differentiate~\eqref{xxc:eq:Pdefinition}, apply the product rule, and substitute
\eqref{xxc:eq:basicderivatives}. The result is exactly~\eqref{xxc:eq:PRec}.
The base case is $f^{(0)}=f$. Induction proves the identity at every order,
and also proves integrality of every coefficient.
\end{proof}

For one coefficient $c=c_n(k,j,\ell)$ the seven contributions are as follows.
All contributions with the same destination must be added before zero
coefficients are removed.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Destination at order $n+1$ & Contribution & Source in the derivative\\
\midrule
$(k+1,j,\ell+2)$ & $c$ & $f'/f$: $uz^2$\\
$(k+1,j,\ell+1)$ & $c$ & $f'/f$: $uz$\\
$(k+1,j+1,\ell)$ & $c$ & $f'/f$: $uv$\\
$(k,j,\ell+1)$ & $kc$ & differentiation of $u^k$\\
$(k,j,\ell)$ & $kc$ & differentiation of $u^k$\\
$(k,j+1,\ell)$ & $-jc$ & differentiation of $v^j$\\
$(k,j+1,\ell-1)$ & $\ell c$ & differentiation of $z^\ell$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Transitions with a zero multiplier are omitted. In particular, no negative
logarithm exponent is ever introduced.

\subsubsection{The first cancellation}

The first two polynomials have the compact forms
\begin{align}
 P_1&=u(A+v),\label{xxc:eq:P1}\\
 P_2&=u^2(A+v)^2+
 u\bigl((z+1)A+(3z+2)v-v^2\bigr).\label{xxc:eq:P2}
\end{align}
Expansion gives $a(1)=3$ and $a(2)=12$. At the next order, the coefficient
polynomial of $uv^2$ is
\[
 -(z+1)+\bigl(\partial_z-1\bigr)(3z+2)=-4z.
\]
Its constant term vanishes. The candidate envelope has $31$ cells at order
$3$, but the derivative has $30$ terms. A Boolean reachability calculation
would fail at this very early example.

\subsection{The support envelope and the cubic upper bound}

\begin{definition}
For $n\ge1$, $1\le k\le n$, and $0\le j\le n$, put
\begin{align}
 m_{n,k,j}&=\max(0,k-j),\label{xxc:eq:lo}\\
 U_{n,k,j}&=
 \begin{cases}
 n+k,&j=0,\\
 \min(n+k-j-1,\,2n-2j),&j\ge1.
 \end{cases}\label{xxc:eq:hi}
\end{align}
Let $\mathcal E_n$ consist of the integer triples with these ranges and
$m_{n,k,j}\le\ell\le U_{n,k,j}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Support containment]\label{xxc:prop:envelope}
For $n\ge1$, $\supp P_n\subseteq\mathcal E_n$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Consider a nonzero differentiation path, and let $a,b,c,d,e,r,s$ count its
uses of the seven transitions in the displayed table, in that order. Then
\[
 k=a+b+c,\quad j=c+r+s,\quad
 \ell=2a+b+d-s,\quad
 n=a+b+c+d+e+r+s.
\]
In particular,
\begin{align*}
 n-j&=a+b+d+e,\\
 \ell-(k-j)&=a+d+r\ge0,\\
 2(n-j)-\ell&=b+d+2e+s\ge0,\\
 n+k-j-\ell&=b+c+e+s\ge0.
\end{align*}
If $j>0$, at least one transition of type $c$ or $s$ must occur: the transition
of type $r$ has multiplier $-j$ and cannot create the first positive $j$.
Thus $n+k-j-\ell\ge1$ when $j>0$. The remaining ranges follow immediately
from the transition table and the first differentiation. Every coefficient
is a sum over such paths, so cancellation can remove cells but cannot add a
cell outside these constraints.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Size of the envelope]\label{xxc:prop:envelopesize}
For $n\ge1$, $\#\mathcal E_n=B(n)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $j=0$ there are $n+1$ logarithm exponents for each of $n$ values of $k$,
giving $n(n+1)$ cells. For $j\ge1$, set $d=n-j$, so $0\le d\le n-1$.
The number of cells in this $j$-slice is
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{k=1}^n\bigl(\min(d+k,2d+1)-\max(0,k-n+d)\bigr)
 &=n(2d+1)-d(d+1).
\end{align*}
Indeed, replacing each minimum by $2d+1$ overcounts by
$d(d+1)/2$, and the sum of the subtracted maxima is another $d(d+1)/2$.
Consequently
\begin{align*}
 \#\mathcal E_n
 &=n(n+1)+\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\bigl(n(2d+1)-d(d+1)\bigr)\\
 &=n(n+1)+n^3-\frac{(n-1)n(n+1)}3\\
 &=\frac{2n^3+3n^2+4n}{3}.
\end{align*}
\end{proof}

This proves the upper bound in~\eqref{xxc:eq:mainbounds}. More precisely,
$\Delta(n)$ is exactly the number of zero coefficients \emph{inside}
$\mathcal E_n$. The polynomial $B(n)$ is not being guessed by interpolation:
it is the exact count of a proved finite set of candidate monomials.

\subsection{An independent exact formula for every coefficient}

The recurrence is already an exact algorithm. An independent formula is
valuable for two other reasons: it certifies possible cancellations after a
modular computation, and it proves that every $(k,j)$ row is nonzero.

\subsubsection{Notation for Stirling numbers and auxiliary polynomials}

We use signed Stirling numbers $s(n,k)$ of the first kind and Stirling
numbers $S(n,k)$ of the second kind, with the conventions
\[
 \fall{y}{n}=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(y-i)=\sum_k s(n,k)y^k,
 \qquad y^n=\sum_k S(n,k)\fall{y}{k}.
\]
These conventions agree with DLMF \S26.8~\cite{dlmf}. In particular,
$s(n,k)$ has sign $(-1)^{n-k}$ and is nonzero for $1\le k\le n$;
$S(n,k)>0$ on the same range.

For nonnegative $d,p,q$, define the noncentral Stirling numbers
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:Tdef}
 T_d(p;q)=\sum_{r=0}^d\binom dr q^{d-r}S(r,p).
\end{equation}
They can be computed without rational arithmetic:
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:Trec}
 T_0(p;q)=\mathbf1_{p=0},\qquad
 T_{d+1}(p;q)=(p+q)T_d(p;q)+T_d(p-1;q).
\end{equation}
Indices $p<0$ or $p>d$ have value zero. The useful boundary values are
\[
 T_d(0;q)=q^d,\qquad T_d(d;q)=1,\qquad
 T_d(p;0)=S(d,p),\qquad T_d(p;1)=S(d+1,p+1),
\]
where $0^0=1$. Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:Tmoment}
 e^{-w}\sum_{m\ge0}(m+q)^d\frac{w^m}{m!}
 =\sum_{p=0}^dT_d(p;q)w^p.
\end{equation}
To verify this identity, expand $(m+q)^d$, express powers of $m$ in falling
factorials, and sum $\sum_m\fall{m}{p}w^m/m!=w^pe^w$.

Define
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:Qdef}
 Q_{j,r}(\beta)=\fall{\beta}{j}\,\rise{(\beta+1)}{r}
 =\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}(\beta-i)\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\beta+i).
\end{equation}
These integer polynomials are easy to build by multiplying by linear
factors. Notice that $\deg Q_{j,r}=j+r$.

\subsubsection{A parameterized derivative identity}

For parameters $\alpha,\beta$, Taylor expansion with the increment $xt$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:taylorparam}
 \frac{(x+xt)^{\alpha(x+xt)+\beta}}{x^{\alpha x+\beta}}
 =(1+t)^\beta
 \exp\!\left(\alpha x\,[tz+(1+t)\log(1+t)]\right).
\end{equation}
Comparing coefficients of $t^n$ and then of $(\alpha x)^d$ yields
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:parameteridentity}
 \frac{d^n}{dx^n}x^{\alpha x+\beta}
 =x^{\alpha x+\beta-n}\sum_{d=0}^n(\alpha x)^d
 C_{n,d}(z,\beta),
\end{equation}
where, with $j=n-d$,
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:Cformula}
 C_{n,d}(z,\beta)
 =\sum_{s=0}^d\binom ns\frac{z^s}{(d-s)!}
 \partial_\beta^{\,d-s}Q_{j,d-s}(\beta).
\end{equation}
For completeness, choose $s$ factors $tz$ from the $d$th power of the bracket
in~\eqref{xxc:eq:taylorparam}. The remaining factors contribute
$(1+t)^{d-s}\log^{d-s}(1+t)$. Write the logarithm power as
$\partial_\beta^{d-s}$ acting on $(1+t)^{\beta+d-s}$ and use
\[
 [t^{n-s}](1+t)^{\beta+d-s}
 =\frac{\fall{(\beta+d-s)}{n-s}}{(n-s)!}
 =\frac{Q_{j,d-s}(\beta)}{(n-s)!}.
\]
The factorial prefactors simplify to $\binom ns/(d-s)!$, proving the formula.

\subsubsection{The coefficient oracle}

\begin{theorem}[Exact coefficient formula]\label{xxc:thm:oracle}
Suppose $(k,j,\ell)\in\mathcal E_n$, and put
\[
 d=n-j,\qquad h=d+k-\ell,
\]
\[
 q_- =\max(0,k-\ell,k-d),\qquad q_+=\min(k,h,j).
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:oracle}
\boxed{\displaystyle
 c_n(k,j,\ell)=\sum_{q=q_-}^{q_+}
 \binom n{\ell-k+q}\binom hq\,
 T_d(k-q;q)\,[\beta^h]Q_{j,h-q}(\beta).}
\end{equation}
An empty sum is zero. Outside the envelope, the coefficient is zero.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Expand the outer exponential:
\[
 f(x)=\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{x^{mx}z^m}{m!},\qquad
 x^{mx}z^m=\left.\partial_\beta^m x^{mx+\beta}\right|_{\beta=0}.
\]
Apply~\eqref{xxc:eq:parameteridentity} with $\alpha=m$. For any polynomial
$C(\beta)$,
\[
 \left.\partial_\beta^m\bigl(e^{\beta z}C(\beta)\bigr)\right|_{\beta=0}
 =\sum_{q=0}^m\frac{m!}{(m-q)!}z^{m-q}[\beta^q]C(\beta).
\]
After division by $f=e^{uz}$, the sum over $m$ occurring for fixed $d,q$ is
\begin{align*}
 e^{-uz}\sum_{m\ge q}\frac{m^d u^m z^{m-q}}{(m-q)!}
 &=u^q e^{-uz}\sum_{r\ge0}(r+q)^d\frac{(uz)^r}{r!}\\
 &=u^q\sum_{p=0}^dT_d(p;q)(uz)^p.
\end{align*}
To obtain $u^kz^\ell$, take $p=k-q$ and take
$s=\ell-k+q$ from~\eqref{xxc:eq:Cformula}. Set $r=d-s=h-q$.
Finally,
\[
 [\beta^q]\frac{\partial_\beta^rQ_{j,r}(\beta)}{r!}
 =\binom{q+r}{q}[\beta^{q+r}]Q_{j,r}(\beta),
\]
and $q+r=h$. This proves~\eqref{xxc:eq:oracle}; the stated bounds on $q$ enforce
$p\in[0,d]$, $s\in[0,d]$, and $q\le j$.

The infinite series can be read as locally normally convergent analytic
series for positive $x$ near any fixed point, or as formal exponential
identities followed by extraction of the finitely many relevant coefficients.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Three boundary checks}

Useful independently checkable consequences are
\begin{align}
 [v^0]P_n&=(z+1)^n\sum_{k=1}^nS(n,k)(uz)^k,
                 &&n\ge1,\label{xxc:eq:vzero}\\
 [u^n]P_n&=(z(z+1)+v)^n,\label{xxc:eq:umax}\\
 [v^n]P_n&=\fall{u}{n}.\label{xxc:eq:vmax}
\end{align}
The first follows either from~\eqref{xxc:eq:oracle} or from setting $v=0$ in the
recurrence. The second follows because reaching $u$-degree $n$ requires every
step to differentiate the outer factor $f$. For the third, the coefficient
of the next highest $v$-power is obtained by multiplication by $u-n$,
starting from $1$.

\subsection{A rigorous cubic lower bound}

An upper bound alone does not establish cubic growth. We now show that a
positive cubic number of the candidate cells must survive, without
assuming that cancellations are rare.

\subsubsection{Every row has its advertised highest coefficient}

Let
\[
 R_{n,k,j}(z)=[u^kv^j]P_n(u,v,z).
\]

\begin{lemma}[Nonzero leading coefficient]\label{xxc:lem:leading}
For every $1\le k\le n$ and $0\le j\le n$, the polynomial $R_{n,k,j}$ is
nonzero and has degree exactly $U_{n,k,j}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $j=0$, equation~\eqref{xxc:eq:vzero} gives
\[
 R_{n,k,0}(z)=S(n,k)z^k(z+1)^n.
\]
Now let $j\ge1$ and put $d=n-j$. If $k\le d$, the proposed highest exponent
is $\ell=d+k-1$, so $h=1$ in~\eqref{xxc:eq:oracle}. Only $q=0,1$ contribute.
The coefficient is
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:leadlowk}
 (-1)^{j-1}(j-1)!\left(
 \binom n{d-1}S(d,k)+\binom nd S(d+1,k)\right),
\end{equation}
which is nonzero. Here we used
$[\beta]Q_{j,0}=[\beta]Q_{j,1}=(-1)^{j-1}(j-1)!$.

If $k\ge d+1$, the highest exponent is $\ell=2d$, and $h=k-d$.
The only possible summand is $q=h$, giving
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:leadhighk}
 [z^{2d}]R_{n,k,j}(z)=\binom nd s(j,k-d).
\end{equation}
It is nonzero because $1\le k-d\le j$. The support envelope already rules
out higher exponents, completing the proof.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{A high-multiplicity root}

\begin{lemma}[Divisibility]\label{xxc:lem:divisibility}
The row polynomial is divisible by
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:divisibility}
 z^{\max(k-j,0)}(z+1)^{M_{n,k,j}},
 \qquad M_{n,k,j}=\max(n-2j,k-j,0).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Extracting $u^kv^j$ from~\eqref{xxc:eq:PRec} gives
\begin{align}\label{xxc:eq:rowrec}
 R_{n+1,k,j}={}&z(z+1)R_{n,k-1,j}+R_{n,k-1,j-1}
              +k(z+1)R_{n,k,j}\notag\\
 &+\bigl(\partial_z-(j-1)\bigr)R_{n,k,j-1}.
\end{align}
Rows with out-of-range indices are zero. Begin with $R_{0,0,0}=1$.
For the factor $z+1$, the first and third terms increase the inherited
multiplicity by one. The second term has inherited multiplicity at least
$\max(n-2j+2,k-j,0)$. The derivative in the last term can reduce its inherited
multiplicity by at most one; before differentiation it is at least
$\max(n-2j+2,k-j+1,0)$. Each is sufficient for the target exponent
$\max(n+1-2j,k-j,0)$. This proves the $(z+1)$ assertion by induction.
The same argument with the factor $z$ proves the exponent $\max(k-j,0)$.
Since $z$ and $z+1$ are relatively prime, both divisibilities hold together.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Multiplicity forces many terms]\label{xxc:lem:sparse}
A nonzero polynomial with a nonzero root of multiplicity at least $M$ has
at least $M+1$ nonzero coefficients.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $p(z)=\sum_{i=1}^t b_i z^{e_i}$, with distinct nonnegative integers
$e_i$ and $b_i\ne0$. If a root $\alpha\ne0$ has multiplicity at least $t$,
then $(z\partial_z)^r p(\alpha)=0$ for $0\le r<t$. Thus
\[
 \sum_{i=1}^t e_i^r b_i\alpha^{e_i}=0,\qquad 0\le r<t.
\]
The matrix $(e_i^r)$ is an invertible Vandermonde matrix, contradicting
$b_i\alpha^{e_i}\ne0$. Therefore the multiplicity is at most $t-1$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Summing the mandatory terms}

By Lemmas~\ref{xxc:lem:leading}--\ref{xxc:lem:sparse}, each $(k,j)$ row contributes at
least $1+M_{n,k,j}$ nonzero monomials. Rows are disjoint in the canonical
basis, so
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:lowersum}
 a(n)\ge\sum_{k=1}^n\sum_{j=0}^n
 \bigl(1+\max(n-2j,k-j,0)\bigr).
\end{equation}
For fixed $j$, the sum of the maxima over $k$ is
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:Mslice}
 \sum_{k=1}^n M_{n,k,j}=
 \begin{cases}
 n(n-2j)+\dfrac{j(j+1)}2,&0\le j\le\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\[5pt]
 \dfrac{(n-j)(n-j+1)}2,&j>\lfloor n/2\rfloor.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
In the first case, start with $n-2j$ in each row and add the excess
$1+\cdots+j$. In the second case, only $k>j$ contributes.
Summing these elementary progressions, and adding the $n(n+1)$ ones
in~\eqref{xxc:eq:lowersum}, gives exactly $L(n)$ in~\eqref{xxc:eq:mainbounds}.
This completes the proof of the growth bounds in Theorem~\ref{xxc:thm:main}.

In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:limbounds}
 \frac7{24}\le\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a(n)}{n^3}
 \le\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{a(n)}{n^3}\le\frac23.
\end{equation}
These constants are proved bounds, not estimates of an experimentally fitted
leading coefficient.

\subsection{Exact cancellation families}

\subsubsection{Logarithm-free coefficients and derivatives of a falling factorial}

Let $F_n(y)=\fall{y}{n}$. In~\eqref{xxc:eq:oracle}, setting $\ell=0$ forces
$q=k$, so for $d=n-j$ and $1\le k\le j$,
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:logfree}
\boxed{\displaystyle
 c_n(k,j,0)=\frac{k^d}{d!\,k!}F_n^{(d+k)}(d).}
\end{equation}
Although written using factorial quotients, this expression is an integer;
the implementation instead uses integer polynomial coefficients and binomial
coefficients. Formula~\eqref{xxc:eq:logfree} reduces every logarithm-free
cancellation to an exact derivative evaluation of a single elementary
polynomial.

\begin{proposition}[Central cancellations at every odd order]\label{xxc:prop:odd}
For odd $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:odddeficit}
 \Delta(n)\ge\left\lfloor\frac{n+1}{4}\right\rfloor.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Write $n=2d+1$ and set $j=d+1$. Then
\[
 F_n(d+\beta)=\beta\prod_{r=1}^d(\beta^2-r^2)
\]
is odd. For each $1\le k\le d+1$ with $k\equiv d\pmod2$, its coefficient of
$\beta^{d+k}$ vanishes. Formula~\eqref{xxc:eq:logfree} gives a missing monomial
$(k,d+1,0)$. There are $\lfloor(d+1)/2\rfloor=\lfloor(n+1)/4\rfloor$ such
values of $k$.
\end{proof}

Thus $a(n)=B(n)$ is impossible at every odd $n\ge3$. This does not resolve
whether equality occurs infinitely often at even orders. Nor does it
classify all logarithm-free cancellations: additional integer derivative
zeros occur away from the central symmetry.

\subsubsection{An infinite family with positive logarithm exponent}

It is tempting to guess that all cancellations are logarithm-free.
The following family disproves that guess.

Since $f=e^g$ with $g=uz$, the $u^{n-1}$ part of $f^{(n)}/f$ consists of the
partition type having one block of size two and all other blocks of size one.
Equivalently, induction in~\eqref{xxc:eq:PRec} gives
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:almosttop}
 [u^{n-1}]P_n=\binom n2(A+v)^{n-2}
 \bigl((z+1)A+(3z+2)v-v^2\bigr),\qquad n\ge2.
\end{equation}
For $2\le j\le n-1$, put $m=n-j-1$ and
\[
 a=\binom{n-2}{j},\qquad b=\binom{n-2}{j-1},\qquad
 c=\binom{n-2}{j-2}.
\]
The coefficient of $v^j$, after removing the common factor $\binom n2$, is
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:quadraticrow}
 z^m(z+1)^m\left[-cz^2+(a+3b-c)z+(a+2b)\right].
\end{equation}
Its coefficient of $z^{2m+1}$ is
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:nexttotop}
 a+3b-(m+1)c.
\end{equation}
Using
\[
 \frac ac=\frac{m(m+1)}{j(j-1)},\qquad
 \frac bc=\frac{m+1}{j-1},
\]
this vanishes exactly when $m=j^2-4j$ in the stated range.

\begin{proposition}[Non-logarithm-free cancellation family]\label{xxc:prop:family}
For every integer $t\ge2$, let
\begin{equation}\label{xxc:eq:family}
 n=t^2+t-1,\quad k=n-1,\quad j=t+2,\quad \ell=2t^2-7.
\end{equation}
Then $(k,j,\ell)\in\mathcal E_n$, $\ell>0$, and
$c_n(k,j,\ell)=0$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Substitute $j=t+2$ into $m=j^2-4j$: it gives $m=t^2-4$ and
$n=m+j+1=t^2+t-1$. Then $\ell=2m+1$ is positive, is inside the envelope,
and its coefficient vanishes by~\eqref{xxc:eq:nexttotop}. The next higher
coefficient is $-\binom n2c\ne0$, so this is a genuine internal missing term.
\end{proof}

The first corresponding orders are $5,11,19,29,41,55,\ldots$, with missing
triples
\[
 (4,4,1),\ (10,5,11),\ (18,6,25),\ (28,7,43),\ldots.
\]
These are additional to the logarithm-free central cancellations. Since all
orders in~\eqref{xxc:eq:family} are odd, this proves
$\Delta(n)\ge\lfloor(n+1)/4\rfloor+1$ along that infinite subsequence.

\subsection{Algorithms and complexity}

\subsubsection{The dependency-free exact recurrence}

The most direct implementation stores a dictionary from triples to integer
coefficients. At each differentiation it applies the seven transitions,
combines equal keys, and removes zeros. A short reference version is given
in Section~\ref{xxc:app:code}. It uses no symbolic-algebra system and no
numerical approximation.

The supplied production version packs a triple into one integer key. For a
requested maximum order $N$, take
\[
 b=N+2,\qquad c=2N+3,\qquad \operatorname{key}(k,j,\ell)=(kb+j)c+\ell.
\]
Incrementing $k,j,\ell$ corresponds to adding $bc,c,1$, respectively. The
chosen bases exceed every possible exponent, so no carries can identify
different monomials. The packed representation reduces tuple allocation
and dictionary overhead; it does not change the mathematics.

\begin{proposition}[Arithmetic complexity]\label{xxc:prop:complexity}
The exact recurrence computes the entire prefix through $N$ in $O(N^4)$
integer arithmetic operations and $O(N^3)$ live integer coefficients.
Each coefficient has $O(N\log N)$ bits.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
At order $n$ there are at most $B(n)=O(n^3)$ terms, and each contributes to
at most seven destinations. Summing through $N$ gives $O(N^4)$ operations.
Only the current and next dictionaries are needed, giving $O(N^3)$ storage.

Let $H_n$ be the sum of the absolute values of the coefficients of $P_n$.
The absolute sum of all multipliers leaving a term is
$3+2k+j+\ell\le3+5n$. Therefore
\[
 H_{n+1}\le(5n+3)H_n,\qquad H_0=1,
 \qquad H_n\le\prod_{r=0}^{n-1}(5r+3)\le5^n n!.
\]
Every individual coefficient satisfies this bound, so its bit length is
$O(n\log n)$.
\end{proof}

This is a unit-cost \emph{integer-arithmetic} bound, not a claim that
arbitrarily large integers are machine words. With elementary small-integer
multiplication, a conservative bit bound is $O(N^5\log^2N)$ time and
$O(N^4\log N)$ coefficient-storage bits. Hashing and object overhead affect
practical memory. We do not claim the algorithm is asymptotically optimal.

\subsubsection{A deterministic modular sieve with exact refinement}

Most coefficients need never be constructed as large integers. Fix any
integer modulus $M\ge2$, and run the same recurrence in $\Z/M\Z$.
If a residue is nonzero, the original integer coefficient is certainly
nonzero. Only zero residues need further attention.

The exact single-term algorithm is:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Compute $P_n\bmod M$ by the seven-transition recurrence.
\item Enumerate the triples in $\mathcal E_n$ whose residues are zero.
\item Evaluate each such coefficient with~\eqref{xxc:eq:oracle}, using integer
      arithmetic, and count only the coefficients that are actually zero.
\item Return $B(n)$ minus that exact zero count.
\end{enumerate}
A zero residue is \emph{never} accepted as proof of a zero coefficient.
Consequently this algorithm is correct for every admissible modulus,
including small or composite moduli. No randomness, primality assumption,
or conjecture about support is required. Tests deliberately use moduli
$2$, $7$, and $12$ to force many false modular zeros.

The supplied NumPy implementation uses signed 64-bit arrays and the modulus
$1\,000\,000\,007$ by default. At order $m$, the array has shape
$(m+1,m+1,2m+1)$. The next array has shape
$(m+2,m+2,2m+3)$, and each transition becomes a shifted slice addition.
All entries are reduced modulo $M$ after each step. The implementation checks
\[
 (10n+10)M<2^{63}
\]
before starting; this conservative condition bounds the intermediate
products and sums before modular reduction. Overflow would invalidate the
certificate, so the check is part of correctness rather than merely an
optimization detail.

The dense modular pass takes $O(n^4)$ fixed-width operations and $O(n^3)$
fixed-width words. Let $Z_M(n)$ denote the number of zero residues inside
the envelope. The oracle tables $Q_{j,r}$ and $T_d(p;q)$, and Pascal's
triangle for binomial coefficients, are built lazily. For a single target
$n$, filling all potentially needed tables takes $O(n^3)$ integer arithmetic
operations and storage; each candidate coefficient then requires at most
$O(n)$ integer products and additions. Thus a conservative oracle bound is
\[
 O(n^3+nZ_M(n))\subseteq O(n^4).
\]
In practice the lazy tables are much smaller when the modular pass leaves
few candidates. Unlike the reference recurrence, the oracle may multiply
two large integers, so its unit-arithmetic bound should again not be read
as a bit-operation bound.

\subsubsection{Which implementation to use}

For a whole prefix, the dependency-free recurrence has the simplest predictable
cost and automatically supplies every intermediate count. For an isolated
larger index, the modular method usually avoids nearly all large-integer
coefficient work. Running a new modular pass separately for every prefix
index would discard this advantage and incur repeated work.

Neither method differentiates a transcendental expression in a computer
algebra system. Both return the exact OEIS term count, including signed
cancellations, rather than an expression-size estimate.

\subsection{Computational validation and results}

\subsubsection{Checks actually performed}

The accompanying files record ten passing test groups. The tests compare the
reference recurrence with all $43$ published A281434 values through $n=42$,
and compare the generated prefix with all $80$ published A352697 deficits
through $n=80$~\cite{oeis281434,oeis352697}. Every candidate coefficient
through $n=12$ is checked against the independent formula~\eqref{xxc:eq:oracle}.
The two methods are also compared through $n=12$ for each of four moduli,
including the deliberately small and composite choices described above.

Additional tests verify the envelope, its exact cardinality, the lower-bound
sum, the $(z+1)$ multiplicities, the two leading-coefficient formulas,
the odd central cancellations, and initial instances of the infinite
non-logarithm-free family. Such finite tests check the implementations and
help detect algebraic mistakes; the universal statements are justified by
the proofs, not by the tests.

The integer recurrence was run through $n=100$. The modular implementation
was separately checked against it at $n=20,40,60,80,100$. Selected larger
computations used the modular pass and exact verification of all candidate
zeros. The archive contains the complete generated prefix, the tests, and
the selected missing triples.

On the execution environment used for this report, the packed integer

are different workloads. The exact timings, interpreter information, and
